A child's crying had once seemed to him an embarrassing piece of evidence, something he needed to outgrow before he could be believed about how hard the training had been. Hearing his mother say she had seen it made the old shame move without quite leaving.

"Main kamzor tha?"

"Tum bachche the."

The answer came immediately.

Not a promise that he had secretly been the strongest thing in the courtyard. Not a claim that he had never needed to learn. A child could need teaching without having to deserve the right to cry.

---

For a while neither knew what to do with the quiet.

Rudra had always been able to ask his mother for something to eat. He had not known how to ask whether she remembered the person who had sat at the table with split skin and tried to act as though hunger was the only thing wrong.

Now she had answered a question he had never managed to make complete.

He wanted to comfort her. The wish came automatically when her voice thinned, a small domestic reflex as old as fetching the glass before she could reach for it.

He did not tell her she had done nothing wrong.

"Aapki awaaz theek nahi lag rahi," he said instead.

"Rona aa raha hai. Saans theek hai."

The precision nearly undid him.

She was letting him care about her without making that care disprove what he had said.

"Paani paas hai?"

"Haan. Abhi liya."

They waited. A nurse passed at Rudra's end and checked that the phone was not making him turn against the support. He let her move it nearer. The small interruption gave both of them time without requiring either to pretend the conversation had become easy.

---

"Tumhare papa tumse pyaar karte hain," Rukmini said later.

Rudra's face tightened.

"Mujhe pata hai."

She heard what he had been expecting after the sentence and did not give it to him.

"Uske baad lekin nahi bol rahi," she said. "Pyaar tha. Jo chot pahunchi, woh bhi thi. Tumse dono mein se ek chunne ko nahi keh rahi."

He looked at the strip of daylight on his blanket.

All his life, the demand had felt like a choice even when nobody stated it: either understand the father or speak for the child. If he did one honestly enough, the other would have to become a mistake.

His mother had not made the old mornings harmless.

She had stopped asking love to perform that work.

"Mujhe abhi maaf karna nahi aata," he said.

"Abhi maang bhi nahi rahi."

She would have to live with what he did not yet know how to give. So would he. Neither could finish the years in the small allowance of one call.

---

Before they ended, she asked about the shirt from the bag.

"Saamne se khulne waali pehni?"

"Haan. Madad se."

"Akele haath upar karke mat badalna."

"Pata hai, Ma."

The irritation was ordinary enough to make her smile, though he could not see it.

"Pata hona aur madad lena alag kaam hain."

He almost told her she could say that to Papa too.

Then he did tell her.

She gave a short laugh and stopped before it pulled at the shoulder. "Unse bhi wahi bolti hoon."

They said goodbye because both needed rest, not because either had absolved the other. After the line ended, Rukmini called Dadi to help her settle more comfortably.

In Prayagraj, Rudra asked for the stand to be lowered.

For the first time in years, remembering himself crying in the courtyard did not make him want to leave the child there alone.

---

In Mirzapur, Aadhya had managed half a paragraph of the college work before the rest period.

She had dictated it. The helper read it back, including a word which made no sense, and Aadhya corrected that word twice before realizing she had been the one saying it wrong. Ira laughed softly from the other bed.

"Bahut achha paragraph," she said. "Ab bas matlab aa jaaye."

Aadhya looked at her. "Tum likh lo."

The old answer was ready on Ira's tongue: give it here. She let the answer pass. The covered left eye, the limited work the right could comfortably do, the hand which still complained after too much use were not going to become somebody else's restrictions because the joke had been good.

"Kal bhi college rahega," she said.

"Dhamki mat do."

Sitara came in after speaking to the receiving contact. Rudra had asked for a short call later, after his rest. Did they want it?

They did. Sitara checked the interval with their team before confirming it. The paragraph stayed unfinished without either girl making an apology to the page.

---

When the call came, Rudra began by asking whether they had the time for something difficult.

"Haan," Aadhya said. "Bol."

He told them about his father's parents.

The words sounded different when he was responsible for giving them to somebody else. He could not use the word sent without explaining that the parents sending the baby were dying. He could not make massacre mean the loss of fighters and leave out the children his father had expressly included.

He took the time to say those things plainly.

Then he told them who Ram had held responsible: those governing the Lokas, the authority above human Kuls, choosing to destroy the lineage because its growing strength threatened them.

Nobody on the line hurried him through the account so they could tell him how to feel about it.

Aadhya rested her injured hand more comfortably with help. The phone stayed on its support. Ira listened with her eyes closed, not searching for another sight inside what a friend had actually said.

---

"Unhone tujhe kuchh karne ko kaha?" Aadhya asked.

"Nahi. Maine poochha. Bola, badla lene ka hukum nahi de rahe."

She let out a breath.

Rudra heard it and, for a moment, was angry. He had just told her about people being killed. Relief seemed like the wrong thing to have brought out of it.

"Tu khush hai?" he asked too sharply.

"Nahi."

She waited. He could hear a footstep at her end, then the quiet again.

"Mujhe darr laga ki ab tu apna ilaaj bhi unka karza samajhke karega. Jaldi theek hona hai kyunki kisi se badla lena hai."

Rudra looked towards Vedant's empty chair. The man was taking the rest arranged at the last handover. He had not appointed somebody to make sure the boy arrived at the right interpretation while he was away.

"Main bhi wahi sochne laga tha," Rudra admitted. "Seedha nahi. Par jaise abhi sabko bataana padega main kis taraf hoon."

"Humein abhi mat saabit kar," Aadhya said.

The softness of it hurt more than a demand might have.

---

Ira asked whether he wanted them to do anything with the account.

"Bas abhi apne paas rakho," he said. "Veer ko bhejne ki zaroorat nahi. Usse main baat kar loonga."

"Theek."

No one began making a list of people who would be entitled to hear it next. The fact that the story concerned a lineage did not make it the property of every person with a question about his blood.

Ira had not opened the private reminder from the earlier call. Nothing needed to be written while he was still deciding what he wanted to keep.

"Aapko kya laga?" Rudra asked suddenly.

Both girls were quiet.

"Aapko?" Aadhya repeated.

He shut his eyes. The politeness had escaped from another part of his life, the voice in which he asked someone older whether his answer was acceptable.

"Tum dono ko," he said, irritated with himself.

Aadhya did not laugh at the mistake.

"Abhi sun rahe hain," she said. "Ek minute de."

So he gave her one, though a minute on a hospital phone could feel longer than whole afternoons had once felt at the ghats.

---

"Yeh kisi bachche ki galti nahi thi," Ira said at last. "Aur tu us waqt tha hi nahi."

He knew. He had said something like it to himself through the night, and still his mind kept trying to volunteer him as the part of the story which could be changed.

Hearing it in her voice did not cure the thought.

It made him less alone while he refused it.

He asked about the paragraph because there was a little time left. Aadhya told him how far she had got, and about the word she had lost an argument with.

"Mujhe mat bhejna," Rudra said. "Main hospital mein hoon. Itni sazaa kaafi hai."

Her laugh was small and real. They ended soon afterwards, before the joke could become an excuse to overrun what either team had allowed.

The paragraph remained in Mirzapur.

The boy remained in Prayagraj, tired, with something terrible newly known and the sound of his friends still able to be themselves around it.

---

Ram woke from the afternoon rest when somebody said his Earth name.

"Ram-ji?"

He answered before he had quite opened his eyes. The nurse waited until he was properly awake before asking about the pain, and he gave her the answer from the body he was in, not the answer he wished could be true.

The site needed care. He still tired. The plan for support had not become unnecessary because he could speak to his son for longer than before. She checked what needed checking and helped him with the position which had begun to pull.

When Rukmini came back, she asked how he had slept.

"Thoda," he said.

"Maine bhi."

Dadi brought the small bag in with her and put it where neither patient would have to reach across an injured side to find anything. She asked the nurse about the next care interval, listened to the answer and went to sit down herself.

The room was quieter than the courtyard Ram kept hearing inside it.

---

"Baat hui?" he asked Rukmini.

"Haan."

He looked at her face.

She had cried. He could see it in the skin around her eyes, and the sight began collecting all the old impulses in him: ask what had happened, determine where the pain was coming from, decide what could be done to end it.

"Usne kya—"

He stopped.

Rukmini waited. She did not answer the question he had not yet chosen whether to ask.

"Tum dono ki baat thi," he said at last.

"Haan."

It would have been easier if she had offered to tell him everything anyway. He could have called the telling closeness and never had to name the relief he was trying to obtain from it.

She asked for help with her support before sitting. He waited for the movement to finish.

"Usse kya chahiye, woh usse poochhna," she said afterwards. "Mujhse uski taraf se jawaab mat lena."

Ram nodded.

It was an uncomfortable kindness, returning him to the person whose answer he was frightened of hearing.

---

He had survived long enough to become a father.

For years, that survival had looked to him like something borrowed in a place where it could be withdrawn without warning. A meal eaten, a gate shut, a boy asleep in another room: he had loved those things and listened through them for the sound which might end them.

He had not imagined every threat. He had fought. He had won against danger which would not have ceased to exist merely because he became gentler at home.

That fact had been useful to him in too many arguments with himself.

It could not tell him which morning should have stopped earlier. It could not make his son's frightened voice, described to him without shouting, into the price the world had demanded rather than the price he had chosen to exact.

Ram looked at the cup beside his bed.

He wanted water. The reach was wrong from this position. He called the nurse instead of testing whether he could manage it quickly enough that nobody would notice.

She helped, waited and took the cup back.

There had been no enemy in the room to overcome by refusing her.

---

Dhruv arrived during the period he had agreed to cover.

He asked after both of them, heard the answer and took the chair Dadi had left free. He did not bring a declaration about what the exposed colours must mean for the future. The people in the room already had enough future to be afraid of.

Ram asked whether Rudra had wanted another call.

"Poochein," Dhruv said, and the receiving contact was asked to check.

Nothing in the arrangement made a son answer because a father had become ready. In Prayagraj, Rudra was resting after speaking to the girls. The reply came back that he wanted the evening call if both teams allowed it.

Ram listened to that small condition.

He had once made readiness something another person was required to achieve when he said so. Now he had to be one of the people waiting for a time which did not belong to him alone.

---

Rukmini stayed until she needed the next rest.

She told him before leaving, instead of remaining in the chair until pain made the decision for her. Dhruv knew the receiving arrangement. Dadi would be nearby. The team was not leaving him unattended.

"Aaj har baat khatam mat karna," she said.

For a moment he thought she meant the history.

Then she touched the edge of the water tray with her good fingers, making sure it sat where the nurse had put it.

She meant the strength with which he would try to tell it.

"Jitna bol sakoon," he said.

"Jitna bolne dein."

He gave her a tired look, and this time the small smile belonged to both of them.

After she had gone, Ram lay still. The history would not become less true if he accepted the time his body needed before he spoke again.

---

By evening, Rudra no longer wanted to look at the ceiling.

He asked for the phone to be set where it had been in the morning and waited while the nurse made sure he was comfortable. Vedant had returned after rest, his arm supported, but did not sit in the place where Rudra's eyes would have to pass over him with every question.

At the far end, Ram had finished care. Rukmini was resting nearby. The nurse placed the phone within hearing and stayed available.

"Unke paas itni taaqat thi," Rudra said. "Phir bhi darr tha?"

"Haan."

"Toh main kitna bhi seekh loon, kisi ko phir bhi khatra lag sakta hoon."

Ram heard the boy find a terrible way to become responsible for somebody else's choice.

"Lag sakta hai," he said. "Usse tum galat nahi ho jaaoge."

The answer was less comforting than a promise that enough discipline would make him safe. It was also the first one Rudra could imagine remaining true after he left this bed.

---

"Phir main kya badal sakta hoon?"

Ram looked down at the hand which had once corrected the position of a child's foot with no patience for how much the correction hurt.

"Apna kiya," he said. "Kisi ko tumse khatra lagta hai, isliye tumhe us jaisa hona zaroori nahi. Aur darr lagta hai, isliye mere har kiye ko tum sahi mat maan lena."

Rudra stayed quiet.

He had not expected the admission to travel into an answer about power. His father had usually described a world hard enough to make hardness sound like the only sensible preparation for it.

There were still hard things in the world. The dead in Pataal had not been saved by someone refusing to become cruel in imitation of their killers.

Yet the man telling the story had a son who could ask a question. He had been given that much of a future. What he did with it could not be determined entirely by the people who had tried to end his own.

"Mujhe achha nahi lag raha aapse yeh poochhna," Rudra said.

"Poochho."

The word arrived without again behind it.

---

"Aapne kabhi socha, agar main kabhi ladna hi nahi chaahoon?"

The question had come larger than he intended. He was not promising never to fight. He could remember Veer falling and the people at the ghat, could not imagine standing safely aside while those things happened again.

He was asking whether the possibility belonged to him at all.

Ram took time.

"Maine tumhare bachne ke baare mein socha," he said. "Bahut. Tum kaise jeena chaahoge, uske baare mein utna nahi poochha."

Rudra looked towards the curtain opening. Nobody was listening there on behalf of a lineage, waiting to hear what the boy would offer.

"Ab poochh sakte hain."

"Haan."

The exchange did not settle what he would choose. It changed who might be allowed into the choosing.

There had been afternoons when going to the ghats instead of straight home had felt like a rebellion serious enough to require an explanation. He had not known how to ask for a life larger than that detour. Now the question had reached his father without either of them turning it into a test of how much he loved the family.

---

He asked whether his father thought somebody was coming to this hospital.

It was the practical fear beneath the ceiling, the one he had been putting off because the old history was easier to give a shape than an unknown person walking through the duty station.

"Abhi kaun kya jaanta hai, main uska daava nahi karoonga," Ram said. "Jitna pakka pata hoga, tumse chhupaane ko jawaab nahi banaaoonga."

He gave no invented assurance that every danger had ended, no certainty about a person or an arrival for which he had no evidence. Rudra would still tell the team about anything new he felt or saw. The people caring for him would not be made to work around a silence his family called protection.

The nurse checked his fatigue. He told her he could manage a little longer, and she allowed only a little.

"Aap bade ho gaye the," Rudra said. "Yahaan reh rahe the. Phir bhi aisa laga ki kabhi nahi ruk sakte?"

Ram's answer was quiet.

"Khatra sirf bachpan mein nahi tha. Baad mein bhi saamna hua. Main jeeta bhi."

---

For years, Rudra had imagined his father's victories as the places where fear must have ended. A man who could do what Ram did should surely have found a day on which enough became enough.

"Phir?"

"Tumhaare janam se chhe saal pehle bhi ek ladaai jeeti thi," Ram said. "Uske baad bhi ghar aake darr khatam nahi hua."

Rudra lay still.

That fight belonged to a time before his own life. Six years before it, Ram confirmed when he asked. Rudra could not search his childhood for the day his father had returned; there had been no child waiting for him then.

"Pehle se darr tha. Us saal aur badha."

Rudra could hear the distinction without yet knowing the event inside it.

His father had gone out into a world Rudra had not yet entered, fought somebody and come home. Years later, that fear had still occupied the courtyard where Rudra trained. The boy had understood his father's silence as another judgement on whether the morning's work had been good enough.

He looked at the phone.

"Mere janam se chhe saal pehle kya hua tha, Papa?"

---

# CHAPTER EIGHTY-FIVE — THE VICTORY HE BROUGHT HOME

---

> *"He came home from the fight alive.*
> *For years, I thought that meant he had left the danger outside."*
>
> *— Rudra's later notes on the fight before his birth*

---

"Adhira se lada tha," Ram said. "Tumhaare janam se chhe saal pehle. Maine haraaya."

His son listened to the name without finding a place for it in his own memory.

"Aap jeet gaye the?"

"Haan. Us ladaai mein."

Ram gave him the outcome plainly. He had not run from that fight or mistaken escape for victory. He also did not promise that winning one encounter had made every future danger knowable.

The nurse reminded him that the call had reached its limit. In Prayagraj, Rudra's team was saying the same thing. He had the first answer; the rest would have to wait until they could speak again after the night.

"Kal poochhoonga," Rudra said.

"Poochhna."

They ended. The two phones were put down in separate cities.

While the nurse helped him settle, Ram found the old ground rising into memory, clear in some places and missing at the edges. Not an infant's borrowed history now. He had been there. The body remembering the fight was the body which had fought it.

---

Six years before Rudra's birth, the stone under his left heel had moved.

Only a little. Enough.

Adhira's next blow reached the place where Ram's head had been, struck the edge of the opening behind him and sent grit against his cheek. He had turned instead of trying to set the foot again. His shoulder hit the wall. The force took the air from him in a short, humiliating sound.

No time to draw it back properly.

He ducked under the reaching arm, drove forward and felt his first strike stopped before it could reach the body. The impact ran through his knuckles to the elbow. He let the arm bend rather than hold it straight and lose the joint.

Adhira pushed him off.

Ram slid across the broken stone, found ground and moved before the next strike arrived. Red-black force tightened along his forearm. He met the blow at an angle; a section of the low brick edge behind him cracked where the impact went through.

Dust rose between them.

He did not wait for it to clear.

---

The next few moments had never returned to him in a neat sequence.

A heel beside a narrow drainage cut. His own palm scraping something sharp. The horrible certainty that he had not moved quite far enough, followed by pain along the side which proved it. He remembered those pieces better than he remembered the words either of them might have said.

He would not improve the memory for a listener.

Adhira pressed him towards the opening again. The broken paving gave him little room on one side. The low wall gave him less on the other. He could move back into the place where his foot had already failed or try to go through the attack before it was complete.

He went forward.

His first blow was caught. The second made room for his knee. He struck, felt the opposing weight change and drove with the planted leg while he still had a place to put it.

They hit the low wall together.

Brick scraped his back. The breath he had been trying to save was gone again. A hand closed against his upper arm, found leverage and began to turn him into the broken edge.

Ram did not hold himself rigid against it.

He went with the turn, ugly and too close, and drove his free forearm between them.

---

For a moment he had a strip of space no wider than his own wrist.

He used it to breathe.

That was all. No speech. No view of the whole struggle from somewhere above himself. Air, pain and the next hand coming for his throat.

He caught the arm before it closed, shifted his weight and made Adhira bear the effort of holding him against the wall. The brick under his heel ground loose. He had one chance to change the position before the ground changed it for him.

He let his knee give.

The grip followed him down. Not far enough.

Ram drove up beneath it, shoulder and hip together, red-black force burning close to skin. Adhira struck the remaining wall hard enough to loosen a second run of brick. Ram tore himself clear before the fragments came down.

His foot landed badly.

Pain flashed up from the ankle. He moved anyway, not well, only soon enough that the next impact passed across his raised arm instead of his jaw.

He was still standing.

He had not won yet.

---

Adhira came through the dust before Ram could put the injured foot down properly.

He met the first strike, failed to stop the second and took it across the ribs. Pain cut his next breath short. The body wanted to fold around the hurt; he let it turn with the impact and kept the turn moving.

A fist passed close enough to brush his ear.

He struck underneath it.

Adhira gave ground, one hard step. Ram followed, then stopped himself. The broken paving narrowed towards the opening. His opponent had more room to turn there than he did, and his foot could no longer correct a bad decision as quickly as it had a few moments earlier.

He stayed where the floor was wider.

Adhira's head lifted.

Ram could see blood at the edge of the mouth now, could see the effort it took to pull air through it. The sight did not make the next blow less dangerous. It made the distance between an impossible enemy and a body terribly small.

Both of them were breathing.

Either could still stop the other doing it.

---

The next attack was slower only at the beginning.

Ram saw the change in the shoulder and moved to meet it. The force came from the other side. He had answered the invitation instead of the strike.

It hit him high in the chest.

For a moment the ground vanished beneath his feet. The remaining wall struck his back, then the floor caught him on one hip. Loose grit went into his mouth. He tasted dirt and blood together.

He rolled before he knew whether the ankle would take his weight.

Stone cracked where his head had been.

He got a knee underneath himself. Adhira reached him before he could rise, seized the cloth at his shoulder and began hauling him up into the next blow.

Ram did not try to get upright.

He drove his weight down through the planted knee, turned across the grip and struck at the leg which was bearing Adhira's advance. The grip dragged him sideways. His shirt tore. He heard the cloth go with an absurd clarity, a cheap domestic sound in the middle of the struggle.

He would remember that sound for years.

---

Adhira's footing broke.

Not the leg. The position.

Ram went after it before the balance could be recovered. The injured ankle protested when he drove forward, but he had already put his shoulder beneath the opposing arm. He could no longer turn away without offering his neck.

He kept moving.

One impact against the low edge. A second against the ground. Red-black force shuddered through his arms, close and violent, making the loose fragments jump. He held the position until the hand pushing against him lost its line.

Then he changed the hold.

No graceful finish remained in the memory. His own forearm was slippery. Adhira was still trying to move. Ram used the uninjured leg, pinned the shoulder and struck hard enough to stop the effort to rise.

He drew back, ready for another attack.

It did not come.

He waited, breath rough, his hand shaking with the force he had used. Adhira's chest moved. The eyes opened, then narrowed against the dust. The hand beside the broken brick no longer reached for him.

The fight was over.

Ram had won.

---

For a long moment he could not stand straight.

He had expected the next breath to feel different once the struggle ended. It hurt in the same place. The ankle had not been waiting for victory before deciding whether it was injured. Blood continued moving down his palm until he pressed torn cloth against the cut.

He looked at Adhira again.

The defeated figure was neither a vanished nightmare nor proof that the world had become harmless. It was the person he had fought, down on the broken ground, unable to continue that fight.

He could leave the next blow unstruck.

That was part of having won too.

Ram got his weight over the sound leg and used what remained of the wall to rise. The bricks shifted under his hand. He let them settle, then tried again, more carefully.

This time he stood.

There was no crowd to tell him what it meant. He had the torn cloth, the hurting side and the knowledge that the attack in front of him had ended.

He began the work of getting home.

---

The memory returned to 2026 by way of the nurse's voice.

Ram opened his eyes. He had been quiet while she finished helping him, not gone from the ward. The support was where it needed to be. The chest pain belonged to the injury being treated now; a remembered blow had not put another wound beneath the dressing.

"Yahaan tak theek?" she asked.

He told her what the position felt like.

She adjusted it once, checked the answer and moved the phone out of the way. The call was over. Nothing he remembered required him to summon the strength for another one tonight.

He let his eyes close again without trying to finish the story aloud to an absent listener.

In another city, his son knew a name and an outcome.

He had not heard every blow his father had just remembered.

---

Rudra tried to imagine his father losing.

Not the way he had seen Ram fall at Manikarnika, where the knowledge of an injured parent had been too immediate to leave room for thinking about anything else. An earlier defeat, before Rudra had been born. A loss which would have left no father for him to know, no ordinary future in which he could complain to Veer about a difficult morning.

There had been an ordinary day around that danger, but it held no younger Rudra waiting for news. He could imagine losing his father now; he could not remember a time before his own life.

His good hand had curled on the blanket again.

He let it open.

Vedant asked whether he needed anything before the next handover.

"Aapse kuchh poochhna hai."

The man waited until the nurse had finished the check at the next bed. Then he moved his chair nearer, keeping the space for the team clear.

Rudra looked at the supported forearm.

"Rehne dein. Thak gaye honge."

"Thaka hoon," Vedant said. "Thodi baat sun sakta hoon."

Neither part of the answer cancelled the other.

---

"Papa ne aapko bhi sikhaaya."

"Haan."

He knew that much already. Vedant had said it when he first came, long before Rudra understood what else his father's life contained.

"Aapko kabhi laga, jitna seekhenge utna darr kam hoga?"

Vedant rested his arm before answering.

"Kabhi. Har cheez ka jawaab nahi mila."

He did not tell a story from his own past to compete with Ram's. Nor did he give the boy an account of the fight on somebody else's behalf.

"Mujhe lagta tha main galat seekh raha hoon," Rudra said. "Itne saal ke baad bhi har nayi cheez pe darr lagta tha."

"Darr aana galat seekhne ka saboot nahi."

The answer annoyed him. Too simple for the amount of work he had done trying to make it untrue.

"Toh kya seekha?"

Vedant did not point to the wrist or call the damaged body evidence of courage.

"Jo kiya hai, woh tum jaante ho. Ghat pe. Usse pehle bhi. Par usse tumhe har waqt taiyaar mehsoos hona zaroori nahi."

Rudra looked away.

He could remember being useful. He could not remember usefulness ever making him feel finished.

---

He wanted, suddenly, to hear something which had nothing to do with fighting.

"Woh college waala page?"

Vedant glanced at the bag, then asked whether he wanted it brought out. Rudra said yes, only to listen to a little of it. He did not reach for the bag with the braced hand.

The page came from the place where the receiving team had put it with his other things. Vedant held it without making Rudra look up into the light and read the heading first. Then a few ordinary lines.

Rudra had expected them to sound absurd.

They sounded like work he had missed.

That was worse in a smaller, more manageable way. He had missed work. Other people had continued attending, forgetting pens, quarrelling about how much a question could reasonably ask. The world had not spent every interval outside his bed waiting to decide what he was.

"Bas," he said after a little.

Vedant stopped at once.

"Baad mein."

The page went back into the bag, not into a promise that the boy would make up every lost day before anyone noticed he had been gone.

---

"Veer ko ek chhota message bhejna hai," Rudra said.

The next check came first. He answered it, rested as asked and had the phone placed near enough to record without holding it. Vedant would not send until he had heard his own words played back.

"Papa ne mere janam se chhe saal pehle ki ek ladaai bataayi. Jeet gaye the, par darr phir bhi saath aaya."

Two sentences.

He listened. He had not included the name, the older massacre or an instruction to find something out. He asked for the message to go only to Veer through the existing contact, when his friend's team said it would not interrupt care.

Vedant checked the destination with him and sent it.

There was no demand for a reply tonight.

---

The man left at the handover instead of extending the conversation until his own pain made rest unavoidable.

Rudra watched the curtain settle behind him. Beyond it the night team was already working, knowing where the bag belonged and which movement still needed help. No one asked the boy to reward the handover by sleeping immediately.

He lay awake for a while.

His father had come home six years before Rudra was born. This much was no longer an imagined ending to an imagined fight. The boy at the later breakfast table had not witnessed that return. He had grown up with a fear whose earlier history he was only now being told.

Rudra could feel the two histories approaching each other.

He did not know yet what his father would say when he asked where one had been allowed to enter the other.

---

Veer heard the message after the post's night check.

The volunteer put the supported phone where he could listen without reaching across the shoulder. Rudra's voice came out quieter than the noises in the passage, but clear enough.

Two sentences. A fight six years before Rudra's birth. A man who won and still came home afraid.

"Phir sunna hai?" the volunteer asked.

Veer shook his head, then answered aloud so the man did not have to interpret it.

"Nahi. Samajh aaya."

He looked at the phone after the voice stopped.

It would have been easy to send back that Ram was right to be frightened. The people at Manikarnika had given Veer enough reason to understand an adult lying awake. It would have been just as easy to say Rudra had never deserved what the fear had cost him.

One sentence would not have made the other untrue.

He did not need to send a judgement through a tired friend's phone tonight.

---

"Kal baat kar lenge," he recorded instead. "Abhi mere liye jaagna mat."

He listened, approved the destination and asked for it to be passed on through the contact without waking Rudra. The volunteer confirmed that it could wait until his friend was awake and the team agreed.

The phone went away from the edge.

Veer asked for help with the blanket before it caught the knee. While it was being moved he admitted that the shoulder had begun aching more in the last position. The volunteer called the nurse rather than deciding himself that more padding must be the answer.

The nurse checked, adjusted what needed adjusting and reminded him what he could do before the morning review. Nothing in the message from Prayagraj's other bedside had changed his own restrictions.

Veer thanked her.

Then, because she was still there and he had already begun being honest, he said he did not think he would sleep immediately.

"Theek," she said. "Dard badhe toh bataana."

No one asked him to turn concern into sleep on command.

---

He remembered Rudra at the college table, pretending a hurting hand was an inconvenience caused by everybody else's conversation.

Veer had known about the training. He had told Aadhya about it when she asked. Knowing had not given him a way to walk backwards into a courtyard and stand beside every younger version of his friend.

He could answer the person who was here now.

He could stop making that answer an instruction to be brave for him too.

The volunteer came back to say the reply had reached the receiving contact and would be held as requested. Then he left for his own rest after the handover, telling Veer who would be nearby.

Veer let him go.

He watched the light at the passage opening narrow when somebody drew the curtain, listened to the ordinary work beyond it and eventually fell asleep without one more message.

---

In Varanasi, Rukmini had already settled in the nearby room when Dadi came to check the arrangements for the night.

The shoulder needed the support. A small thing had been put on the wrong side of the bed, and Rukmini asked for it to be moved instead of twisting to retrieve it.

Dadi moved it, put the water where it could be reached comfortably and sat long enough to hear that the pain was not new or suddenly worse. Rukmini had followed the prescribed plan; if anything changed, they knew whom to call.

"Ab kuchh aur?" Dadi asked.

Rukmini looked towards the phone.

She wanted to check whether her son had slept. She wanted somebody to check whether her husband had slept. The want had the same familiar shape as care, but following it from one room to another could keep every person awake.

"Nahi," she said. "Subah."

Dadi nodded without congratulating her for resisting something so small and so difficult.

---

Dhruv left Ram only after the agreed handover.

The nurse knew the nearby family arrangement. The next shift had the actual clinical details, not a relative's reassurance that he always managed. His phone was placed out of the way of the care equipment, close enough for staff to help with it when needed.

Ram did not ask Dhruv to carry the account of that earlier fight to his son.

What he had said belonged to their conversation. What he remembered had not become a message merely because remembering it hurt.

He had once come home from the old fight and tried to wash the signs of it from his hands before the house could see how near the danger had been. He could remember the water running pink and then clear, could remember his own absurd irritation with a small stain which would not leave the skin beside a nail.

Clear water had not made him calm.

He did not have to search that memory for an excuse tonight.

He could let it be something he would need to answer for honestly when the boy asked again.

---

The night between days forty and forty-one passed in staffed intervals in all three cities.

Rudra woke for a check, answered it and went back to sleep before the waiting message was mentioned. The receiving contact kept it where it would be available in the morning. In Mirzapur the girls' unfinished work stayed aside; nobody used a quiet night to demand one more page from tired eyes and hands.

There was no shared dream carrying the old fight between beds. No single room into which the families and friends had gathered without travelling.

Only separate people sleeping when they could, with enough ordinary arrangements around them to let somebody else remain responsible for a while.

---

In the morning, Rudra heard Veer's reply after the first check.

"Kal baat kar lenge. Abhi mere liye jaagna mat."

He looked towards the grey light beside the curtain and smiled a little. Kal had become today without either friend having to keep watch over the change.

He asked the nurse to thank the receiving contact. Then came the review, breakfast within what was allowed and another rest. The clinicians were still deciding the next small steps by what his body could manage, not by how long he had been patient with the bed. His wrist and ankle remained protected.

When the short family call was approved at both ends, he asked for it.

Ram was awake after his own care. The nurse helped with the phone. His mother knew the call was happening but was not required to leave her rest to take a place beside it.

"Us ladaai ki baat poochhni hai," Rudra said.

"Haan."

---

Ram told him a little more.

He had taken blows. The ground had broken badly underfoot. There had been no clean moment in which winning removed the hurt already done. He had stopped Adhira and come home alive, carrying the fear with the victory.

He did not recite every movement as though his son needed a combat lesson before he could understand what happened to a parent. Nor did the boy receive the whole private memory merely because the father gave him these parts of it.

"Aapko chot lagi thi?"

"Haan."

Rudra looked down at his own brace.

He had seen his father injured in his own lifetime. This answer belonged to the years before it. The man who had seemed always capable of delivering one more instruction had survived pain long before there was a son to imagine him untouched by it.

"Mere bade hone par bhi kyun nahi bataaya?"

Ram took time over the answer.

"Main ghar mein woh darr nahi laana chahta tha."

Rudra almost laughed.

It would not have been a kind sound. He swallowed it and let the next words come without it.

"Par aaya toh tha."

---

In Varanasi, Ram kept his eyes on the phone.

"Haan."

"Mujhe laga main kuchh galat kar raha hoon. Aap chup hote the, toh aur theek karna padta tha."

The boy was speaking about his own childhood, not claiming a memory from before his birth. He could not certify which later silence belonged to Adhira or to any other fear in his father's life.

He did not need to attach a date to every frightened breakfast before the pattern became real.

"Aapka chup rehna mere liye khaali nahi tha," he said.

Ram's good hand moved on the blanket. He let it rest again without turning the movement into a signal for the boy to stop.

"Mujhe laga, naam nahi doonga toh tumhare liye nahi hoga," he said. "Jo ghar mein kar raha tha, usmein woh darr tha. Tumhe naam nahi pata tha."

The distinction had once helped him feel he was keeping a boundary intact. Now it sounded like a child being left to explain the consequences of an event the adults would not acknowledge.

He did not argue with how it sounded.

---

"Agar mujhe bataate toh sab theek ho jaata, main yeh nahi bol raha," Rudra said.

"Pata hai."

"Mujhe poori ladaai nahi dekhni thi. Par har buri subah meri kami nahi thi, itna toh—"

He stopped because the last words had become difficult.

His father waited.

The nurse in Prayagraj checked his breathing and asked whether he needed to pause. He said yes. The line remained open while he took the rest of the answer from his own body, not from a count he could use to force the conversation onward.

In Varanasi, Ram let the silence remain a pause. He did not fill it with an apology so large the son would have to stop being hurt to receive it.

When Rudra could speak again, he finished.

"Itna toh bol sakte the."

"Haan," Ram said. "Bol sakta tha."

---

The name Adhira now belonged to one part of the father's history.

The encounter had preceded Rudra's birth by six years; his father had carried its fear into the years when training began. That order could help explain the urgency. It could not supply a child's consent, or make every blow in the courtyard necessary.

Rudra had known about his father's fear for only a little while. His body had lived with its methods for nearly all the years he could remember.

He wanted to understand that beginning without offering to repay it.

For a moment the old question tried to become a boast: had Ram seen how strong he would be? Had all the pain been preparation for something only this son could do?

He let the boast go before it could make the hurt sound like a prize.

There was a smaller child in the question than the young man who could now listen to that history. A child who could not have promised to avenge anybody, who would have had to be taught even the words for what a promise meant.

Rudra looked at his supported hand.

"Main ek saal ka tha, Papa," he said. "Aap mujhe kiski ladaai ke liye taiyaar kar rahe the?"

---

# CHAPTER EIGHTY-SIX — A LIFE NOT OWED

---

> *"They had sent a child away so that he could live.*
> *We were not required to turn every life after his into a repayment."*
>
> *— Ira's later notes on inherited debts*

---

"Kisi ki ladaai tumhare naam karne ka haq nahi tha mujhe," Ram said. "Main tumhe zinda rakhna chahta tha."

The answer reached the smaller child in the question before it reached the young man asking it.

Rudra looked at the phone.

"Ek saal ki umar se?"

"Haan. Main chahta tha tumhaara shareer taiyaar ho, jab samajh paao usse pehle bhi. Mujhe lagta tha, der ki toh bachne ka waqt nahi milega."

There it was: the reason, given without asking him to call the reason enough.

"Maine tumhe apne maa-baap ka badla lene ko paida nahi kiya," Ram said. "Na tumne kisi ko aisa waada kiya tha."

He did not name an enemy for a baby to inherit. He did not make the boy's survival a gift which would be taken back if he chose the wrong future.

Rudra breathed out slowly.

For years, the earliest training had seemed like evidence that the plan for his life had been completed before he could enter the room where it was kept. His father was saying the intention had been survival, not a vow. The methods had still occupied years of the life they were meant to preserve.

Both things remained in the answer.

---

"Mujhe zinda rakhna tha," he said. "Par zinda rehne ke alawa bhi kuchh tha na?"

Ram shut his eyes for a moment.

A small boy waking without pain. A morning which did not have to be won before breakfast. Ordinary things, made almost impossible to think about when a parent had taught himself that attending to them could be the lapse in which danger arrived.

"Tha," he said. "Maine uske liye jagah kam rakhi."

The old impulse was close: say what would change, offer a better timetable, make even this admission useful enough to become another instruction.

He let the impulse pass.

His son did not need an improved childhood assigned from a hospital bed.

"Ab tum jo chaahte ho, use sunna padega mujhe," he said.

"Agar achha na lage toh?"

"Tab bhi pehle sunoonga."

It was not a promise never to be frightened or disagree. It was a promise about what the man would do before he let either feeling become an order.

---

Rudra could remember accepting a hand after a fall only when he had become too tired to refuse it.

He did not have to decide this morning what every such memory meant. Nor did he have to turn one better answer into evidence that nothing bad had happened.

"Main abhi aapse gussa hoon," he said.

"Pata hai."

"Baat bhi karni hai."

"Karo."

For a little while that was all the repair either could manage: not going away, not pretending the anger had been a misunderstanding, leaving room for another sentence.

The nurse asked Rudra whether he needed the call ended. He did. The question had cost more than he had expected, even without a raised voice.

He told his father plainly.

"Aaraam karo," Ram said.

This time the words followed an answer instead of replacing one.

---

After the phone was lowered, Rudra watched the nurse move the stand clear.

His father had wanted him alive. He had known that, somewhere, before he knew Pataal or Daavansh. The knowledge had never quite been enough to explain why being loved could feel so much like being prepared for a death somebody else had already pictured.

Now he could ask the man who had done the picturing.

Not every answer would be good. Not every good answer would come soon enough to undo what it explained. He could still ask, and decide what to do with the answer without offering the whole rest of his life as proof that he had understood.

Vedant returned after speaking to the team about the next care interval. Rudra asked whether he could try sitting somewhere other than the bed.

The man did not turn the request into a drill.

"Team se poochhenge," he said.

Rudra nodded.

A question about an ordinary chair could have an ordinary answer, smaller than all the things the exposed colours had made other people fear. He wanted to find out what that answer would be, not discover whether he could force his body to ignore it.

---

While they waited for the assessment, he rested with his eyes closed.

No battlefield arrived. No lost parent spoke through the space behind his ribs. He had heard enough of real voices this morning to recognize that another person's history did not have to appear inside him before it counted as his family's.

The wrist lay still in its brace.

When the nurse came back with the next member of the team, he opened his eyes and listened.

---

The answer was a short trial, with help.

The clinician and therapist checked the morning's findings and what happened when his position changed. The chest symptoms had stayed quieter. That allowed a small step in the plan, not a declaration that the night of the shattering had left nothing behind.

He could be moved into the supported chair within the bay. He was not to stand, push through the wrist or put the injured ankle down to take his weight. The team would do the transfer with the equipment, keeping the supports and monitoring in place. They would decide together when he needed to go back.

Rudra listened to the whole answer.

Then he asked whether wanting to stop would count as the trial failing.

The therapist looked at him.

"Jab thako toh bataana hai. Wahi karna hai."

He nodded before the old shame could turn that into another instruction he had to endure silently.

---

The chair had to be made ready first.

The nurse checked the space, the supports and the things which would have to move with him. Vedant stepped out of the working area instead of offering the injured forearm as one more place for a patient to lean. The team explained what they needed Rudra to do and, more importantly, what they did not want him trying to do.

He wanted to help.

The wanting was almost physical: take some of his own weight, stop being a heavy piece of work, show that he understood how much trouble everybody was taking.

"Haath se mat dhakelna," the therapist reminded him.

He let the hand remain where it had been placed.

They moved him with the transfer equipment, the injured wrist protected and the leg supported. The leads and support went with him. Nobody asked him to prove he could stand between the bed and chair before they would let him sit.

When they had finished adjusting him, the nurse asked what he felt.

He told her.

She listened to the ordinary answer instead of searching the colours at his collar for a more important one.

---

The room looked different from the chair.

Not transformed. The same curtain had a faint stain lower down which he had not noticed from the bed. The shelf on the opposite side seemed nearer the floor. He could see the line where a fresh coat of paint had stopped short of a bracket and left a little island of the older colour behind.

Somebody had painted this room badly and people had continued being treated in it.

The thought pleased him for reasons he could not have explained without making it sound foolish.

"Kaisa lag raha hai?" Vedant asked from beyond the working space.

Rudra looked at the empty bed.

"Kisi aur ka lag raha hai."

The man followed his gaze.

"Wapas tumhara hi hoga."

"Jaanta hoon. Itni jaldi kabzaa chhodne waala nahi."

The joke took no force from the wrist, no bright line from the chest. It cost only enough breath to make him aware that he should not keep adding to it.

He rested.

---

A short while later he said he was getting tired.

He almost improved the statement with a promise that he could manage longer if necessary. The promise reached his mouth and stayed there, unused.

The therapist checked him. They did not call the fatigue betrayal, and they did not decide that admission required him to remain sitting until he had overcome it. The team prepared the return.

He let them help again.

Back in the bed, the familiar support beneath his ankle felt less like the edge of a world he could never leave. He had left it by a few feet and returned without having to pretend that the feet did not matter.

His wrist ached with the tiredness of the day, not because he had secretly pushed through it. He answered the nurse's question about it and let her check the position rather than treating silence as the price of keeping the chair permission.

The next attempt would depend on the next assessment.

No one had cleared a walk, a journey or a fight.

---

After he had rested, Vedant asked what could be included in the update to the agreed contacts.

Rudra wanted them to know about the chair. He also wanted the limits included, so that nobody waiting in another city would hear sat up and begin planning a homecoming the team had not approved.

The receiving contact passed on that small, accurate account: a short fully assisted trial in the bay, return for fatigue, supports and observation still needed, no loading or travel clearance.

No family history went with it.

In Varanasi, Ram heard the update after care and asked the contact to thank the team. Rukmini heard it in the nearby room, asked whether the return had been planned rather than another emergency and was told it had.

In Mirzapur, Sitara checked that the girls were awake before giving them the same permitted care news. Aadhya smiled at the idea of Rudra pretending not to like an ordinary chair. Ira asked no one to make the news into proof that he was ready for more.

At the Prayagraj post, Veer heard it after his own rest.

The chair had travelled nowhere.

The truth about it had reached the people Rudra wanted told.

---

The call to Veer came after another rest, when the team at the post confirmed he could speak.

"Chair tak pahunch gaye," his friend said.

"Haan. Baahar ghoomne mat aa jaana. Team ko uthaa ke waapas rakhna pada."

"Main khud kaun sa darwaaze tak daud raha hoon."

The humour stayed between the two bodies they actually had. Veer shifted with help before the phone was placed closer; the shoulder had settled after the night's check but was not going to hold the device for him. Rudra waited until he said he was ready.

Then he gave his friend the missing parts of the account.

Ram had held the authority above human Kuls, those governing the Lokas, responsible for choosing the massacre. Not another local rival family. The father had also named the opponent he defeated six years before Rudra's birth: Adhira. That encounter had made an existing fear worse. The training had come years later, when Rudra was one; the sequence did not make its cost necessary.

He did not supply the fight his father had remembered in private. He knew only the parts Ram had actually told him.

Veer listened.

"Aur tu?" he asked.

---

Rudra was ready to explain what he thought about the authority, the killing and the way power could make a child into a threat before the child could act.

That was not what Veer had asked.

"Mera kya?"

"Itni baat sunke. Abhi kaisa hai?"

He looked at the brace, then away from it. The question was not a request for another medical report either.

"Thoda halka," he said. "Phir uske liye bhi bura lagta hai. Unke saath itna hua, aur mujhe—"

He stopped before making relief into another offence which the dead would supposedly want punished.

Veer did not finish the accusation for him.

"Halka kyun?" he asked.

"Papa ne bola, mujhe unka badla lene ke liye nahi paida kiya."

The words sounded younger than the history surrounding them. He had needed them more than he wanted his friend to hear.

"Achha hai ki bola," Veer said.

He did not add that this made the rest acceptable.

---

"Main kuchh nahi karoonga, aisa bhi nahi hai," Rudra said quickly.

"Maine kab kaha?"

"Bas. Aisa na lage—"

"Tu mujhse bhi number le raha hai?"

Rudra shut his eyes.

There it was, caught without being made into a joke: the instinct to demonstrate that he had taken the history seriously by offering something difficult in return.

"Shayad."

At the post, Veer looked towards the passage while somebody waited for a nurse to finish with another patient. Nobody outside the call was keeping a mark for how well a boy had heard his family's dead.

"Mujhe bataana hai toh bata," he said. "Mere liye apna iraada bada mat kar."

Rudra stayed quiet long enough to hear the difference between a friend who would stand beside a choice and a friend demanding a choice impressive enough to stand beside.

He wanted the first kind.

He wanted to learn how to offer it back.

---

"Tera kal shoulder zyada dard kar raha tha?" he asked.

Veer gave him the actual answer. It had been checked; the changed position helped, and he still needed the support. The next movements were the ones the team permitted, not whatever the young man might decide was tolerable once the phone went away.

"Mujhe bol sakta hai," Rudra said.

"Bol diya."

He almost laughed.

"Haan."

There had been a time when he would have taken the short answer as dismissal. Now he could hear that no brave ending had been added for his benefit. His friend's shoulder hurt. Somebody qualified had checked it. A reply did not have to become a rescue before it counted as care.

They talked a little about the college page and the way both of them had begun wanting ordinary unfinished work merely because it belonged to a future less immediate than the next injury.

Veer made no promise that catching up would be easy.

Rudra did not ask for one.

---

Before ending, he asked that the new history stay within the call for now.

"Haan," Veer said. "Tu khud bataana jise bataana hai."

"Girls ko mere janam se pehle waali poori baat nahi boli abhi."

"Meri taraf se nahi jaayegi."

There was no grand secrecy oath in it. Just a destination the message did not have permission to reach.

The nurse at Rudra's end asked whether he was ready to stop. He said yes without waiting for the voice to change from request to instruction.

"Shaam ko Papa se phir baat karni hai," he told Veer. "Agar dono taraf bolne dein."

"Theek."

His friend did not ask him to report the whole conversation afterwards.

The line ended. In the quiet which followed, Rudra found that the thing he wanted to ask his father had survived being left unspoken for an afternoon.

It was not a boast or a promise to hurt somebody.

For once, he did not need to make it sound like either before he could take it seriously.

---

Rukmini's shoulder was reviewed that afternoon.

She answered for herself: what hurt, what helped, which movement still pulled. Dadi carried the small bag and let her speak. The prescribed support and limits remained; having managed the previous day did not make the next one free of care.

When she was settled again, she asked after Ram. The receiving team told her he was resting before the evening interval. She left him to it.

There were years in which she might have taken worry from one bedside to another, believing that keeping every fear in circulation was the same as keeping everyone safe. Today she had told the clinician what was happening in her own body. She could let that be work enough for a little while.

She ate when the food came.

The son she wanted to protect had been helped into a chair and back to bed. She did not need to become unwell herself to prove how much the news meant.

---

Ram woke wanting to tell the boy not to try again too soon.

He could already hear the message: rest first, do not push, we cannot afford another night like that. Sensible words, until he listened to whom the we was making responsible for his fear.

The team had supervised the trial. The son had reported fatigue instead of hiding it. They had returned him to bed before the attempt became an emergency. Ram had heard all those things in the update and still begun planning an instruction as though nobody had thought to keep the boy safe until he did.

He asked the nurse to confirm the plan he himself was meant to follow that evening.

She did. He listened.

Then he asked whether the family call was still possible after care. The receiving contact checked the other end, and the answer came back with the same condition as before: short, if both could manage it.

He did not send the warning ahead of the call.

He would ask what the chair had been like.

---

When the line connected, Rudra expected his father to begin with a correction.

It was almost funny how faithfully the body prepared for an old conversation. His shoulders had tightened before Ram said a word, though he had done precisely what the clinicians asked.

"Suna, chair pe baithe the," his father said.

"Madad se."

"Pata hai. Kaisa laga?"

Rudra waited for the rest of the sentence.

None came.

"Achha," he said. "Phir thak gaya. Waapas rakh diya."

Ram's breath made a small sound against the phone. The nurse helped him settle; Rudra waited until the movement was finished instead of taking the sound for a judgement.

"Ab dard badha hai?" Ram asked.

"Check hua. Position theek ki. Abhi rest hi."

"Theek."

His father did not grant the permission a second time, as though the team's first permission had been provisional until he agreed. He did not promise a bigger movement tomorrow either.

Rudra felt his shoulders ease by a little.

---

"Aapko laga mujhe rokna chahiye tha?" he asked.

The words had come before he could make them polite.

Ram considered hiding the impulse. He had not acted on it; perhaps there was no need to give the boy another reason to distrust an answer which had been better this time.

"Pehle laga," he said. "Update poori suni. Tumne thakaan bataayi. Team thi. Phir apni taraf se hukum bhejne ka kya matlab?"

Rudra kept his eyes on the stand.

This was not his father becoming a different man in three days. The fear was still there. The thing which had changed was smaller and more difficult to trust: Ram had felt it and had not immediately made it somebody else's task.

"Kal phir aisa lage toh?"

"Poore haal poochhoonga. Sirf darr se faisla nahi bhejoonga."

Rudra did not tell him that this made everything all right.

"Theek," he said.

He could hear the promise without awarding it more years of proof than it had yet earned.

---

His mother was resting after her own care. Ram told him she had eaten and that Dadi was nearby; he did not turn her absence from the call into a worry the son needed to solve.

Rudra said he would speak to her at another permitted time.

"Unse jo baat ki, woh aapse chhupaane ke liye nahi thi," he added. "Par meri thi. Unki thi."

"Pata hai," Ram said. "Unse tumhara jawaab nahi maanga."

The son could not see the earlier half-question his father had stopped himself asking. He did not receive a claim that there had never been an impulse to intrude. He received the boundary which had survived it.

For a moment neither spoke.

Beyond the curtain in Prayagraj, somebody asked whether an ordinary bag could be moved off the spare chair. It could. The nurse moved it and returned to the work in front of her.

Rudra listened to the small answer.

He wanted the next question he asked to belong to him in the same plain way.

---

Elsewhere, the sheet carried away from the brass bowl lay open under a lamp.

A hand set a small weight beside the copied trace. The page flattened. Red-black and gold remained distinct in the marks which had been made when the concealment failed; folding the paper had not turned the two into one colour.

Another hand moved the lamp closer.

No hospital name appeared in the light. No ward, bed or road arrived on the page. It was a record of a signature, not a window into the life from which the signature came.

The sheet was covered with a second, blank piece of paper. Both were put aside together. The lamp stayed burning after the hands withdrew.

In Prayagraj, Rudra saw none of it.

The faint colours visible at his collar had not become a joined hiding edge. The room in which he spoke to his father remained an ordinary room around a body which could no longer be concealed in the old way.

---

"Jitna suna, usmein sab kuchh marne ke aas-paas aa jaata hai," he said.

Ram listened.

"Woh log mar rahe the. Aapko maar dete. Mujhe bachna hai."

He did not say any of those things was false. He could not make them less terrible by asking the account to become more pleasant.

"Par log the na. Sirf kisi ke khilaaf taaqat nahi."

"Haan," his father said.

Rudra looked at the place where the page had lain beside his bag. A few lines of college work had been enough to remind him that other people had entire afternoons outside the history he was hearing. Surely there had been ordinary hours in the lives being destroyed too, whether anybody could now tell him exactly what had happened in them or not.

He would not furnish those hours with invented voices. His father had refused to do that for him, and he understood the refusal better now.

Wanting something was not evidence that it could be remembered.

It was still a reason to ask what could be known.

---

"Mujhe unke baare mein jaanne ka mann hai," he said. "Isliye nahi ki phir main kuchh saabit karoon."

The last part came more firmly than the first.

Ram had begun the account because his son asked what the kavach had hidden. The boy had not asked to take charge of all the history which came out with the name.

He did not try to finish the desire for him.

"Sunoonga," he said.

Rudra waited through a small noise from the other ward. His father's nurse was making an adjustment. There was nothing hidden in the interruption; when Ram answered the check, the boy could hear that he was still there.

He had spent years learning to read a pause as preparation for the next demand.

This one could remain time given to another person's injured body.

---

He thought about Varanasi.

The approach to the ghats at an hour when the stalls were only beginning to open. The college table. The broken stones of the courtyard, which he could remember hurting and still remember as the place where evening light reached the neem first.

He did not want those memories removed so a grander origin could take their place. He had friends there. Work left unfinished. A mother whose laughter could catch on pain now, a father who had once wasted five furious minutes arguing with a screw.

Daavansh had not erased that man.

Pataal did not have to erase the city in which the man had become his father.

But keeping Varanasi could not require him to make everything before it into a name which appeared only when somebody needed him frightened enough to train.

There had been a place from which a child was sent. There were lives connected to his own which he could not reach by looking harder at the inside of his eyelids.

The distance was real.

He wanted to stop treating it as something shameful to want to cross.

---

"Ma ko laga toh nahi ki main ghar ki baat bhool jaaoonga?" he asked.

"Unse poochhna," Ram said gently. "Unki taraf se nahi boloonga. Main apni keh sakta hoon."

"Kahiye."

"Jinhone mujhe paala, unke saath mera jeena sach tha. Usse pehle jo tha, woh jaanne se jhooth nahi ho gaya. Tum bhi dono ko jaan sakte ho."

Rudra let the answer stay within what it could honestly give. His father's permission to want did not answer for his mother, approve an expedition or make an injured ankle ready for a doorway.

It did not have to do all those things to matter.

"Main aaj kahin jaane ki baat nahi kar raha," he said.

"Pata hai."

At the far end, Ram did not ask him to prove the sentence by withdrawing whatever came after it.

---

Rudra could feel the tiredness coming back.

The phone was still supported. He did not tighten his hand, hold a breath or ask the colours for the strength to finish. The nurses in both places were near enough to hear if either patient needed help. His mother was allowed to remain resting. His friends were not waiting on the line to turn his next words into a pledge.

For once he did not need an audience to make the question brave.

He had thought, before the first name, that the story would tell him which person to become. It had given him a father who could be loved and questioned, two real belongings in that father's life and dead people whose existence did not require him to promise another death.

What he wanted now was not an answer hidden in his own strength.

It was to stand, one day and only when he could, in a place whose existence he had finally been allowed to know.

He kept his foot supported and his voice plain.

"Papa," he said. "Aap mujhe Pataal dikhaayenge?"

---

# CHAPTER EIGHTY-SEVEN — THE RUINS HE COULD NOT REMEMBER

---

> *"There were places I inherited only as an absence.*
> *Seeing them would not make me remember them."*
>
> *— Rudra's later notes, before the first journey*

---

"Haan," Ram said. "Wahaan jaane ki taiyaari mein tumhaari madad karoonga. Abhi tumhe le chalne ka waada nahi kar sakta."

Rudra looked at the phone.

He had prepared himself to argue for the wish. The answer left him with the unfamiliar work of explaining it without having to defend his right to want it first.

"Abhi nahi bol raha," he said.

"Suna maine."

Ram took the next breath carefully. At the far end, the nurse checked his position before letting him continue.

"Tumhara ilaaj. Raasta. Wahaan ke haalaat. Sab dekhna padega. Main khud saath chal paaoonga, iska bhi abhi daava nahi karoonga. Par sawaal ko galat bolke band nahi karoonga."

The answer had boundaries which belonged to real things. Not a test of whether the son could become strong enough to stop asking. Not a date hidden behind an order to be patient.

Rudra let the first tightness leave his good hand.

"Wahaan kya milega?"

His father's silence was brief.

"Ujaad," he said. "Bahut kuchh khandhar hai. Jis duniya se mujhe bheja gaya, use bachaa ke nahi rakha gaya."

---

Rudra had known about the killing.

He had not understood how much of his own picture still depended on an intact doorway, a place in which someone could stand and say this is where the child belonged. His mind had been keeping a house ready while his father spoke of people who had not survived to bring its child back.

"Aapka ghar?"

"Main tumhe apne bachpan ke kamre nahi dikha sakta," Ram said. "Mera yaad waala bachpan yahaan hai. Jo wahaan hua, baad mein jaana."

He did not claim a remembered route from one doorstep to another. Nor did he give the boy an address which would turn a whole ruined place into a promise about one particular wall.

"Toh jaake—"

Rudra stopped.

What had he been expecting to do? Recognize a window? Find that his body knew the turn into a lane it had never walked? He had already refused the borrowed faces of the dead. The borrowed house was harder to release because it had seemed such a modest thing to want.

His father waited.

"Phir bhi dekhna hai," Rudra said.

"Haan."

The second yes was not a permission repeated. It was a man hearing what remained of a wish after the comforting part had been taken out of it.

---

"Mujhe kuchh yaad nahi aa jaayega wahaan," Rudra said.

He meant it as a question, then heard that it was not one.

He had never lived there. His father's beginnings did not give his own mind a childhood beneath another sky.

"Tum dekhoge," Ram said. "Jo tumhaare saamne hoga. Yaad hone ka naatak nahi karna padega."

A ruined place would not have to recognize him for the destruction to have been real. His grandfather and grandmother would not become less his people if he could stand beside what was left and feel nothing more useful than the ordinary ignorance of a grandson who had never met them.

He had been frightened of failing even that.

The discovery made him angry in a quiet, tired way. He had found another examination in a place where nobody had yet asked him to perform.

"Main samajhna chahta hoon," he said.

"Samajhne ke liye apne ko yaad dilaana zaroori nahi jo kabhi dekha hi nahi."

---

They ended because the teams asked them to end.

The request had been answered. The unknowns had not been disguised as a journey already arranged. Ram would help with the wish; neither injured man had promised to carry the other out of care because the story had reached a point which felt like a departure.

Rudra asked for the phone to be lowered and told the nurse he was tired. The ankle support was checked before the night settled into its quieter work.

He slept more deeply in the first interval than he expected.

Later he woke thinking of the house he had made up. There had been a courtyard in it, of course. His own imagination had supplied the same square of open sky he had spent years wanting to leave, then placed it in a world he knew almost nothing about.

He let that courtyard go.

Beyond the curtain somebody was speaking softly to a patient who had been awake longer than he had. The voice repeated an ordinary instruction until it was understood, with no anger at the time understanding took.

Rudra listened until he could sleep again.

---

Day forty-two began after a staffed night, not after an unseen journey.

The morning review took the chair trial into account. His chest symptoms had remained quieter; the team still wanted observation and the same protection for the injured wrist and ankle. Another assisted trial would depend on their assessment later, not on his father's answer about Pataal.

After rest, Rudra asked whether he could speak to his mother.

She should hear the wish from him, not from a warning passed around the family before he had found the words for it himself.

---

Rukmini was ready for the call after her own morning care.

The phone stood on the table beside her supported shoulder. Dadi had helped place the water within reach and then moved far enough away to leave the conversation to the two people having it. Ram was resting in the ward, not listening from the next chair.

"Ma," Rudra said. "Maine Papa se Pataal dekhne ko bola. Jab jaana ho sake."

There was a little quiet at the other end.

He waited without improving the sentence into something safer.

"Tum dekhna chaahte ho," she said.

"Haan."

"Woh kya bole?"

He gave her the answer, including the limits. Help with preparing, no departure now, no certainty that Ram could go with him. A ruined place, not a house kept ready for his arrival.

"Mujhe aapse khud kehna tha," he finished.

Rukmini looked at the water. The fingers of her good hand had tightened without moving towards it.

"Achha kiya," she said.

---

"Aapko bura laga?"

"Darr laga."

The honesty was immediate enough to hurt.

"Phir?"

"Phir tumhari baat sun rahi hoon."

She did not deny the fear so he would believe her generous. Nor did she put it in his hands as something he had to cure before he could want anything for himself.

"Main ghar bhoolne ko nahi—"

"Pata hai," she said, then stopped herself. "Tum bolo."

Rudra looked towards the opening of the bay. Somebody carried a tray past it and disappeared into the next ordinary part of the morning.

"Mujhe lagta tha, jo jaanna hai woh sab mere andar se nikalna chahiye. Taaqat hai toh samajh bhi aa jaayega. Ab Papa bol rahe hain, mainne dekha hi nahi. Mujhe kuchh yaad nahi ho sakta."

His voice had grown rougher.

"Phir bhi jaana hai. Kya bacha hai, dekhna hai."

Rukmini let the words reach their own end.

"Ghar yaad rakhne ke liye har doosri jagah se aankh band karna zaroori nahi," she said.

---

She asked whether he imagined leaving as soon as he could reach a chair.

"Nahi. Team ne abhi chalne ko bhi nahi bola."

"Aur tumhare saath jo log honge?"

"Abhi kisi ko kaha nahi."

"Unse poochhna. Unki chot. Unka mann. Sirf tumhara faisla hoga toh unka saath nahi hoga."

Rudra heard the distinction without yet knowing how difficult asking would feel.

He could imagine Veer saying yes before he had finished, could imagine making that quick yes into proof that no one needed any more time. His mother was asking him to leave room for the answer which would cost him more to hear.

"Aapko bhi nahi bolna padega ki chalo," he said.

"Maine apni taraf se kaha hi nahi."

He smiled at the dry precision.

She did too, though neither could see it. She wanted her son alive and near; the wish was not made false by refusing to turn nearness into the condition on which she would love him.

---

He told her about the college page.

Not the whole work. Only that Vedant had read a little and he had wanted it to stop, then wanted to know it would still be there later. Rukmini asked whether the page had been put back in the bag instead of left where somebody might clear it away with a tray.

"Haan, Ma."

"Theek."

"Bas itna? Padhne ko nahi bolengi?"

"Padhoge jab padh paaoge. Kaagaz sambhaalne ko bola hai. Tumse aaj poora college nahi sambhalwaa rahi."

The laugh surprised him. It pulled a little at the chest; he stopped, told the nurse when she checked and let her make sure nothing new had changed before they continued.

Rukmini waited without talking over the check.

"Aap bhi rest karna," he said afterwards.

"Karoongi."

He had heard adults use the future tense as a place to hide things they had no intention of doing. Today she told him when, and who would help her settle.

He believed that small part because it could happen.

---

They ended before either had to make the other ask twice.

Dadi came back to help with the support. Rukmini told her that Rudra wanted to see the place his father came from one day. Not the details of their private call, and not a journey the family had secretly arranged without asking anyone else.

"Abhi?" Dadi asked.

"Nahi. Jab ho sake."

Dadi nodded, adjusted the thing which had been slipping and asked whether Rukmini wanted the water moved closer.

There was comfort in that order of work. The great distance could remain a great distance for the moment. A glass was in the wrong place; somebody could fix that now.

Rukmini drank, then rested as she had said she would.

---

In Mirzapur, Ira was trying to listen to Aadhya read without helping.

It was more difficult than reading had once been. Aadhya would stop at the end of a sentence to decide whether she liked it, and Ira would already be preparing a correction to something which had not been a question. Twice she caught herself. The third time she interrupted, and Aadhya stopped altogether.

"Ab bol hi do."

"Kuchh nahi."

"Tumhaara kuchh nahi bahut bolta hai."

Ira smiled with her eyes closed. The ordinary right eye had reached the limit of what she wanted to ask from it before the next rest; the left remained covered. She could hear the paper being turned without needing to turn the sound into a different kind of sight.

"Woh line chhoti karo," she admitted.

"Meri line hai."

"Isliye poochh rahi hoon."

"Poochha kab?"

They were still arguing over that when Sitara came with the proposed call.

Rudra wanted to tell them a little more of the family account. Had enough rest passed? Did they want it? Aadhya glanced at Ira rather than answering for both. Ira said yes. The team confirmed the short interval, and the paper was put aside.

---

Rudra asked first whether he had already told them about the fight six years before his birth.

"Nahi," Ira said. "Uski baat nahi hui."

He was glad she answered plainly. The days had begun putting information into different small conversations, and he did not want a friend left guessing because he remembered saying a thing somewhere.

He told them what Ram had actually said: the fight with Adhira, the defeat, the injuries, coming home without leaving fear outside. The encounter had happened years before his training, which began when he was one. Knowing the order did not make every lesson or every hurt necessary.

He had no detailed scene to give them. His father's remembered struggle had not played itself inside the call.

Aadhya asked whether the fear explained what Rudra had lived with.

"Kuchh samajh aa raha hai," he said. "Iska matlab jo hua sab zaroori tha, woh nahi."

"Haan."

She did not make him argue the distinction again to earn her agreement.

---

Then he told them he wanted to see Pataal.

The phone stayed on its support while he spoke. Ira's hand lay still; no notebook opened, no mirror supplied a view of the place being named.

"Papa bol rahe hain, khandhar hai," he said. "Unka bachpan yahaan yaad hai. Wahaan ka ghar yaad karke dikhaane jaisa nahi."

Aadhya was quiet for a moment.

"Phir bhi dekhna hai?"

"Haan."

"Theek."

The simplicity annoyed him before he understood it.

"Bas theek?"

"Tu chahta hai main abhi bataa doon wahaan jaake tujhe kya mehsoos hoga?"

He looked towards the curtain.

"Nahi."

"Mujhe bhi nahi pata."

A laugh escaped him, smaller and safer than the one in the morning. He let it end there.

She was not leaving him alone with the wish. She was refusing to furnish it with a response neither of them could honestly promise.

---

"Mainne abhi koi safar tay nahi kiya," he said. "Raasta bhi nahi. Team ne yahaan se nikalne ko nahi bola."

Sitara had remained within call distance without taking the conversation over. When Aadhya needed the hand moved, she helped and waited for the girl's answer before letting the talk continue.

Rudra heard the pause and did not apologize for it on his own behalf.

These were bodies with their own histories of the last weeks, not a waiting set of companions placed beside his father's story until he called them in.

"Tum dono se bhi baat karni hai," he said. "Par pehle khud samajh loon main kya poochh raha hoon."

"Hum apna jawaab de denge jab poochhoge," Ira said. "Hamaari taraf se pehle mat likh lena."

He could hear the smile in it. He could also hear that she meant what she said.

"Nahi likhoonga."

---
