After the call, Aadhya asked whether Ira wanted the paragraph read once more.

"Abhi nahi."

"Theek."

The girl beside her had a real belonging she had chosen. She also had questions about her birth for which the empty places in a record were still only empty places. Ram's story had not quietly filled them, and wanting to hear it did not make Ira a person who existed only to keep somebody else's past straight.

Aadhya put the paper down within the helper's reach and asked about the next rest interval instead of recruiting her friend into another attempt at work.

Sitara gave them the answer from the care plan, not from how much determination she thought two nineteen-year-olds ought to have.

In Prayagraj, Rudra had the stand moved away and lay back, tired from having said the same history in his own words.

The girls knew the part he had chosen to tell them now.

He did not have to keep talking until it became a story simple enough for all three of them to feel the same thing about it.

---

There was no departure on the morning of day forty-three.

There was a check before the next assisted movement, another adjustment to the support beneath the ankle and a warning not to confuse a quieter chest with permission to push through the braced wrist. Rudra listened without treating each limit as a postponement invented by somebody who disliked where he wanted to go.

He sat with help again. This time he noticed the moment before tiredness became difficult to admit.

He told the therapist then.

The return to bed was ordinary enough that no one else in the bay stopped what they were doing. He found that easier to bear than praise would have been. He had not climbed out of pain by becoming exceptional. He had said what was happening, and the people working with him had been able to use the answer.

Later, when the team checked whether anything had grown worse, he did not use the success of the morning to improve his reply.

The wrist still ached. The ankle still needed its protection.

They checked both.

---

The next night he slept poorly.

Day forty-four's trial was shorter. He hated that more than he expected, the small reduction feeling like something being taken back, until the therapist asked whether he wanted the care plan to answer yesterday's body or today's.

"Aaj ka," he said, with no grace at all.

"Toh aaj ka bataao."

He did.

Nobody called the shorter interval defeat. Nobody promised that wanting Pataal badly enough would make the following morning longer. He had seen enough real costs to know that a body did not become honest merely because its owner had learned the correct words about being patient.

He was still learning to use them when he was disappointed.

The phone spent most of the afternoon away from his face. The agreed contacts received the care update he permitted; no one turned it into an order for another round of difficult conversations while he was tired.

In the evening he listened to a little ordinary news from Veer, not another verdict on the family history. His friend had his own review and his own rest to get through. They ended before either needed to pretend otherwise.

---

In Varanasi, Ram began measuring the day less often by whether he had been useful to somebody else.

It was not a peaceful habit to acquire. He would wake with an instruction already prepared, find the instruction had no proper destination and have to remain with the fear underneath it instead of making it into a task.

The nurse asked about his pain. He answered. The site was checked. He accepted help with the position which made breathing easier and did not offer a demonstration of how well he could control discomfort.

Rukmini came after her own care, stayed for the period she could manage and left before the shoulder had to insist. Dhruv's agreed visits and the family's nearby arrangement did not replace the shifts of people whose work continued through the night.

Once, Ram began saying he ought to be doing something.

Dadi looked at the cup he had not finished.

"Woh karo," she said.

He looked at her, then at the cup. Rukmini laughed quietly, and for a moment the room contained an irritation so familiar that no one had to explain how they belonged to one another.

Ram drank with help.

The world did not become safe while he did it. He had still needed water.

---

At the tournament ground in Prayagraj, a worker pressed a boot beside the repaired length and watched the edge give.

The organizer had come hoping to reopen that part of the ground. He crouched, saw the movement and straightened without asking the man to stamp a little less hard the next time.

"Abhi nahi," he told the people waiting at the rope.

Somebody complained that the fighting had finished days ago. It had. That did not make the repair hold. The worker wiped the tool, put it aside and asked about the hours already done; the answer concerned payment, not how much more risk the ground could be made to carry before another claim arrived.

The organizer checked those hours and paid what was due for that work. It reduced what remained in the reserve. The other unresolved claims did not vanish because this one could be met.

Beyond the rope, two people retold the final score differently and were corrected by a third. None of them knew what had been said in a family call at a hospital, and the ground's slow repair supplied no answer about anybody's blood.

The damaged length stayed closed.

---

By day forty-five, the quieter stretches in Rudra's breathing had held through further observation.

The team had reduced support only as the checks allowed, watching the periods without it rather than announcing that it had become unnecessary forever. He remained in the hospital. The same injured limbs still set limits on transfers, and a supported chair inside the ward was still the extent of the attempt, not permission to walk out through the receiving desk.

The days had contained actual sleep, care and repeated individual reviews. Nothing had healed in a missing interval because a future journey needed people ready.

When he was rested enough for a short call, he asked for his father.

Not to test whether the answer about Pataal had survived three uneventful days. It had. He had begun to notice another part of the story, one which had been there since Ram said who raised him and which the massacre had temporarily pushed to the edge.

The people in Varanasi had taught the child who arrived.

They had taught him how to mend.

---

At the other end, Ram heard his son ask whether he wanted to talk about something quieter.

"Kya?"

"Jinhone aapko paala. Unke yahaan seekhne ki baat."

Ram's good hand lay open beside the blanket. A small crease from the sheet ran across the palm and disappeared when he moved the fingers.

He could remember those fingers shorter, clumsier, reaching to help before they knew what helping would require.

"Poochho," he said.

---

"Unhone aapko badalne ko kaha tha?" Rudra asked. "Jo aap paida hue the, usse alag banne ko?"

Ram looked at his open hand.

"Asuran hona chhodke nahi paala mujhe," he said. "Aur jo unse seekha, woh mera nahi hua, aisa bhi nahi. Dono the."

The answer did not divide him into a dangerous child and a respectable skill. Rudra had begun to understand how quickly a story could be made to do that: keep the part which mended, put the part which fought somewhere outside the household and call the arrangement belonging.

His father was refusing the division.

"Chhota tha," Ram said. "Kaam dekhna chahta tha. Haath badha deta tha jahaan abhi poochhna chahiye tha."

Rudra smiled a little.

He had trouble imagining the man clumsy, though the broken pot in the earlier memory should already have taught him better. A child's reach came into the account, eager and inconvenient, not the arrival of a fighter whose whole life had been waiting to turn useful.

---

"Daant padti thi?"

"Haan."

"Phir?"

"Phir kaam samjhaate the. Kya karna hai. Kya abhi nahi karna. Kab sirf paas baithna hai."

Ram moved the fingers again, remembering the effort it once took to keep them out of the way.

"Jiska dard hai, uski baat pehle. Mujhe kya karna achha lagega, woh baad mein."

Rudra lay quiet.

The nurse outside the bay answered a question. Somebody had asked to wait before being moved, and the answer was a small adjustment to the work, made without any great declaration about kindness.

He could hear his father describing something which happened in ordinary care all around him.

"Yeh unhone sikhaaya?"

"Haan. Baar-baar. Main jaldi karna chahta tha."

No named state had appeared in the answer. No secret instruction which the son could apply to make the wrist inside its brace stop hurting. His father was talking about the child who learned beside other people, not offering a power in return for understanding his past.

---

Ram could remember the impatience more clearly than he could remember how long each lesson took.

He had wanted the result. A person comfortable, a wound no longer pulling everybody's attention towards it, the pleased moment when somebody could say that what he had done helped. The people raising him had made him remain through the slower parts too.

He gave his son only the part he could honestly give now: not every face or every day, but the repeated work of learning to ask before deciding what another body could bear.

The rest would need room to be told properly.

Rudra did not hear that as a new name being withheld. He had the important part already. The man who trained him had not grown up with only one language for pain.

He had learned another.

---

For a moment the son wanted to set the two men apart.

The child at a Vedic household's work, being made to wait. The father in the courtyard, unable to let his own child stop. If they could remain different people, one history would not have to make the other harder to bear.

They were the same person.

That was what Ram had said when the second name first arrived. Ram and Daavansh. The boy who had been sent away and the man who had stayed. The hands which had learned to mend were the hands which had made a frightened child believe that crying meant another part of him was not ready.

Rudra looked down at the support beneath his wrist.

He had wanted the childhood story to make his father easier to understand. It had. Understanding had not moved in the direction he expected.

It meant the man had once been taught how to hear somebody asking him to stop.

---

The phone was still steady on the stand.

Rudra did not reach for it, did not use the good hand to hide the part of his face he could feel changing. In Varanasi, Ram's nurse stayed close enough to help him. His mother's rest did not have to be broken so she could stand between the next question and its destination.

He knew the call could not go on indefinitely. The team had given them this short interval after actual care and sleep, not a whole evening in which the boy could make the past yield an answer by exhausting the man who carried it.

But he could ask one thing plainly.

He did not need to decide in advance whether the answer would make him kinder or angrier. Nor did he have to pretend the people who raised his father had taught him nothing except how to survive.

"Papa," he said. "Aapko jab rukna aata tha, mere liye kyun nahi ruke?"

---

# CHAPTER EIGHTY-EIGHT — WHAT THE HANDS HAD LEARNED

---

> *"Knowing how to mend had not made him incapable of hurting.*
> *It made the hurt harder to explain away."*
>
> *— Rudra's later account of the Vedic household stories*

---

"Maine apne darr ko tumhaare dard se bada maana," Ram said. "Rukna aata tha. Tumhaare saath rukne se lagta tha ki tumhe khatre ke liye chhod raha hoon."

Rudra listened without moving the supported hand.

"Aata tha," he repeated.

"Haan."

The father did not claim he had forgotten kindness or never been shown a different way. The terror had been real. So had the choices he made while obeying it.

"Dard rukne ki wajah thi," Ram said. "Maine use aur taiyaari ki wajah bana diya."

At the Prayagraj end, the nurse glanced at Rudra's face. He answered her quiet question before speaking again.

"Mujhe aapka bachpan sunna hai," he said. "Par usse jo mera tha, woh chhota nahi hoga."

Ram looked at the open hand on the blanket.

"Nahi hoga."

The sentence asked the boy for nothing afterwards. No forgiveness added to the cost of hearing. No instruction to let the answer settle into a kinder meaning before the call could end.

---

They spoke only a little longer.

Ram said he could tell him what learning beside those people had been like, when both had rested enough for it. Rudra wanted that. He did not want another lesson about how to use his own injured body.

"Kahaani sunni hai," he said.

"Samajh gaya."

When the teams asked them to stop, they stopped with the first answer already given.

The line went quiet. Ram's nurse helped move the phone away from the equipment and checked what needed checking. He told her where the position pulled, waited for the adjustment and heard himself answer properly when she asked whether it was easier.

He had been taught to answer that question long before he had taught his son to bear more than the answer allowed.

The memory came back without a battlefield in it.

---

He was a child at the side of a low workbench.

The bowl was too full. He could remember that clearly: water trembling against the rim, his fingers spread too far around the warm metal, the stubborn belief that if he carried it quickly enough he would arrive before it spilled.

It spilled.

A little over his fingers. More onto the floor beside the bench.

"Dheere," the woman working there said.

He hated the word at once. Slowly was what people said when they had decided not to trust him with the work he wanted.

He set the bowl down too hard. The water jumped again.

The person on the other side of the bench had a cloth around one hand. A red patch had worked through it. Ram looked at the patch instead of the face and felt the important, frightened excitement of being allowed near something which needed to be made better.

He reached for the cloth.

The hand moved away.

---

"Ruko," the injured person said.

Ram's fingers stayed in the air.

He had not pulled hard. He had barely touched it. The refusal felt unfair in the immediate way a child's own good intention can feel like proof that nobody ought to object to what he does next.

The woman put her hand between his and the bench. Not gripping him, not striking the reach away. A plain boundary he could see.

"Suna?"

"Main madad—"

"Pehle suna?"

He looked at her, angry now because the red patch was still there and they were spending time on a word.

"Rukne ko bola."

"Toh pehle rukna hai."

The injured hand had not ceased to need care. Its owner had asked for a moment, and the woman gave it without treating the request as an insult to what she knew how to do.

Ram stood beside the bench with damp fingers and nowhere useful to put them.

He could remember the discomfort of that waiting more sharply than the shape of the room behind it.

---

When the person was ready, the woman asked before moving the cloth.

There was work to do after the yes. Ordinary, careful work. Ram watched the face this time as well as the hand, saw a tightening at the mouth and was about to announce it when the woman had already paused.

"Yahaan kheench raha hai?"

The answer changed what she did next.

He had thought helping meant knowing the right thing and doing it firmly enough that the hurt had to yield.

Here the right thing could change because the person hurting had spoken.

---

The woman worked with the patient's agreement, stopping when the hand tightened, asking again when the answer changed.

Ram wanted to ask when the mending would happen.

He had seen enough to know that the people raising him could do more than replace a cloth. The possibility drew his eyes towards their hands every time somebody came needing help. He wanted the moment which made the rest of the waiting worthwhile.

A warmth gathered beneath the woman's fingers. The bleeding eased. The face across the bench changed, though not into the astonishing relief the boy had been expecting.

There was still pain. The injured hand still needed covering and care.

The woman drew back, tired enough that she let the next task wait while she sat properly. Ram stared at the hand, certain he had missed the part which would make it look as though nothing had happened to it.

It continued to look like a hand somebody had hurt.

"Ab theek?" he asked too quickly.

The patient flexed a little, stopped and said where it still pulled.

The woman listened.

---

Ram remembered feeling almost betrayed.

They had all done the work. The waiting, the asking, the things whose purpose he did not yet understand. Surely the end of it should have been easier to recognize.

"Poora kyun nahi?" he asked after the patient had gone to sit nearby.

The woman looked at him for a moment. She was wiping her hands slowly, giving him time to hear the question he had asked.

"Jitna hua, utna bataaya," she said. "Jo baaki hai, use theek bol doon?"

He knew the answer was no. He did not like that knowing it failed to make the remaining hurt disappear.

"Phir dard hoga?"

"Ho sakta hai. Isliye poochhna hai. Dekhna hai. Jo kaha, woh karna hai."

No one had offered the child a world in which care could guarantee the right ending if only it was performed without doubt. They were teaching him to remain answerable for the work after the part he wanted to call success.

At the time, he mostly wanted to know when he would be allowed to do the remarkable part himself.

---

"Aur yeh?" the woman asked.

He followed her gaze to the water on the floor.

He had forgotten it.

The cloth she gave him was ordinary, worn soft at one edge. He crouched to wipe the spill and found that it had run farther beneath the bench than he thought. The work took longer than carrying the bowl. He was about to stand when another wet patch caught the light.

He wiped that too.

The patient sitting nearby watched him emerge, knees dusty, offended by the distance water could travel when nobody was paying attention.

"Ab phisloonga nahi," the person said.

Ram looked up, unsure whether he was being laughed at.

The smile was tired but not cruel.

He had wanted to be thanked for something great. It took him a while to understand that something small had actually been done, and that making it small did not make the person who might have fallen less real.

---

Later there was food, and a place for him to eat it.

He had not earned that place by mopping the floor. He had it on the days he spilled more, on the days he was told to sit out of the way and stop trying to turn every explanation into permission to touch.

He could remember being impatient with that too.

The house did not become a house full of flawless people in recollection. Sometimes someone spoke sharply. Someone forgot the thing another person had asked for. A correction could arrive with irritation rather than wisdom, and the next correction might be an adult admitting that the first had been too sharp.

What remained was not an absence of anger. It was the repeated possibility that another person's answer could change what happened next.

As a boy he had taken that possibility for granted long before he learned to name what its absence could do.

---

The healing he learned in that household had not been a birth gift arriving with the infant from Pataal.

There had been work, correction, waiting beside people rather than merely watching the moments in which a wound changed. The remembered afternoon was one piece of that upbringing, not a hidden sequence by which another person could copy a movement and acquire it.

He had told his son where the learning came from. He had not told him that a bloodline name could carry all the years between wanting to help and knowing when help was being refused.

Ram opened his eyes in the Varanasi ward.

His nurse was asking whether the new position was still comfortable. He had been quiet only a little while; the work around the bed had continued.

"Ab yahaan kheench raha hai," he said, indicating what he could without straining.

She checked it and helped him settle again.

He did not tell her about the bowl. He did not turn the memory into evidence that, because he knew how care should sound, the present pain ought to be something he could manage alone.

The skill had been real.

So had the lesson he had failed to carry safely into his son's childhood.

---

Rudra did not hear the bowl story that night.

He slept after the evening care, woke for a check and slept again. In the morning of day forty-six, the team reviewed the quieter breathing, the observed periods with less support and what had happened during assisted movement. They did not take the wrist or ankle out of their protections to celebrate the change.

They did agree that he could move to a quieter bed farther along the same ward.

The closer observation of the first nights after the shattering was no longer needed in exactly the same way. The care was not over. The receiving staff would have the plan, the supports and the reasons to call for reassessment; the change of bed was not a discharge or permission to begin walking.

Rudra listened, asked what he would do if the pressure returned and heard the answer from the people who would actually be there.

Only then did he say yes to moving.

---

He had expected to want the duty station's noises gone.

When the time came, he found himself listening to them as though they were something he had been using to hold the room together. A question answered, a drawer closed, the little rise in one voice which meant somebody else needed help now.

Vedant noticed him looking towards the opening.

"Kuchh reh gaya?"

"Nahi."

The man waited.

"Wahaan se kam sunaai dega," Rudra admitted.

"Haan. Team paas hogi. Poochh lo."

He did ask. The nurse showed him where help would be and checked that he could call without reaching through the injured wrist. The answer did not make him foolish for needing it. A quieter bed was allowed to be both something wanted and something unfamiliar.

The bag moved with him. So did the phone, charger, college page and the actual clinical information the receiving team needed. No one made him recite his father's whole history to earn the new space, and no symptom relevant to his care was left behind with the old bed.

---

The transfer was done with help and the equipment.

His ankle did not touch down to carry him. He did not push himself across on the braced wrist. The team kept the support he needed with him, checked him after the move and waited for a proper answer about the new position before going on to the next task.

The bed had a different view of the window.

There was more wall than sky. A branch from somewhere beyond the building entered the upper corner and shifted when the air moved. Rudra watched it for a while, trying to decide whether he liked the view before realizing that no one was going to move him back merely because he had an opinion about it.

"Thoda aur takiya?" the nurse asked.

He said yes.

She adjusted it. He answered when she asked again.

The simplest parts of living kept turning out to require practice.

---

Later, after rest, he asked for the permitted update to the agreed contacts.

Quieter bed in the same ward. Continued care and the same limits on the injured limbs. Assistance still needed. No journey or discharge arranged.

He listened to the wording before it went out. His mother would hear that he had moved and ask where; he wanted the answer to reach her with the first news, not leave her to fill an empty interval with the worst of the old nights.

The contacts confirmed receipt in Varanasi, at Veer's post and in Mirzapur. A short reply from his mother came back asking whether he had his bag.

Rudra smiled.

He asked for yes to be sent, and that he could reach help. He did not add a brave claim about how much better he was feeling than the checks had shown.

Then he let the phone be moved away.

---

The afternoon was quieter than he expected.

Vedant took the rest arranged at handover. The staff went on working beyond the edge of the new view. Rudra could not hear every word, which had been the point of moving, and every small silence did not mean somebody had forgotten the bed was occupied.

He looked at the branch until he stopped waiting for it to become a sign.

No old voice came with the air moving through the leaves. The colours at his collar had faded locally without drawing together into a new concealing edge. What had broken was still broken; the team was helping the person left alive around it.

When the nurse returned, he told her the new position was easier and the wrist was still aching. Both things could be true without one being offered to cancel the other.

She checked, helped and made sure he had what he needed.

He was tired enough afterwards to sleep through the next ordinary noise from the passage.

---

Ram told him the bowl story that evening, after rest and permission at both ends.

Not as an instruction for the injured hand. He described the spilled water, the reach towards somebody who had asked him to wait, the part of the mending which helped without making the wound vanish. He remembered wanting to do something extraordinary and being handed a cloth to wipe the floor.

Rudra listened from the new bed.

"Gussa aaya tha?"

"Bahut."

"Kyun?"

"Mujhe lagta tha madad karne aaya hoon. Jitni mujhe karni thi, utni karne nahi de rahe."

The son looked towards the window. The branch was no longer visible in the dark glass.

"Jiski madad thi, usse poochhna tha," he said.

"Haan."

Neither needed to turn the answer into another accusation before they could hear it. The accusation from the previous call had already reached its proper place.

---

"Aapko ab rukne ko bolte hain toh?" Rudra asked.

Ram looked towards the nurse, who was giving him a little distance without leaving him without help.

"Abhi bhi achha nahi lagta har baar."

The honesty loosened something in the conversation. Rudra had been expecting an account in which his father had finally become wise enough to lie peacefully in a hospital bed.

"Dadi ne kal cup poora karne ko kaha," Ram added. "Mujhe kuchh kaam karna tha. Unhone wahi de diya."

Rudra smiled.

He had not been there to hear it. Now the ordinary family moment belonged to something actually told to him, not to a silence he had to read correctly.

"Kiya?"

"Madad se."

"Achha hai."

The little reversal surprised both of them. The son was not assigning a task. The father was not being examined on whether accepting help made him less the man he had been.

They ended when they needed to. Rudra did not offer forgiveness to make the ending kind. Ram did not ask why the story had failed to produce it.

---

Day forty-seven brought its own checks and fatigue.

Rudra's quieter bed remained a place of treatment. The new staff knew the course of the chest symptoms and the limits on movement; the braced wrist and protected ankle did not become less injured because the view had improved. He rested between the permitted assisted attempts, and the next plan followed what he reported rather than a tally of how many days he had been good.

Veer had his own review at the post. When the knee position needed changing, he asked for help before trying to take weight through the shoulder to do it himself. The answer concerned what could be adjusted now, not whether a friend in another bed had endured something worse.

The people doing the work changed shifts. The patients did not have to keep one particular helper awake to keep care available.

There was no important family call that morning.

The world continued through an interval in which nobody explained a bloodline.

---

In Mirzapur, the girls' team allowed a short, assisted visit to the covered veranda beside the ward after their individual checks.

Sitara did not make one girl's permission stand for the other's. The supports and eye protection stayed where they were needed. A helper moved the chairs into the shade; neither girl was asked to carry something to prove that getting outside had been a sensible idea.

Aadhya sat where she could see the edge of a planted bed.

One leaf had been caught under another. She noticed it immediately, felt the old desire to put the small thing right and looked instead at the hand resting on its support. The leaf was not dying because she had failed to intervene. Air moved through the planted bed, and a moment later it slipped free by itself.

Ira heard her laugh.

"Kya?"

"Kuchh nahi."

"Tumhaara bhi bolta hai."

Aadhya turned towards her. The joke from the earlier paragraph argument came back without either girl needing to explain it.

---

Ira did not try to take in the whole veranda with the ordinary eye.

She looked for a little while at what was comfortable, then let the lids close. Beyond the covered space somebody moved a bucket. There was a conversation about a missing key, a call from farther inside the building and the faint noise of traffic which had been there all the days they had spent indoors.

The outside world sounded less like an event than she expected.

"Waapas chalna hai?" Aadhya asked after a while.

"Haan."

There was still room in the permitted interval. Ira did not spend it merely because it had been offered.

Aadhya waited while the helper made the return ready, then said she would go back too. Not because one girl's fatigue proved that the other had been weak, and not because staying longer would have meant abandoning her. She was tired herself. She could use the answer which belonged to her own body.

Sitara heard them both.

The visit ended without a test, a new sight or a sudden cure. The little planted bed remained outside, needing no power from a girl who was allowed to rest.

---

On the morning of day forty-eight, the team allowed a short, staff-assisted wheelchair movement along the ward after the review.

Not out of the hospital. Not an attempt to stand between the chair and bed. The transfer was helped, the injured limbs supported, the necessary equipment kept with him. A member of staff moved the chair; Vedant did not lend the injured forearm to the work.

Rudra passed the old bay.

Someone else lay in the place from which he had spent nights listening to the duty station. He saw an ordinary bag under a chair, a relative making room for the nurse and the brief lift of a face at the sound of wheels. The patient looked towards him. Rudra nodded, and the face settled again.

Nobody had kept the space empty as a monument to the night his body broke its concealment.

He was glad, suddenly and without any useful lesson attached to it. Somebody else needed the space. The people working there had given it to them.

---

Near the window, he could see a small part of Prayagraj carrying on without reference to the bed he had just left.

Traffic slowed where a vehicle was being unloaded. A person carrying food waited, annoyed, for room to pass. Somebody else moved a loose crate instead of debating who ought to have put it there. The person with the food went on, and the quarrel began anyway, now no longer blocking the same small piece of road.

Rudra watched until he began to feel tired.

There were people in this city who would never know the story of Pataal. They still needed roads and work and an afternoon in which their own business could matter. Knowing about a massacre had not made their ordinary lives an evasion of it.

He asked to go back.

The staff member checked him and took the chair back at an unhurried pace. Nothing had to go wrong before he was allowed to decide the short interval was enough.

---

Back in bed, he answered the questions about the movement and rested.

The call to his father came later, when both teams confirmed they could speak. Ram had finished care; his voice was clearer than it had been in the first disclosures, but the phone still needed its support and the nurse remained nearby. His mother was taking her own rest after the day's work of being a patient too.

Rudra told him about passing the old bay.

"Koi aur hai ab wahaan."

"Haan."

"Ajeeb laga. Phir achha laga."

Ram waited rather than choosing the meaning for him.

"Mere liye khaali rakhte toh kya hota," Rudra said. "Kisi aur ko zaroorat hai."

It was not a statement about how little his own need had mattered. The bed had held him when he needed it. He could leave one place by a few yards without requiring it to stop being useful to anybody else.

---

"Tumhaari movement kaisi rahi?" Ram asked.

Rudra gave the actual answer, including the tiredness and the help. He did not let the chair acquire a larger journey in the retelling.

His father heard it without making a new rule from a distance.

They spoke a little about the household story. The warmth which had eased a wound without making the hand new. The woman asking before continuing. The cloth given to a child who had wanted something more impressive to do.

"Aapko lagta tha kaam chhota de rahe hain," Rudra said.

"Tab laga."

"Ab?"

"Jis aadmi ko phisalne se bachaaya, uske liye chhota nahi tha."

Rudra looked at his own open fingers.

He could hear that answer without believing he had been given healing by hearing it. His father's learned skill belonged to the life which formed it. The thing he could use was harder and more ordinary: another person's need was not made smaller because it failed to resemble the work he had hoped to do.

---

He thought of his friends.

Veer at the post with the knee and shoulder which still needed care. The girls in Mirzapur, hearing the history in short permitted calls, leaving paper unfinished when their hands and eyes had reached the day's limit. They had not been waiting in a storyless interval until the son of Daavansh found out where he wanted to go.

His mother had told him to ask.

Ira had told him not to write the answer for them.

It sounded simple until he imagined hearing no.

Veer might want to go and still need to stay. Aadhya might have a task of her own which did not fit around the request. Ira might want time before answering. None of those possibilities required a failure of courage. He could know that in advance and still discover, when the answer came, how much of his kindness had been depending on being agreed with.

He could picture himself agreeing at once, saying of course, then letting disappointment thicken the next ordinary conversation until a friend had to find something brave enough to offer him instead.

An order did not always have to be spoken as one.

He knew that too well to pretend a polite question would be enough by itself.

---

The nurse at his end checked whether he was tiring. He said a little, listened and kept the next words short. He would not use another person's willingness to hear him as permission to spend all the strength either of them had.

He had wanted to know his father's birthplace without making the journey a debt to the dead.

Now he wanted to ask a living person to share it.

The two wishes could become the same old demand if he made love responsible for producing the answer he wanted. He could imagine the friends going because they wanted to know, and the friends staying because their bodies or their own lives needed something else. He did not want the second picture to turn them into lesser people in his mind.

He looked at the phone.

"Papa," he said. "Main kisi ko saath kaise bulaaoon, agar uska na kehna bhi sach mein theek rehna hai?"

---

# CHAPTER EIGHTY-NINE — THE RIGHT TO STAY

---

> *"You were allowed to come.*
> *You were allowed to stay.*
> *The second sentence cost us more to mean."*
> *— Ira's later register, on the first invitations*

---

"Uske na kehne ke baad bhi use wahi jagah dena jo pehle thi," Ram said. "Sirf poochhte waqt nahi."

Rudra looked at the phone.

"Bura lage toh?"

"Lag sakta hai. Bura lagne ko uska kiya hua galat mat bana dena."

The father took the next breath before continuing. The nurse stayed within reach, the phone on its support.

"Maine tumhare saath bahut baar nahi kiya. Tumse wahi dohraane ko nahi kahoonga. Kisi ka jawaab sunna hai toh uske baad ki baat bhi uske liye bachaa ke rakhni padegi."

Rudra had wanted a sentence he could use correctly, a way to ask which would keep the answer from making either person smaller. His father was describing the less comfortable part: what he would do tomorrow if the sentence failed to obtain what he wanted today.

He would have to remain a friend after the asking.

---

"Aur jisko abhi pata hi na ho?"

"Usse abhi pata hone ka kaam mat do."

Rudra let the answer settle.

He did not know the route. There were no travel arrangements, no clinical clearance to leave, no honest account of the conditions in which a companion would have to make each later choice. An invitation to want to see a place could not become advance consent to every unknown between this bed and that place.

"Phir poori yaatra ki haan kaise maangoon?"

"Abhi jitna jaante ho, utna bolo. Jo nahi jaante, woh bhi. Baaki jab pata chale, tab phir poochhna."

The words were not a secret of how to make people willing. They left room for willingness to change when the facts did.

Rudra breathed out.

He could not make the ask painless by making it perfect. He could stop calling the other person's answer a mistake merely because it hurt.

---

"Mujhe Pataal dekhna hai," he said. "Par sabko leke hi dekhna hai, aisa nahi bolna chahta."

"Haan."

"Aapko bura nahi lagega agar koi yahin rehna chahe?"

Ram looked towards the ordinary things beside his bed: water, a folded cloth, the place where the phone would be put when the call ended.

"Mujhe yahaan logon ne zinda rakha," he said. "Yahaan rehne ko main chhota kaise boloon?"

He did not need to make Earth a refuge which counted only until a greater world became available. The people who raised him had not kept the infant alive so that every person after him could be commanded to leave their own work unfinished in proof of love.

"Jo saath aaye, uska apna bhi faisla hona chahiye," Ram added. "Tumhaara dukh uski poori zindagi nahi."

Rudra heard the sentence and let it hurt without arguing.

---

They ended the call soon afterwards.

The question had been answered. No father had decided the son's whole future, and no son had been given a promise that the friends he asked would find the unknown road easier because his reasons for wanting it were real.

He had the nurse move the stand away, answered the next check and rested.

In Varanasi, Ram let his own care interrupt the thoughts which followed. He had said something to the boy which he himself had not always known how to practise. Speaking it clearly did not make the past disappear, and he did not ask the next quiet interval to provide him with the relief of pretending it had.

Rukmini remained resting nearby. The people on duty knew the family arrangement. The evening went on without requiring her to return and approve the answer on anybody else's behalf.

---

Night passed between days forty-eight and forty-nine in the three separate cities.

There were checks, short awakenings, stretches of sleep and the ordinary handovers by which another person took responsibility for a while. No message went out recruiting anyone while the patients who might receive it were supposed to rest.

In the morning, Rudra's review kept the same protection on the injured limbs. The calmer breathing allowed the existing ward plan to continue, not a sudden discharge or an unsupervised attempt to stand. The short assisted movement of the previous day was useful information; it was not permission to repeat it however he pleased.

After breakfast and rest he asked whether there would be someone free to read a little of the college page later.

The answer, for the moment, was no. The helper had another task to finish first.

Rudra felt the small disappointment arrive, absurdly sharp after all the larger things he had been saying he could bear.

He did not turn it into an emergency.

---

For a while Rudra lay looking at the bag.

The page was in it. Nobody had refused to give it back, and nobody was making a point about how little his work mattered. Another patient needed the helper first. He understood that easily until the part of him which had been waiting to hear a few ordinary lines began treating the wait as a judgement.

He recognized the feeling without liking it.

When the nurse came for the check, he answered about the body rather than trying to make the college page one more reason she needed to remain. The wrist ached. The new position was easier. He did not hide one answer to be agreeable or enlarge the other to obtain an audience.

She checked what she needed to check.

The page waited.

---

Later the helper returned and asked whether he still wanted a little read aloud.

"Haan. Agar abhi waqt hai."

There was, for a short while.

Rudra listened to the heading and the first few lines again. They were the same lines. His attention was better today, then poorer; he lost the sense halfway through a sentence and asked for it once more without accusing the person reading of going too quickly.

After a little he said that was enough.

The page went back in the bag. The helper returned to the other work.

Nothing in the exchange would have looked like courage to somebody passing the bed. He had wanted a thing, been unable to have it immediately and still been allowed to want it when the time became possible. He could see how much of his old preparation had taught him to hear delay as a sign that he had asked incorrectly.

He did not have to pass that hearing on to a friend.

---

At the Prayagraj post, Veer learned that one of the volunteers would not be there the following day.

There was work at home. The man had arranged the handover and was telling him who would be nearby, not asking to be released from a debt. The care team knew. Nothing clinically necessary would depend on Veer persuading this one person to remain.

"Theek," Veer said.

Then he heard how short it sounded.

"Ghar ka kaam karo," he added. "Yahaan bataa diya hai."

The volunteer nodded and finished setting the water where it could be reached without the shoulder doing work it was not supposed to do.

Veer watched him.

He had got used to a particular way of asking whether the knee needed adjusting, to someone remembering the position before the next request. That familiarity mattered. It did not mean the man owed him the next morning too.

"Shukriya," he said before the volunteer went.

He meant the days already given, not an attempt to buy another one.

---

The next person asked where the support was most comfortable.

Veer gave the answer instead of saying that the other volunteer already knew. The nurse checked the shoulder when he said it was aching again and helped with the permitted position. His rib and knee limits had not left with the person whose questions he liked best.

Later he rested.

He knew about Ram's birth, the massacre, the authority responsible and the limited account of the fight before Rudra's birth which his friend had actually told him. He did not know that his friend had since asked to see Pataal or heard it described as ruins. Nobody at the post was reading the private family calls out to him between checks.

The next conversation would have to bring its own information.

For now there was a quieter interval, a supported shoulder and the sound of somebody new learning the ordinary work of the bed.

---

In Varanasi, Rukmini asked for the later call to wait until after her care and rest.

The receiving contact told Rudra. She was not unwell in some newly hidden way; she was following the plan and had chosen not to squeeze another conversation into the time before it.

He asked for a brief message to be passed back: he understood, and she could speak when ready. He did not ask her to use the interval proving she had meant the things she said about wanting him to have a life.

The answer was a small thing he could do before any larger choice arrived.

When she was rested, she called at an agreed time and asked how the new bed was.

He told her about the view and the college page. She told him the shoulder still needed its support, that she had eaten and that Dadi had remembered where everything in the little bag belonged better than either of the people using it.

They ended with an ordinary goodbye.

No one had been punished for being unavailable earlier.

---

On day fifty, after the review and rest, Rudra asked the receiving contact to offer the girls a short call about Pataal.

Not an urgent call. Not news which Sitara had to put into two tired bodies before they had agreed to hear it. The answer came back later: both wanted to speak, and their team had allowed the interval after their own care.

He waited until the phone was steady on the stand.

At the other end, the helper placed it where neither girl had to hold it. Sitara remained near enough to help without making herself the person who would answer.

"Poochhna hai," Rudra said.

"Poochho," Aadhya answered.

He looked at the bag, at the ordinary page which had been unavailable when he wanted it and still there when help became free.

Then he asked.

"Jab sach mein jaana ho sake, tum dono mere saath Pataal dekhna chaahogi?"

---

The quiet which followed was not refusal yet.

He tried not to fill it with more reasons.

"Mujhe dekhna hai," Aadhya said at last. "Kya hua tha. Sirf naam sunke apni tasveer banaane se aage. Par main abhi safar ke liye haan bol doon, utna nahi pata."

"Kya?"

"Kaise jaayenge. Care kaise chalegi. Kya karne jaa rahe hain, aur kya nahi. Mera haath kal kya karega, woh bhi main aaj decide nahi kar sakti."

She was not asking him to produce a perfect world before anybody could leave. She was asking him not to make her interest carry facts he had not yet given her, some of which he did not yet have himself.

He heard the first part more loudly than the rest: she wanted to see it.

Then he made himself hear the conditions too.

She asked what would happen if someone needed to turn back, or stop before the others did. He had not thought beyond getting everyone there together. The way back was suddenly part of the journey too, a need he had left outside the picture because seeing the ruined place had seemed like the only thing with an ending. He could not answer by promising to be strong enough for everybody.

"Abhi plan nahi hai," he said. "Main bataaoonga jab pata hoga."

"Tab main apni baat phir boloongi."

"Haan."

---

Ira spoke after the pause.

"Meri abhi haan mat maan lena."

Rudra's good fingers tightened against the blanket.

Only for a moment. He let them ease before the wrist had to object.

"Theek," he said.

The word sounded thin to his own ears. He waited rather than making her repair its sound for him.

"Mujhe tumhari baat sunni hai," she continued. "Jagah ke baare mein bhi. Par jo abhi nahi pata, uske liye waada nahi kar sakti. Aur mera dekhna abhi kitna saath dega, uska jawaab bhi care ke baad hi hoga."

"Tumhe samjhaane ki zaroorat nahi—"

He stopped. She had chosen to tell him. Refusing the explanation could be another way of deciding what her answer ought to contain.

"Sun raha hoon," he said instead.

She finished in her own time. The left eye was still covered. The right could do only what it could, and wanting to know was not the same as being able to use it through a difficult journey.

He had not needed a reminder of the injury to respect the answer.

He listened to the reminder because it was hers to give.

---

"Dono ka abhi ka jawaab yahi hai," Aadhya said. "Jaanne mein dilchaspi hai. Safar ka waada nahi."

Rudra looked towards the quieter ward's window.

The wanting-to-know mattered. It did not entitle him to turn the answer into yes before anybody had used the word.

"Theek," he said again, and this time it had more of his voice in it. "Main tumhaari jagah khud nahi bhar loonga."

He could feel disappointment looking for something useful to do. Offer to take care of every need. Promise a route. Say he would make himself strong enough that neither girl would have to worry. Each promise would have moved an unknown onto a person who was supposed to trust him.

He left the promises unmade.

"Abhi jo poochha, woh kisi ko bhejne ki zaroorat nahi," he added. "Veer se bhi main khud baat karoonga."

"Haan," Ira said.

No one asked her to sign the refusal in a notebook or convert it into proof that she cared.

---

Before they ended, Aadhya asked about the college page.

It took Rudra a second to answer. The ordinary question had reached him while he was still braced for the next careful sentence about Pataal.

"Thoda suna. Phir bas."

"Matlab ab bhi hamaare paragraph pe hansne ka haq nahi mila."

He smiled despite himself.

"Woh haq toh bina padhe bhi hai."

"Bahut achha. Phir mat padhna."

The helper moved the phone a little when Ira asked; she had reached the end of the comfortable interval. Rudra heard the adjustment, asked no extra question and let the call finish.

Aadhya's hand still needed care. Ira's answer still had not become a promise. The joke had not been offered as a payment to make that easier for him to bear.

He lay back afterwards, relieved and disappointed at once.

Neither feeling required him to send another message correcting what the girls had said.

---

Day fifty-one began after another night of checks and sleep.

Rudra's review continued the ward plan. The quieter breathing had held; the injured wrist and ankle still needed protection, assistance and their own follow-up. He was not given a departure date, and he did not ask the therapist to make the next movement a demonstration for people who had not promised to travel with him.

After rest, he asked the contact to offer the girls an ordinary message when it would not interrupt care.

He recorded that the college page had still failed to make him intelligent, then listened to the line and asked for it to be sent. There was no question about whether either girl had reconsidered. No photograph of a lonely bed, no suggestion that disappointment had made his condition worse.

The receiving contact passed it to Sitara, who checked whether they wanted it played.

They did.

Aadhya sent back that the page had made no such promise. Ira asked for that sentence to be kept exactly as Aadhya said it instead of improved into a kinder one.

When Rudra heard the reply after his next rest, he laughed softly and let the conversation end there.

---

It mattered that the next words had been about the page.

The invitation remained unanswered in the larger sense. The girls had interest, conditions and no promise for the journey as yet. They had not become less close because those were the answers they could give.

Rudra could feel the relief in himself without pretending it made him generous. Keeping a friendship ordinary after asking something difficult was not a favour performed for the people who had failed to give the desired answer.

He would need to remember that if a later answer was simply no.

For now the day contained the small work he could actually do: answer the nurse, accept the help, rest before asking for another person to spend time with him. The phone stayed supported when used and out of the way when it was not.

He did not rehearse arguments for changing the girls' conditions.

---

In the permitted family call later, he told Ram he had asked them.

"Abhi kisi ne safar ka waada nahi kiya," he said.

His father let the sentence remain complete.

"Theek."

"Unke sawaal hain. Kuchh mere paas jawaab nahi."

"Jitna nahi pata, uski haan mat banaao."

Rudra looked at the bag. He could have passed the girls' exact words to his father, asked him to interpret whether they meant to come or wanted a reason not to. He could have made an elder's opinion into the answer he had been told not to write himself.

He gave no transcript.

"Baaki unse baat karoonga," he said.

"Haan."

The boundary did not need an argument to become real.

---

"Saath na aana, chhod dena nahi hota," Ram said after a while.

Rudra was quiet.

His father did not make that a rule excusing every person who had ever walked away. He was speaking about people asked to share a journey which still had unknown conditions, about a request whose answer did not become a moral offence because a boy had a terrible reason to want company.

"Mere maa-baap se alag hona maine nahi chuna," Ram said. "Uska matlab har alag rehna wahi kho jaana nahi. Yahaan jinhone paala, unka saath bhi sach tha. Tumhare dost yahaan rahen toh unki zindagi tumse kam nahi ho jaayegi."

The son looked towards the window.

He had heard the infant's survival first as a distance which would always need crossing. Now he could hear another part of the life which followed: people staying in one place long enough to raise a child, mend a wound, make the next meal possible.

A ruined origin did not make the places still living into waiting rooms.

He did not have to choose between knowing the loss and respecting what another person wanted to keep.

---

They ended while both still had strength to say goodbye properly.

In Varanasi, Rukmini was resting after her own care, with Dadi nearby and the team available. Ram did not ask for her to return so she could hear that he had answered well. The answer was not a performance owed to a household witness.

Dhruv came for the period he had agreed to, heard the clinical update from the right people and made time for his own rest when the handover was complete. None of the adults had been assigned a passage out of Earth by a son asking what he might want to see.

In Mirzapur, the girls' next care interval concerned their own symptoms and what each could manage. There was no suddenly recovered eye or hand to make a conditional answer unnecessary.

The three cities remained three cities through the following night.

---

On day fifty-two, Rudra rested after the review and the permitted assisted movement before asking about a call to Veer.

The post checked first. His friend wanted to hear from him; they would arrange a short interval after care, with the phone supported. No helper would have to abandon another patient because a conversation about the future had been described as urgent.

Rudra waited.

He knew what he wanted to ask now. He also knew how little of the journey had been decided, and how much of friendship would remain after the answer.

When the nurse placed the phone on the stand, he asked that it be moved a little closer, not into the injured hand.

The line connected.

---

"Haan, bol," Veer said.

Rudra listened to the familiar voice for a moment before beginning. The phone at the post had been settled on its support; a small movement and the answer to a nurse's question told him his friend was ready, not pretending the care around the bed had ceased to exist.

"Pehle ek baat bataani hai. Papa se Pataal dekhne ko kaha hai. Jab jaana ho sake."

Veer was quiet.

"Unhone kya kaha?"

"Taiyaari mein madad karenge. Abhi le chalne ka waada nahi. Khud jaa paayenge, woh bhi nahi bola."

He gave the limits with the first answer, before excitement could make the space between wanting and travelling disappear.

"Aur?"

"Bol rahe hain, ujaad hai. Khandhar. Unka bachpan jo yaad hai, yahaan ka hai. Wahaan jaake puraana ghar pehchaanne jaisa nahi."

This was new information for Veer. Rudra let him have time for it.

---

"Tu dekhna chahta hai," his friend said.

"Haan."

"Kya dhoondhne?"

Rudra looked at the branch in the upper corner of the window. In the days since he first saw it, he had stopped expecting every movement outside the glass to carry an answer about the world his father came from.

"Abhi jitna pata hai, usse aage sach mein jo hai," he said. "Kisi ko pehchaanne ka daava nahi. Koi cheez laake sab theek karne ka bhi nahi. Bas log the. Jo unke saath hua, usse nazar hataake nahi rakhna chahta."

He heard how easily the last part could become an accusation against somebody choosing not to go. He corrected the reach before making Veer do it for him.

"Main apni baat keh raha hoon. Dekhne ke liye jaana zaroori hai, aisa sabke liye nahi bol raha."

"Samajh raha hoon," Veer said.

The answer did not contain a promise of company. It did not need one before the friend could understand the wish.

---

Rudra told him a little of the other story too.

Ram learning beside the people who raised him, wanting to mend before knowing how to hear the person being helped. The spilled bowl. The cloth for the floor. The lesson about stopping, and the son's question about why a man who had learned it had not stopped for him.

He told Veer what Ram had actually answered: fear made larger than pain, not a claim that every morning had been necessary. It was the account a son could give of what he had been told, with spaces where he still had questions. The child at the bench did not become proof that the father had never hurt anyone; the father in the courtyard did not make the child's learning false.

Veer listened without deciding whether a better answer meant Rudra was required to stop being angry.

"Ab baat kar paa rahe ho," he said.

"Thodi. Har baar aasaan nahi."

"Haan."

---

There was a pause while the nurse at Rudra's end asked about fatigue.

He answered honestly and waited through the check before returning to the phone. At the post, Veer let his own helper make the small adjustment he needed instead of holding the shoulder tight until the conversation ended.

The two intervals belonged to the call. Neither friend had left the other by needing help.

"Abhi yahaan se nikalne ka kuchh tay nahi," Rudra said. "Team ka plan chal raha hai. Raasta mujhe pakka nahi pata. Jo aage pata chalega, tab phir baat karni padegi."

He did not sell uncertainty as something the two of them could settle by being brave enough together. They had survived too much actual damage to mistake a shared willingness for the means of keeping everybody alive.

Veer asked whether the girls knew about the wish.

"Haan. Poochha bhi. Abhi safar ka waada kisi ne nahi kiya. Apne sawaal hain. Jawaab milenge toh phir poochhoonga."

He did not repeat Ira's exact words or make Aadhya's interest sound like an agreement already secured.

He was not offering his friend an empty place which had to be filled to rescue the meaning of another person's answer.

---

Rudra could remember Veer beside him in all the ordinary places which had once seemed large enough to contain their future.

The college table. The ghat steps. An argument continued for so long that neither remembered which one had begun it. There had been more difficult places since then, bodies falling where friendship could not make the ground soft enough, and help given before there was time to decide what being grateful would cost.

He did not own the next act of courage because the earlier ones had been real.

The friend at the post had a life which was not an extension of the boy in this bed. It could remain joined to his without taking every road he wanted to take.

He felt the sadness of that possibility, and the relief beneath it. If company came, he wanted it to arrive from a person, not from the part of love which had been frightened into agreement.

---

The phone remained on its stand.

His wrist stayed inside the brace. The ankle had not moved from its support. There was no returned hiding edge at the collar, no promise that hearing the old story had made the next place safe or taught either boy a new way to survive it.

He had asked his father for help seeing Pataal. He had heard his mother's fear without making it a prohibition, heard his friends' conditions without calling them a failure.

Now there was only the asking he had spent days learning not to disguise as a duty.

He kept his voice ordinary.

"Veer," he said. "Jab jaana sach mein mumkin ho, mere saath Pataal dekhna chaahega?"

---

# CHAPTER NINETY — BESIDE HIM, NOT AHEAD

---

> *"In the later telling, he agreed to stand between his friend and a world.*
> *What he actually asked for was a place beside him."*
> *— Ira's later register, correction beside the account of Veer's answer*

---

"Haan," Veer said. "Jaana chahta hoon. Par abhi nikalne ki haan mat sun lena."

Rudra heard the answer before he knew what to do with the relief.

At the post, the phone had been placed close enough for Veer to speak without lifting the bad shoulder. There was a nurse nearby, a supported knee beneath the sheet, a body which could not be volunteered into readiness by the voice coming out of it.

"Woh nahi sun raha," Rudra said.

"Achha. Kyunki abhi yahaan se paani lene jaaoon toh bhi aadhe log poochhenge kahaan."

Rudra smiled. The breath caught at the end of it, not painfully enough to hide or enlarge; he answered the nurse's glance and let it settle.

"Apne liye poochh raha hoon," he said. "Tumse pehle girne ka waada nahi."

For a moment Veer said nothing.

Then, "Pehle girna meri aadat thi. Kaam nahi."

The sentence had come out more quietly than either expected. Rudra did not help it become a joke.

"Pata hai," he said.

"Ab pata rahe."

---

Veer looked at the water cup which someone else had put within reach.

He had heard the word ruins only a little earlier. Until this call, Pataal had belonged to the account of a baby sent out of a massacre, a place whose terrible distance had made the known river and the ordinary roof over the post feel briefly insufficient. Now it also had a future tense: something a friend wanted to see.

He wanted to see it too.

The wanting was not simple enough to call bravery. He was frightened of what there might be, of how little they understood about getting there and returning, of finding that the part of himself which always moved first would move before the rest had been allowed to think. He was also curious. He wanted the place to become more than a word he stood guard around because it hurt Rudra.

"Mujhe bhi dekhna hai," he said. "Tumhaare papa ka janm jahaan hua. Jo bacha hai. Sirf tumhe akela na lage isliye nahi."

Rudra listened to that last part.

He had been preparing to accept a refusal. He had not prepared as carefully to hear a yes which belonged to another person's desire rather than the generosity of agreeing with his.

"Aur darr?" he asked.

"Woh bhi mera hai. Saath laaoonga. Chhupaa ke nahi."

Veer shifted his good hand, then left it resting. The helper asked whether the shoulder needed changing. It did. The friends waited while the pillow was moved and the position checked.

When Veer spoke again, the voice was less strained.

"Jab raasta, care, waapas aana—yeh sab samajh aayega, tab apna faisla phir boloonga. Abhi itna keh sakta hoon."

"Itna hi poochha tha," Rudra said.

He meant it now more completely than when he had asked.

---

There had been a time when the two of them could make a whole afternoon out of deciding which side of a college bench belonged to whom.

Veer remembered sitting first, spreading his books wider than he needed and refusing to recognize the hand which Rudra had put down to claim the remaining space. The quarrel had ended with both of them pushed towards the same edge while someone else used the room they were arguing over.

Neither had learned much from the incident. It had become a story they told differently whenever being right was easier than admitting they had both been idiots.

"Waise jagah rakh lena," Veer said.

"Kahaan?"

"Dimaag mein. Saath jaana chahta hoon bola hai. Har cheez ka jawaab mil gaya, yeh nahi."

"Bench pe bhi aise hi jagah lete the."

"Bench pe tumne kitaab rakhi thi. Khud nahi aaye the."

"Main paani lene gaya tha."

"Abhi bhi wahi bahaana."

Rudra laughed softly. This time the nurse asked him to stop talking for a moment, and he did. Veer could hear the short check, the answer that nothing had changed into a new pain, the instruction to rest after the call.

He waited without filling the interval.

The old argument had survived the larger conversation. That mattered more than either boy knew how to say. Friendship had not become a service which began only when somebody was in danger.

"Baad mein baat karenge," Rudra said when he could.

"Haan. Aur kisi ko bolna toh abhi waali baat bolna."

"Dekhna chaahte ho. Safar abhi tay nahi."

"Bas."

The helper at the post checked the time. Veer gave the ordinary goodbye first, then stopped his friend for one more thing.

"Papa se jo poochhna hai, poochh lena. Mere haan kehne se woh baat khatam nahi hoti."

"Kaunsi?"

"Jo abhi bhi unse poochhni hai. Tumhaari."

Rudra looked towards the edge of the phone stand.

Veer did not name a question for him. He had no access to all the spaces in the family calls, and no right to fill them because he had offered company on a road. He was only reminding the boy that a friend could be willing while a son's anger remained unfinished.

"Haan," Rudra said.

They ended the call there.

The rest of day fifty-two did not turn into preparations to leave.

The phone was put away. Rudra answered the later checks, ate what he could and slept before the next change of staff. When he woke in the night, the wish to tell someone Veer's answer arrived ahead of the patience required to wait until morning. He looked at the dark phone and let it stay dark.

There was no emergency in being glad.

By morning the feeling had become less like something he might lose unless he repeated it and more like company he could keep quietly. Someone else wanted to see the place. He did not have to begin carrying that person there from this bed.

The clinician heard about the previous afternoon's tiredness. The breathing had settled again with rest; the quieter periods were continuing through the actual observations, not merely through Rudra's recollection of them. His wrist and ankle were checked separately. He answered about the ache at night, about sleep, about what became uncomfortable when he had been sitting.

The day's permission was a small extension of the ward work already under way: after rest, a short part of a meal in the supported chair, with the staff doing the transfer and staying to see how he tolerated it. The equipment and limb supports would come with him. He was not to push through the wrist or put weight through the injured ankle. Getting into the chair himself was not included.

Rudra asked whether he could use the good hand for the spoon if the table was placed correctly.

They checked the arrangement before answering.

Yes, within that arrangement. If he tired, they would help.

He held on to the yes at its proper size.

---

The meal smelled better away from the pillow.

That was the first ridiculous thing he noticed once the transfer was finished, the ankle supported, the brace clear of the table edge and the nurse satisfied with the position. It was the same food. Nothing had been added to it while he was being helped across. Yet the steam had somewhere to go other than towards his face, and the bowl stood in front of him as though lunch were a thing which could happen at a table again.

He took the first small mouthful slowly.

The spoon shook a little in the uninjured hand. He had expected to feel insulted by that. Instead he was annoyed because some of the food slipped back before reaching his mouth.

"Thoda paas?" the helper asked.

He looked at the bowl rather than at the people watching.

"Haan. Bas itna."

They adjusted the table. He tried again.

For several mouthfuls it worked. There was a kind of pleasure in doing something which had an end as modest as eating it. No audience would remember the result. The next spoonful was not a test of whether he deserved the one before.

Then the effort began to spread, not a new sharp pain but the familiar loss of ease which made his shoulders want to tighten around everything else. He put the spoon down and said he was tired.

The nurse checked him. They ended the chair interval within the plan, helped him back with the same care and finished the meal where he could rest between mouthfuls.

He did not ask how much longer a stronger person would have lasted.

Later, when the ache had been checked and the body settled, he found himself thinking of Veer's water cup. The friend who wanted to come with him had let somebody move a pillow. Neither act had cancelled the wanting.

---

At the post, day fifty-three brought a review of Veer's own shoulder, ribs and knee before the usual supervised movement.

The new volunteer asked him to describe the comfortable position again. Veer did, less impatiently this time. The man listened, got one part wrong and waited while Veer corrected it. The nurse checked the support; the two ordinary attempts became the arrangement which worked.

During the permitted knee movement later, Veer wanted to compare the effort with yesterday. The therapist asked first where it hurt today.

He answered that question.

They kept the movement within what had been allowed, stopped when asked and let rest follow. There was no invisible preparation for a dangerous place hidden inside the repetitions. If a later journey became possible, the body going on it would need these people to know the truth now.

He thought about Rudra's invitation while the helper cleared the cup.

There would be days when wanting company would not be enough to make the knee follow. He had told his friend he wanted to go. He would have to keep telling the truth if the answer became harder to hold.

For now there was no ticket, no bag to pack, no reason to make the afternoon into a farewell.

He asked whether somebody could move the window curtain a little. The light had reached the pillow.

At the ward, Rudra had fallen asleep after the meal and the check. At the post, Veer lay in the easier shade and listened to a bicycle bell beyond the wall.

Both boys remained in Prayagraj. There was a distance between their beds which affection had not been required to pretend was gone.

Rudra told his mother about the meal in the afternoon.

The call had been offered after she finished her own care. He had slept; she had rested the shoulder. Dadi was near her, within the ordinary little arrangement of a family which could not be everywhere it wanted and still needed to know whether somebody had eaten.

"Table pe?" Rukmini asked.

"Haan. Thoda. Phir bed pe."

"Kya tha khaane mein?"

He began explaining the chair, the way the table had been moved, how the good hand shook towards the end.

"Woh suna," she said. "Khaane mein kya tha?"

He stopped, then told her.

She asked whether he had liked it. It took him a moment to understand that there was no other question beneath this one, no request to make the tired hand a lesson about patience or the half-finished meal an encouraging report which she could send to his father.

"Pehle achha laga," he said. "Phir khaane se zyaada spoon pe dhyaan tha."

"Hota hai."

Dadi asked to be heard for a moment. The helper adjusted the phone where Rukmini did not have to lean or hold it out.

"Achha lagaa toh khaane ko bhi bataa dena," she said. "Warna bechaara soch raha hoga saara kaam kursi ne kiya."

Rudra grinned.

"Kursi ki tareef nahi ki."

"Tabhi. Tum log kisi ki nahi karte. Khaana bhi aaya, kursi bhi aayi. Charcha spoon ki."

Rukmini's laugh was brief. She stopped before the shoulder pulled and accepted the small adjustment the helper offered. The nurse knew about it; the call waited while she was comfortable again.

When the phone settled, Rudra said, "Dadi, aapki bhi tareef kar denge."

"Meri chhodo. Main sunke kaam nahi karti."

The line brought them a few seconds of ordinary pleasure, awkward and unfinished in the way real laughter was when several people had injuries. Rudra did not try to hold it open after the allotted interval began to close.

Before they ended he told his mother the part of Veer's answer he had been allowed to tell.

"Woh bhi dekhna chahta hai. Abhi safar ki haan nahi. Jab samajh aayega, phir baat hogi."

"Achha," she said.

No list of four travellers appeared in her answer. She was glad he had been able to ask a friend. That was what she said.

---

In Varanasi, Ram had his face washed with help that afternoon.

It was undramatic work. The basin came, the towel was placed where it would not catch at the injured side, and he was asked whether the water was comfortable. He answered. The cloth passed over the stubble, behind the ear, along places which had once seemed beneath the notice of a man whose body could do so much more than lie in a bed and need washing.

When he tried to turn too far, the nurse stopped him and helped him find the easier angle.

He let her.

Afterwards, clean-faced and tired by an effort he had mostly received, he looked towards the chair beside the bed. Rukmini would be resting now. Dhruv's agreed visit was later, after the handover. There was no one whose presence required him to turn what he was thinking into advice.

He had heard through the consented care update that Rudra had managed part of a meal in the chair, then needed help and rest. He knew no more of the afternoon call than anyone had chosen to tell him. He did not know about Dadi defending the reputation of the food.

He could imagine his son trying to get the spoon to behave.

That image hurt in a way the clinical facts did not. He had spent years making a child's body repeat things until the motion became reliable, and now another adult had moved a table closer instead of asking the body to earn the distance.

He could learn from that without declaring every lesson he had given useless.

The useful parts were precisely why the rest needed looking at. It was too easy to hide a needless hurt inside an account of the hurt which had helped somebody survive.

He waited for the next check. When the nurse returned, he said the position was beginning to ache. She helped him change it before the ache became the only thing he could think about.

---

Rudra spent the later afternoon awake for a while, then asleep.

He had asked that a brief, accurate message about Veer's interest be offered to his father when convenient. Nothing urgent; no request for a travel decision. The receiving contact confirmed that Ram had heard it. No larger conversation was fitted between either man's care and rest merely because the answer felt important.

As night came, Rudra watched the window darken.

The afternoon had contained friends and food, his mother's ordinary questions and his grandmother's refusal to make a spoon the most interesting member of the family. He could keep all of it while wanting something more difficult from his father.

Company was not the answer to a question he had not yet asked.

The night between days fifty-three and fifty-four passed with the usual interruptions.

Rudra woke to one check from a dream about a classroom in which he could not find the right seat. He remembered nothing important from it by morning except the annoyance of everyone else already knowing where to put their bag. No voice supplied a forgotten country. He told the clinician how he had slept and let the small absurdity remain a dream.

The review used the previous day's actual chair interval: the tiredness, the recovery after it, the fact that he had not developed a new breathing problem while eating. The same protections remained on the wrist and ankle. Another short supported interval could be tried later if he was rested; yesterday's permission was not a licence for every repetition he wanted today.

He listened without searching the instructions for a hidden invitation to prove himself.

After breakfast, the nurse helped him settle the blanket clear of the supported foot. The new position lasted a little before he needed it changed. He asked, they adjusted it, and the morning continued without turning the request into a judgement about whether he was improving.

---

In Mirzapur, the girls finished the paragraph.

Not the whole piece of college work, not a triumphant return to examinations or a proof that either injured body had become ready for journeys. One paragraph, whose last sentence had been said three different ways across several days and remained wrong in a slightly different way each time.

Their separate reviews had kept Aadhya's hand protected and Ira's ordinary viewing intervals short. After rest, the helper read the existing words aloud. Aadhya listened with the hand settled. Ira closed the uncovered eye for part of the reading and opened it only when she wanted to check the line she was hearing.

They argued over a word.

Aadhya liked the first one. Ira said it made them sound much more certain than they were. Aadhya asked whether that was not the point of submitting college work. Ira said no, and the helper laughed before catching herself and asking which word to write.

They chose another.

When the paragraph was read back, Aadhya said, "Ab bas. Isse pyaar nahi hona humein. Poora karna tha."

Ira agreed to stop there.

The hand still ached. The covered eye remained covered. Completion had not repaired either injury or made the next page compulsory. The helper put the ordinary college sheet aside at their request, distinct from the private reminder Ira had dictated earlier about Ram's account. Nobody opened that reminder to settle an argument about an adjective.

Later, when Sitara checked on them, they asked for a short message to be offered to Rudra: the paragraph existed at last; he was not qualified to improve it.

There was no question about Pataal in the message. Their conditions had not expired while they were doing something else.

---

Rudra heard it after his own rest.

"Bahut achha," he said. "Mere naam pe mat bhej dena bas."

The contact asked whether that was the reply he wanted sent. He listened to it once, grinned and said yes. The exchange occupied less than a minute of somebody's work. It did not have to become a longer call to count.

Vedant came later, after his own forearm review and the handover which made the visit possible. He sat where he would not obstruct the nurse and asked whether the boy wanted company for a little while.

Rudra did.

For some minutes they discussed nothing larger than the completed paragraph. Vedant had not read it; he declined the proposed duty of deciding which student had contributed the least. Rudra accused him of protecting the other two from fair competition. Vedant asked which competition, since Rudra had yet to submit anything.

The answer was sufficiently accurate to end the prosecution.

They sat quietly after that.

Rudra could see the weariness in the man's face, the care with which he found a position for his own arm. He did not need another father waiting beside the bed to make sure he used the quiet correctly.

"Papa se baat karni hai," he said.

"Poochh lenge kab dono taraf theek rahega."

"Aapse unke liye jawaab nahi maang raha."

"Samjha."

Vedant did not add that he knew the man better, or that a son would understand when he was older. The nurse came past and asked Rudra about fatigue. He answered her first. When she was satisfied, the man in the chair asked whether he should stay until the call was arranged.

Rudra considered the offer. He was grateful for the company; that did not mean he wanted an audience for every question.

"Aap rest kar lo. Phone jab ho sake, bataa dena."

Vedant nodded. Before leaving, he checked that the ordinary help-call was reachable and told the nurse the visit was over.

Rudra watched the empty chair for a moment, then turned towards the window.

There was room to ask his father something without making the man beside him answer first.
