The call became possible in the evening, after both men had rested.

Rudra had not used the waiting time to attempt another chair transfer. The day's plan remained with the team, not with the anticipation of having something impressive to tell his father. He was in bed when the stand was placed, with the wrist clear and the ankle supported. In Varanasi, Ram had finished the care which needed quiet; the nurse settled the phone before leaving it within hearing.

"Veer se poochha," Rudra began.

"Suna. Dekhna chahta hai. Baaki baat abhi tay nahi."

"Haan."

His father had received the answer at the size it was sent. Rudra could feel how much easier that made the first breath of the conversation.

"Tumhe kaisa laga?" Ram asked.

"Achha. Phir laga uski haan se sab aasaan nahi ho gaya."

"Nahi hua."

"Par achha laga."

"Woh bhi rehne do."

Rudra looked towards the window. Beyond the pane, the branch had become dark enough that he could distinguish it only where it crossed a lighter part of sky.

He told his father about part of the meal: how it smelled different at the table, how the bowl had been moved closer. Not an account of every mouthful. Only what had felt strange enough to keep.

Ram listened.

"Maine bahut baar doori badhaayi," he said after a while. "Jab tumse poochhna chahiye tha ki kitni door tak pahunch paa rahe ho."

The son let the sentence reach him without asking it to mean that every distance in his life had been cruel.

"Abhi table paas karwa li," he said.

"Achha kiya."

There was a little warmth in the answer. Not pride in endurance. Something quieter, almost relief that the boy had been able to ask for an easier arrangement and still finish some of his lunch.

---

Rudra had not meant to begin with food. He was glad he had.

It made the next words harder to say and less like words he had prepared for an enemy. The man at the other end had listened to the bowl and the spoon. He was also the man who had kept a whole beginning of their family outside the son's reach.

"Papa, main aapse naraaz hoon toh bhi aapki baat sunna chahta hoon," he said.

Ram answered without making him wait through an interpretation of what the anger ought to mean.

"Sunoonga main bhi."

"Abhi jo bataaya, usse pehle bhi main aapka beta tha. Sab baat samajh nahi aati, par poochh sakta tha."

The nurse at Ram's end came closer for a check. He answered her, accepted the small change she made to the support and asked for a moment before continuing. Rudra waited. The pause contained care, not a new excuse for the old silence.

When his father returned to the line, he said, "Poochh sakte the. Tumhe poochhne ka mauka kam diya."

Rudra pressed the good fingers flat against the blanket, then let them rest.

He remembered being told which exercise to do next, which foot to put down, how much longer the body had to last. Some of those instructions had kept him alive. None had told him how to ask why his father's face changed when a stranger said a thing he was not permitted to understand.

"Naam kaise poochhta jab pata hi nahi tha ki doosra bhi hai?" he said.

"Nahi poochh sakte the. Mujhe bataana tha."

The answer did not repair the years. It placed the work of speaking where it had belonged.

---

Ram looked at the phone.

He had imagined this part of the conversation often enough since the first call: the boy angry about the secrecy, the father trying to describe a danger without giving the anger another wound to carry. In his imagination he always found a better first sentence. Here, with a nurse near the bed and the familiar tired voice travelling from Prayagraj, there was no first sentence left to improve.

He could only answer the next one.

Rudra heard the quiet and did not rush to reassure him.

The kavach had hidden things which the son had not known to ask about. Its loss had made the questions unavoidable. Yet the need to ask had existed before the emergency, before the raw colours became visible to anybody in the room. A child had lived inside those missing explanations while the adults called the absence protection.

He had begun to accept that his father's fear was real. What he could not accept was fear becoming an answer which could never be questioned because the person asking was always declared too young, too injured, too unready or too much loved.

There would always have been another thing to prepare for.

His wrist remained in the brace. Nothing at the collar had joined into a hiding edge while they talked. The truth had not given him a stronger body in which to hear it, and he had heard it anyway.

He asked without raising his voice.

"Agar kavach nahi tootta, toh aap mujhe kab bataate?"

---

# CHAPTER NINETY-ONE — THE DAY HE HAD NOT CHOSEN

---

> *"There was no date hidden from us.*
> *There was a fear which kept moving the date away."*
> *— Rudra's later notebook, beside the question about telling*

---

"Koi din tay nahi kiya tha," Ram said.

Rudra stared at the phone.

"Socha tha, jab tum aur taiyaar ho jaaoge. Jab kuchh sambhaalna aasaan hoga. Phir ek aur wajah aa jaati thi rukne ki."

"Aur taiyaar ka matlab?"

His father heard the danger in giving another instruction as the answer.

"Mere darr ke hisaab se? Shaayad kabhi kaafi nahi hota."

Rudra had expected a date he had missed, an age, some achievement which would have opened a door he had spent his childhood trying to find by doing the wrong things better. There was no final exercise. Nobody had told the child what score would make him old enough to know his father's name because the man deciding had never settled on a score himself.

"Toh main peeche nahi reh gaya tha," he said.

"Nahi."

Ram took a careful breath.

"Tumhe apne baare mein bataana tha. Maine apne darr ka faisla tumhaari taiyaari ka naam de diya."

The boy's good hand stayed open on the blanket.

For years he had been asked to become sufficient for something. It was strange to hear that, in this one part of the life which had been kept from him, insufficiency had not belonged to him at all.

---

"Bachcha tha toh kam bolte," Rudra said. "Jitna samajh aata."

"Haan."

"Sab nahi samajhta toh poochhta."

"Haan."

The second answer was quieter. Ram did not offer ignorance of that possibility as a new defence. He had raised a child. He knew that children asked the same question badly and repeatedly, learned part of an answer and carried it around until there was room to learn more.

He had allowed repetition in almost everything except this.

"Mujhe khatre ka darr tha," he said. "Woh sach hai. Par tum sunke kya sochoge, mujhse kya poochhoge—uska bhi darr tha. Main usse hifazat ke andar rakhke bolta raha."

Rudra looked towards the nurse. She was close enough to see that he had heard, not close enough to choose what he ought to do with it.

"Ab jo bataaya, woh main sun paaya. Bura bhi lagaa."

"Pata hai."

"Dono hua."

"Haan."

His father could not change the earlier silence into wisdom merely because the truth hurt when it finally came. Rudra had hurt before knowing too. That hurt had been less convenient to name because nobody had given him the words for what was missing.

The nurse at Ram's end asked about tiredness.

He answered honestly. They had reached the limit of the conversation, not the limit of the son's right to ask. Ram said that plainly before they stopped.

"Jo poochha, uska jawaab badlega nahi. Din maine tay nahi kiya tha. Tumne koi mauka nahi khoya."

"Theek," Rudra said.

It did not mean forgiven. It meant heard.

---

After the call, Rudra let the next care interval happen without searching it for another thing he ought to have done earlier.

The supported ankle needed the small adjustment it had needed in the afternoon. The wrist ached in its own unremarkable way. He said so, waited for the check and accepted help. None of it made the answer less important. For once, importance did not demand that he refuse every other need until he had understood it properly.

He slept later, then woke when the ward changed around him.

The sentence came back in the dark: no day had been chosen.

He could be angry at the years without inventing the version of himself who would have been awarded the truth if only he had trained harder. That boy had already been asked to do enough.

Far away in Varanasi, Ram rested after the check.

Rukmini was following her own plan in the nearby room. Dadi had gone with her. Dhruv's period beside the bed had ended in a proper handover, and the next nurse did not need the private conversation repeated in order to know where he hurt.

He lay with a clean face and an unfinished answer still moving inside him.

The fear he had named to his son did not belong only to some old battlefield. There had also been fear of being looked at without the answers, without the certainty which he had made so much of his fatherhood depend on. He knew the life which had raised him. He knew the terrible outline of the life taken before it. Between those truths were things he could not remember and things he could not fill by wanting.

He had treated the gaps as a reason to keep the whole beginning closed.

His own childhood had not required that of him.

He remembered a wet evening at a doorway on Earth.

The rain had stopped. Water still fell from the edge of the shade, one slow drop finding the same dark place on the ground. He was a boy, old enough to be sent back for the clothes he had forgotten to bring in, small enough to resent being reminded that the rain had not arrived without warning.

He had brought them. One corner of a cloth dragged through the mud before he noticed. He lifted it, inspected the stain and tried to arrange the rest so the spoiled part would be found last.

It was found first.

The woman folding the dry clothes looked at him, then at the wet corner. She made the tired sound of somebody being handed a task which should already have been finished. He began explaining about the rain.

"Baarish toh sabne dekhi," she said. "Neeche tumne ghaseeta."

There was no good answer to that.

He took the cloth where she pointed and helped rinse the mud from the edge. It was ordinary work, slow enough for his irritation to lose its first sharpness. Afterwards she hung the cloth again under cover. He stayed by the doorway.

He could remember this much clearly: the smell of wet earth, the corner made darker by water, his own wish to say something which had nothing to do with the mistake everybody could see.

The exact order of the words had worn away. The feeling had not.

He had been thinking about the people from whom he had come.

---

He had known by then that he had a beginning elsewhere, and that his first parents had not been able to remain with him. He could not put the thought beside an actual remembered face. Sometimes that felt like another failure, as though he were being ungrateful to people whose absence he could not even picture correctly.

People passed outside after the rain. Somebody called to someone farther along. A child ran back when an adult called a second time.

He watched the child return.

The woman asked whether he was coming in.

He tried to ask whether it was wrong that he did not remember.

It took several attempts. The question kept sounding as though he wanted a better memory of the wet clothes, or permission to remain outside after being told to come in. At last she stopped folding and listened to the whole of it.

She did not supply him with a face.

She asked what he was missing. He could not give an answer which would fit into a useful description. There were people he had been told about. They belonged to him before the wet doorway, before the cup he could remember dropping, before he had learned how to be troublesome in this house. He wanted them to be real without having to make up the part he could not see.

She let the untidy explanation finish.

There was no great speech afterwards. Ram could not have repeated one honestly if somebody asked him now. He remembered her moving the dry clothes off the place where he could sit. He remembered being allowed to stay quiet beside her while she finished the work. He remembered the absence of an instruction to be grateful instead.

The boy noticed she had folded one dry cloth with the edges uneven. He pointed to it, pleased to have something he could correct in return. She opened it, made the corners meet and put it back on the pile. For a moment they were occupied with the same small problem. It was easier to remain beside her when neither had to be the person who knew everything.

He had still been expected to help with the cloth he had dirtied.

He had also been allowed to miss somebody.

The two things had occupied the same evening without either being used to erase the other.

---

In Varanasi, the ward's night sounds returned around him.

A trolley moved. Someone spoke at the duty station. The rain at the childhood doorway belonged to a day long before this bed, not to weather arriving mysteriously because he had remembered it.

Ram answered when the nurse checked him.

He did not reach into the memory for a voice which could tell him he had been a good father. The woman had listened to a boy who could not make his question come out right. Her patience was not a judgment handed across years in favour of everything the boy would one day do.

He could keep her kindness and be responsible for the kindness he had failed to give.

The thought stayed with him through the next stretch of rest.

---

Day fifty-five began after the actual night of checks and sleep.

Ram's review concerned the injured side, his breathing, the positions and activity the team had allowed. He reported the ache which had woken him and accepted help before the morning conversation with anyone else. His own learned healing was not an argument against needing contemporary care, and memory had not mended the damage beneath the dressing.

In Prayagraj, Rudra's night had been broken but quieter after the last check. The morning team heard that truth rather than a cheerful version prepared to show that the family conversation had done him good. The ward plan continued with the same limb protections. He rested after care before a short call was offered.

Both men wanted it.

When the line connected, Ram asked whether his son had the strength to hear a small remembered thing. No hidden route, no instruction, no test to make the previous answer sound more acceptable.

"Bachpan ki?" Rudra asked.

"Haan. Yaad na hone ke baare mein."

Rudra looked at the branch outside the window, then towards the nurse, who checked that he was comfortable before settling the stand.

"Boliye," he said.

Ram told him about the doorway.

The rain, the cloth, the part of the stain he had tried to hide beneath the clean folds. Rudra smiled at that before the account changed. His father did not pretend to remember every reply from the woman who had let him sit down. He remembered what he had been trying to ask, and that she had remained long enough to hear it.

"Unhe yaad nahi kar paa rahe the," Rudra said.

"Chehra nahi. Aawaaz nahi. Phir lagta tha, unka jo hai woh mujhse chhoot raha hai."

"Aapki galti toh nahi thi."

"Nahi thi. Samajhne mein waqt laga."

Rudra looked down at the good hand on the blanket. He had spent so long imagining his father already grown inside every story that the boy at the door still seemed to arrive from an angle he had not made room for.

A child could be loved and still miss the people he had never known. An adult could learn that and still become frightened by a different child's questions. The second fact made the first neither a lie nor an excuse.

"Ab bhi lagta hai?" Rudra asked.

"Kabhi. Par uske liye jinhone paala unse door hona zaroori nahi. Tab bhi nahi tha."

He stopped for breath and answered the nurse's quiet question before continuing.

"Mujhe dono sach rehne diye gaye the. Maine tumhaare liye bahut baar sirf ek jawaab rakha. Jo main theek samajhta tha."

The admission had a different weight with the wet doorway behind it. His father had not been born knowing only how to command. There had been room made for him, and he had sometimes failed to make it for another person.

Rudra could hear that without being required to decide the whole man before the call ended.

"Mujhe unke liye aapke jaisa dukh nahi aata," he said. "Main sochta hoon, bura lagta hai. Par aapke jaisa nahi."

"Aana bhi zaroori nahi. Tumhaara unse rishta apni jagah banega. Mere jaisa dard karke nahi."

The son breathed out slowly.

He did not have to borrow a memory or perform the correct grief before wanting to see where the people had lived. He could begin with what he actually knew and let the feeling be as unfinished as it was.

They ended after that, while both could still answer the check and say goodbye without making either nurse ask twice.

---

In the afternoon, Rukmini chose a short call of her own.

She had rested after the shoulder care. Dadi had gone to eat with somebody else available nearby, and Ram was not sitting beside her waiting to hear what their son would say. Rudra had slept since the morning conversation and wanted to speak.

"Papa ne bataaya, koi din tay nahi kiya tha," he said.

His mother listened.

"Main sochta tha bas koi cheez aur theek karni hogi. Phir tum log bataa doge."

"Tumhe aise sochna pada," she said. "Humne jagah nahi di poochhne ki."

He could hear a small noise beyond her voice, somebody moving something out of the way. She asked for the phone to be left where it was comfortable and returned to him.

"Ghar mein sab jhooth tha, aisa ab nahi lagta," he said.

The words surprised him with the relief they brought. They did not withdraw the anger. They stopped it from having to burn every ordinary thing in order to stay alive.

Rukmini did not seize them as a verdict in the household's favour.

"Pyaar sach tha," she said. "Jo nahi kiya tumhaare liye, woh bhi sach hai. Ek se doosra mitaa ke mat rakho."

He swallowed.

For a while they spoke about the shirt she had asked after earlier, the kind which could be managed without pulling his injured wrist through a tight sleeve. He said the current one was comfortable enough with help. She asked whether he wanted another familiar one when the ordinary arrangements made it possible, not a reason to send somebody on an urgent journey.

"Jo puraana hai," he said. "Jismein collar tedha rehta hai."

"Do hain."

"Toh achha waala tedha."

She laughed once, carefully.

"Bahut saaf bataaya tumne."

He tried describing the colour, failed to improve the directions and heard her ask someone to remind her later rather than reaching for the bag herself. The conversation could have a future containing a badly described shirt. It did not have to end with either of them explaining how much the future meant.

Before they stopped, she told him she wanted a quiet interval afterwards and would not be taking another call that evening unless care required it.

"Theek," he said. "Kal jab ho sake."

He did not hear the choice as the price of having asked something difficult.

Afterwards he rested too. The wrist still occupied its brace; the collar of the shirt still lay crooked beside an ordinary dressing. No hidden cover had returned to make this little piece of home safe from every danger. It was simply home being remembered without having to be declared innocent.

The night went on in the three cities with no one required to remain awake to prove that the calls had mattered.

On day fifty-six, Rudra's review again considered the breathing, sleep and what the previous chair meal had actually cost. The team allowed another short supported meal after rest, with the same full assistance and protected limbs. He asked for the bowl to be nearer before beginning this time.

The request took less courage when made before anything had gone wrong.

He ate slowly. There were mouthfuls between which he could look towards the window instead of planning the whole movement again. He still needed help towards the end, and the staff returned him to bed when the interval was finished. The easier part did not cancel the tired part. Both went into the account he gave afterwards.

At the post, Veer continued his own reviewed movement and rest. In Varanasi, Ram and Rukmini had separate care to get through before anybody proposed a conversation. In Mirzapur, the girls had their hand and eye checks before deciding whether they wanted the short call offered for later.

They did.

---

Rudra told them the bowl story.

He began by saying it was something his father had told him after their earlier conversation, not a scene he had somehow seen for himself. The child wanting to reach before he had asked. The person being helped saying stop. The healing which did some work and did not finish all of it. The floor which still needed cleaning afterwards.

Aadhya listened without reaching for the protected hand. Ira kept the uncovered eye closed for part of it. The helper had placed the phone so neither girl had to hold it or keep looking at a screen.

Then Rudra told them what he had asked about his own mornings, and the answer about fear being given more weight than his pain.

This was new for them. He left time for it instead of treating their earlier knowledge of Pataal as knowledge of every conversation which followed the name.

Aadhya spoke first.

"Rukna jaante the," she said.

"Haan."

He heard the anger before she found the next words.

"Phir tumhaare liye—"

"Maine bhi poochha."

"Pata hai. Tumhe hi poochhna pada."

For a moment he wanted to defend his father against the sound of a sentence he had said himself. The impulse frightened him. He waited with it long enough to hear the difference: she was angry that he had needed to do this work, not asking him to stop loving the person from whom the answer had come.

"Woh sirf meri choton ka hisaab nahi hain," he said at last.

Aadhya was quiet.

"Aur usse chot kam nahi hui," he added. "Dono bolna chahta hoon."

"Bol sakte ho," she said.

It came awkwardly, with some of her anger still in it. That made the answer easier to trust. She had not arranged her feelings into a lesson for him before he called.

---

He told them a little of the wet doorway too: the question about not remembering, the place made beside the folding clothes. Again he kept to the part his father had actually related. There was no family portrait in the memory, no object which had survived to identify the people in Pataal, no lost voice which Ira could be asked to find by looking harder.

"Unhe khud sab yaad nahi," he said. "Usse jo pata tha, woh bolna toh rukna nahi chahiye tha."

"Haan," Ira said. "Jo pata hai usko gap ke saath rehne dena padta hai. Gap ko jawaab bana dene se kuchh theek nahi hota."

Her own missing records had not filled while he listened to his father's account. She did not turn his answer into a promise about the answer she might someday receive. The real people she had chosen remained real; the things she did not know could remain things she wanted to know.

"Ab unse kya kahoge?" Aadhya asked.

"Abhi soch raha hoon. Sirf aur sawaal nahi. Apni bhi kuchh baat."

Ira opened the ordinary eye briefly, then closed it again. The interval had been enough.

"Humse taiyaar jawaab mat maangna," she said.

"Nahi maang raha."

"Achha. Humne ek paragraph khatam kiya hai. Zindagi ka nahi."

Aadhya laughed softly. Rudra smiled and let the helper begin ending the call before another careful sentence could keep Ira there longer than she wanted.

There had been no new promise of travel. No demand that either girl revise her conditions after hearing that Ram had once been a lonely child. They had listened to a friend, and he had heard their own limits without making them the conclusion of his family's story.

---

Afterwards the nurse asked whether Rudra wanted quiet.

He did.

She checked the position, left the ordinary call device where he could reach it and returned to the work outside the bed. The good hand rested. The injured wrist had not been asked to hold a pen so that an important conversation could leave a more impressive object behind.

He thought about what he wanted to say next.

His father had been the person giving the account, the son the person asking for what had been kept. That had needed to happen. It did not have to be the only shape in which they could speak for the rest of their lives.

Rudra could tell him something too.

Night passed between days fifty-six and fifty-seven.

The phones stayed quiet through the sleeping hours. In the morning, Rudra told the team which part of the seated meal had been easier and when help had still been needed. They checked the body which had done it, continued the ward plan and kept the limits on the injured limbs. There would be another assessment before any larger change, not a discharge hidden in the fact that lunch had gone a little better.

He rested after care.

When a family call was possible later, he asked for it without describing the request as urgent. At the other end, Ram had completed his review and was resting before speaking. Rukmini had her own plans for the interval, and nobody went to fetch her to approve what either man might say.

The stand was placed. The line connected.

For a few moments Rudra listened to the familiar voice saying his name.

Then, unexpectedly, he asked about the hinge.

---

"Kaunsi?" Ram said.

"Ghar waali. Jiske screw se aapka jhagda hua tha."

There was a pause.

"Usne shuru kiya tha."

Rudra smiled.

"Haan. Screw ne. Phir maine galat auzaar diya tha."

"Maine galat maanga tha."

He had not expected the correction to come so easily.

"Ab maan rahe ho?"

"Tab bhi tumhaari ma ne bola tha. Kaafi der tak."

The son remembered the laughter from the kitchen. It came with the light at the door, the screw which would not turn, his father's irritation at being watched while failing at something which ought to have taken a minute. None of those details had needed a grand origin in order to remain inside him.

"Pataal waali baat pehle din boli thi na," Rudra said. "Uske baad yahi yaad aa raha tha."

Ram did not answer immediately.

The boy had been thinking of him with a useless tool in his hand while the name Daavansh entered the family. It was such a small version of himself to be offered after all the terrible facts, and somehow harder to receive than an accusation. An accusation would have let him find the place where he was guilty and stand there. This required him to remain a whole person in the life of someone he had hurt.

"Woh bhi aap ho," Rudra said.

"Haan."

"Isliye bola. Koi baat mitaane ko nahi."

"Samajh raha hoon."

The nurse at Rudra's end checked whether he was comfortable enough to continue. He was, for a little. Ram waited until she had finished before asking about the meal.

Rudra gave the ordinary account, the easier mouthfuls and the help still needed. His father heard both. The call did not become a set of instructions for tomorrow's plate.

---

"Apni baat kehni hai aapse," Rudra said after a while.

"Kaho."

He looked towards the bag beside the bed. There had been a time when wanting to say something to his father meant choosing the safest moment and the shortest possible sentence, leaving out anything which might be heard as an argument with what had already been decided.

He could feel that old calculation begin.

He let himself stop it without having to make the next words perfect. His father had admitted there was no hidden exercise which would have earned the disclosure. There did not have to be a hidden exercise which earned this conversation either.

"Aapki baat suni hai," he said. "Jo bataaya. Uske baad mere andar jo hai, woh bhi sunna padega. Sirf meri samajh theek karne ko nahi."

At the far end, Ram moved the good hand on the blanket, then left it still.

He had wanted his son to understand. He could hear now how easily the desire became another task set for the boy: hear enough of the fear, see enough of the past and arrive at the correct tenderness. Understanding could happen without producing the relief the father wanted.

"Theek," he said.

Not a promise to be without fear. An answer to the actual request in front of him.

---

Rudra heard the word and waited for the correction which sometimes came after an agreement.

There was only the small sound of care continuing near his father's bed. A question from the nurse, Ram's answer, a pause while the support was made comfortable again. The son did not borrow the interruption to withdraw what he had said.

His father returned to the phone.

The names had been spoken. The infant had arrived in the account as an infant, the boy on Earth as someone who could remember wet clothes and a doorway, the adult as someone whose love and terrible choices belonged to the same life. Rudra did not have to turn that life into a single verdict before he could speak from his own.

Ram had spent years telling the child what would be required of him.

For once he left the next requirement unmade. He asked, and the question held no answer ready for the boy to repeat.

"Ab tum mujhse kya chaahte ho, beta?"

---

# CHAPTER NINETY-TWO — A SON, NOT A DEBT

---

> *"The account ended with neither pardon nor oath.*
> *A son kept his father. He did not take his place among the dead."*
> *— Ira's later register, closing note on the first family account*

---

"Sach," Rudra said. "Aur mere faisle ki jagah. Dono chaahiye aapse."

Ram listened.

The answer had come without the list Rudra had once imagined he would need: every morning, every question cut short, every occasion on which a child had mistaken being frightened for being wrong. Those things remained. He did not have to recite them all before he could ask for something now.

"Jo poochhoon, usse bas meri galti theek karne ki baat mat banaaiye," he said. "Kabhi aapki baat se bhi mujhe galat lag sakta hai. Bol paaun."

"Bol sakte ho."

"Abhi kehna aasaan hai. Phir jab mujhe kuchh karna ho jo aap nahi chaahte—"

He stopped for breath. The nurse was close; he answered her check, let the position be adjusted and returned to the sentence without forcing it through the body.

"Tab bhi meri baat sunna. Sirf itna nahi ki mujhe kaise roken."

Ram looked towards the good hand lying open on his own blanket.

"Darr lagega," he said. "Phir bhi poora sunna mera kaam rahega. Sirf darr ko tumhaara jawaab nahi banaaoonga."

It was smaller than a promise that he would never fail again. The son could hear what would have to happen the next time for it to become more than a sentence.

He kept going.

---

"Mujhe aapka beta rehna hai," Rudra said. "Aapki har baat maan kar nahi."

At the other end, Ram's face changed. He had been listening for a departure, a refusal, the place where his son would decide that too much of the family had been taken away for the old word to remain.

The boy had kept the word and changed the obedience inside it.

"Mera beta hona tumhe meri haan se nahi mila," Ram said. "Meri na se chhin bhi nahi jaayega."

Rudra swallowed.

There had been times when a stumble, an unfinished exercise, a hand which could not hold its position had felt like something being removed from him. He had heard his father admit that before. Today he could say what he wanted kept without pretending he no longer remembered the removals.

"Pataal dekhna chahta hoon," he said. "Unke saath jo hua, usse bachke nahi nikalna. Par unki zindagi ke baad jo meri hai, woh poori karz nahi ban sakti."

"Nahi banegi."

"Sirf aapke kehne se nahi hoga. Mujhe bhi yaad rakhna padega."

"Haan."

The answer did not enlist him and did not forbid him every future act of courage. He could choose to help, choose to fight when something real required it, choose a life which contained the dead without making every living hour an instalment paid to them.

He had not come into the world having signed for a massacre he could not prevent.

---

"Aur jo aapse naraaz hoon," he said.

Ram waited.

"Woh abhi hai. Aapki kahaani sunke khatam nahi hui. Par sirf wahi bhi nahi hai."

The older man took his next breath slowly.

"Dono sun raha hoon."

He did not ask which feeling would win by evening. He did not ask for a gentler version to be sent to Rukmini, or for the son to let his friends know that a father had finally answered well.

Rudra could feel the tiredness arriving. He told the nurse. She checked him and said the call needed to end soon.

He looked at the phone, afraid for one irrational moment that ending here would make the unfinished anger the last thing left between them.

"Phir baat hogi," he said.

"Hogi," Ram answered. "Dono ko rest ke baad."

The qualification made the promise more solid, not less. Another conversation would happen through the ordinary arrangements by which their bodies were being kept alive. Neither had to purchase it by pretending to be well enough now.

They said goodbye.

---

Afterwards, Ram let the nurse help him settle.

There was no great movement available to him, no journey across the room which could carry the distance between Varanasi and Prayagraj. He accepted the adjustment, answered about the injured side and rested when asked.

The son had asked him to listen.

He could begin by not using the end of the call to send one more instruction through somebody else's phone.

In Prayagraj, Rudra closed his eyes after the check. The body was tired. The wrist and ankle remained injured; the raw colours were still part of a signature the old kavach no longer hid. He had not gained a new means of surviving the world by saying what he wanted from his father.

He had gained a sentence he had been allowed to finish.

Night carried them from day fifty-seven into fifty-eight.

There were checks and sleep, the sound of shifts changing, a cup moved quietly so that the person waking for care would not have to reach too far. In the morning Rudra told the clinician how tired he had been after the conversation. The chest had settled with rest. The wrist and ankle were reviewed in their own right, not treated as parts of an emotional recovery which could be declared complete because somebody had cried or spoken honestly.

The ward plan allowed a short staff-propelled chair visit to the corridor window after care and rest. The same full assistance would be needed for the transfer, the same supports and equipment would travel with the chair. No standing, no push through the injured wrist, no attempt to let the ankle discover its own limit.

He chose the window instead of another conversation in that interval.

The nurse checked the position before they moved. At the end of the corridor, the chair stopped where the light reached the floor but not directly into his eyes.

Outside, someone had hung washing on a rooftop. One sleeve had caught over the line, turning a shirt into a ridiculous thing with one arm raised and the other hanging as though it regretted the gesture. Farther down, a man was trying to move a bicycle past a parked scooter. The bell rang once, unnecessarily. Nobody in the way appeared impressed.

Prayagraj continued to contain people who did not know what he had said to his father.

He was grateful for them.

---

The air near the window smelled faintly of food from somewhere below.

He could not identify it. For a while he tried anyway, turning the possibilities over with more concentration than any of them deserved. The helper guessed something different. Rudra disagreed. Neither could prove the matter from this distance, and the bicycle had already gone before either noticed it leaving.

When the nurse asked about fatigue, he was tempted to give the answer which would buy a longer view.

He wanted to remain where the morning was happening outside him.

"Thoda," he said instead.

They checked him and began the return. He felt the disappointment of the chair moving away, sharper than he wanted it to be after all the fine things he had been saying about ordinary needs. He let it be sharp without making the nurse wrong for hearing the answer he had actually given.

The transfer back was fully helped. He did not lend a bad wrist to the movement because he was impatient to be settled. Afterwards he reported where the ankle support had become uncomfortable, waited for the adjustment and rested.

The view had been real. So had the limit.

There could be another day with different permissions. There did not have to be a secret attempt later that morning to take the rest of the view for himself.

---

Veer's call came after the next care and rest interval, when the post and ward could both manage it.

Rudra told him the answer about disclosure: no date had been set. The father had kept waiting for readiness which fear could always move farther away. He told the limited doorway memory too, identifying it as something Ram had related on the later call, not something the boys had learned from the account of the infant's arrival.

Veer listened.

"Tumne poochh liya," he said.

"Haan."

"Jawaab pasand aaya?"

Rudra considered it.

"Achha nahi laga. Par jawaab tha."

The friend at the post was quiet for a while. The helper moved the phone a little closer when Veer asked, and he spoke without holding the shoulder rigid to reach it.

"Ab?"

"Unhe bola, meri baat bhi sunni hai. Har baat meri samajh ki galti nahi."

"Theek bola."

"Darr laga."

"Woh bhi theek."

Rudra looked at the place where the chair had stood before the transfer.

He told Veer that wanting to keep his father did not mean he had become comfortable with everything the man had done. It sounded clumsy spoken aloud to a friend. Veer let it be clumsy.

"Mujhe kisi ek taraf khadaa karna hai kya?" he asked at last.

"Nahi. Apni taraf raho. Mere dost ho. Unke wakeel nahi. Mere gusse ke bhi nahi."

Veer breathed out with something like a laugh.

"Achha hai. Itna padhna padta dono mein."

Rudra smiled. The nurse glanced across; he answered that he was comfortable, then let the moment settle before continuing.

---

They spoke for a little about the corridor window.

Veer disagreed with both guesses about the food, despite having neither seen nor smelled anything from it. Rudra accused him of conducting a very confident investigation from the wrong bed. Veer said confidence was the only part of the investigation not currently restricted by the care team.

The joke left the shoulder, knee and ribs where they were. When Veer needed the position changed, he said so. Rudra waited, listening to the ordinary work at the other end rather than asking the colours in his own body to do anything about the distance.

Before they ended, Veer said, "Sirf kuchh tootne pe phone mat karna."

"Khaane ki pehchaan pe bhi kar diya."

"Haan. Ab lag raha hai is shart pe phir baat karni padegi."

Rudra was still smiling when the call finished.

He had not needed his friend to become a witness for the defence or the prosecution. He had told him something difficult, then argued about a breakfast neither of them could identify. The wanting to see Pataal remained between them as a possibility to examine, not a summons which made this ordinary morning less worthy of being kept.

The night between days fifty-eight and fifty-nine contained nothing the patients had to turn into a sign.

There was sleep, not enough in one stretch and useful in the stretches which came. There were checks. In the morning, Rudra reported the tiredness after the window, the ankle discomfort which had eased with adjustment, the conversation which had followed after rest. The team reviewed him before continuing the limited ward activity. Better breathing and an easier meal still did not permit weight through the ankle, a push through the wrist or a departure from care.

In Varanasi, Rukmini finished her own review before choosing a call with him.

Her shoulder still needed protection. She accepted help with the support, ate and rested first. When the helper settled the phone, she asked for it to be moved slightly so she would not have to turn towards the injured side. The small change made her voice sound easier when the line connected.

Rudra noticed.

"Ab theek jagah hai?" he asked.

"Haan. Ab tum bataao."

He told her about the window and the shirt caught on the line outside it. She said he had no right to criticize other people's collars after the description he had given of his own.

"Mera jaan-boojhke tedha nahi hota."

"Usne tumhe bataaya kya? Shirt ne?"

He smiled, and for a while that was the conversation: an argument without a cost attached, his mother being funny because something was funny and not because somebody needed reassuring that the family had survived a revelation.

---

Later he told her he had said what he wanted from his father.

Not the whole private conversation. The part which belonged to his own choice: he wanted to remain a son without being required to make obedience the proof, wanted to hear the history without having his life written as repayment for it.

Rukmini listened.

"Bolne ke baad kaisa laga?"

"Thak gaya. Phir laga abhi bhi ghar jaana hai. Aapse milna hai. Unse bhi."

The last two words came carefully.

"Naraaz hoon toh bhi."

"Haan," she said.

He waited for the claim which might once have followed: that wanting to come home proved the anger had never been serious, that love would settle the rest when they were together again.

She made none.

"Mujhe bhi tumse milna hai," she said instead. "Jab sach mein ho sake. Abhi phone pe tum bol rahe ho, woh bhi sun rahi hoon."

There was relief in being missed without being hurried out of the bed. He could want the familiar door while remaining in Prayagraj, could want to see Pataal without making his mother's city a place he had outgrown.

"Aapko darr lagta hai," he said.

"Haan."

"Mujhe bhi."

The admissions stayed beside each other. Neither became the other's instruction.

Rukmini asked him to keep reporting what his body was actually doing, even when it made a hoped-for thing take longer. Not for her to know every private thought; for the people caring for him to know when they needed to help. He said he would. It was a promise about truth he could practise, not a guarantee that wanting to live would make every later place safe.

---

Before they ended, he asked whether Dadi had found the right shirt.

"Abhi kaam chal raha hai," Rukmini said. "Tumhaari itni badi pehchaan di hui hai."

"Rang bhi bataaya tha."

"Do alag rang bataaye the."

He tried again. The helper at her end laughed. Rukmini repeated the new description so somebody could remember it later, when the ordinary work allowed; nobody was being sent away from a patient to prove that a familiar shirt could be found on command.

For the moment, the one he had was comfortable with help. That would do.

They said goodbye before tiredness made it difficult.

---

Rukmini lay quietly after the phone was taken away.

Her free hand rested palm upwards on the sheet. Near the wrist, a small pulse moved beneath the skin, almost lost whenever the light changed across it. She watched until she no longer had to look for each movement.

Her son was far away. He had asked hard questions and still wanted to come home. He had spoken of wanting to see another place without asking her to pretend she was not frightened. There was no photograph which could make all of that simple enough to send to someone else.

The pulse went on.

She let her fingers uncurl. The shoulder stayed supported. When the nurse came, she answered about the body in the bed, not only the boy whose voice had just left the room. The help offered was for her too.

Dadi returned a little later and saw the quiet face.

She did not ask for the son's exact words. She moved the water where Rukmini could reach it safely and sat for the interval she had available before her own rest.

Rukmini turned the good hand over on the sheet.

Neither woman needed to make the quiet into a message for Ram. He would have his own conversation with the boy when both could manage it. This one had belonged to a mother and a son, and the space after it was allowed to remain hers.

Day sixty began after another staffed night, in the three cities still separate.

Rudra's morning review used the actual outcomes of the recent meals and window visit. The quieter breathing had continued. His wrist and ankle still required protection, help and further review; a conversation about a journey could not supply the missing permissions. Veer's team had their own shoulder, rib and knee questions. In Mirzapur, Aadhya's hand and Ira's eyes remained individual matters, with no shared recovery date for friends who wanted the same thing.

After rest, a brief call linking the four became possible. Each had been asked whether they wanted it. Each team knew the interval; the phones were supported where they would not have to be held.

Rudra could hear the two girls at one end and Veer at the other. The familiar arrangement made him want to turn and find them in the room.

He stayed in the bed and listened.

"Paragraph ka mazaak baad mein," Aadhya said before he could begin.

"Main toh poochhne waala tha kaisi ho."

"Phir poochho."

He did.

They gave the actual answers: some things easier, some still tiring, the same needs which did not disappear because everyone could hear everyone else at once. Veer complained that a conversation requiring no travel still involved more arrangements than their old college plans. Ira pointed out that those plans had mostly consisted of arriving late and asking someone else what had been decided.

"Bahut galat yaad hai tumhe," Veer said.

"Abhi bhi poochh rahe ho kya tay hua."

For a little they were allowed to enjoy the familiar unfairness of being accurately described by a friend.

---

Rudra told them he wanted to begin asking for the practical information, not collect another round of promises.

What each could currently do, what would need to change before a later journey could be considered, what care could not be interrupted. They could ask their own teams first. If someone did not want to share a private detail with the whole group, they could choose what the people helping with future arrangements actually needed to know.

"Meri abhi waali baat wahi hai," Ira said. "Pehle samajhna hai."

"Haan," Rudra answered. "Usse badalne ko nahi keh raha."

Aadhya wanted the return questions kept beside the questions about arriving. Veer asked that nobody describe him to anyone as already fit to look after the other three. He would give his own account of the body he was in.

"Aur kisi ne rukna bola toh usko sabki himmat ka hisaab na dena pade," Aadhya added.

Rudra nodded, then remembered the others could not see it.

"Theek."

They had not made the unknown place safe. They had made it harder to hide a known difficulty inside excitement. It was a modest beginning and, unlike a declaration that friendship would handle everything, something they could actually do in the cities where they were.

The call ended within the permitted interval. There was no oath at the end, no agreement to practise a form in secret so the next review would find them more useful.

Later Rudra asked Vedant to help offer the practical questions to the appropriate care teams, with the others' agreement and only the necessary information shared. Vedant accepted the limited task. No departure booking or route had been arranged; the questions would have to reach the people who could honestly answer them before a plan could pretend to exist.

---

In Varanasi, the district had already found a less patient version.

Near the tournament ground, somebody said the boy had walked away after the light. By the next retelling, all four had stood together and agreed to fight again. A third voice supplied an audience which nobody who had actually been there remembered seeing.

"Tum the?" a man at the tea stall asked.

The speaker said someone he knew had been.

Beyond the stall, the boundary rope still kept people off the damaged part of the ground. A worker checked the edge from the permitted side, then put his tool down. The repair hours already done had been paid. Payment had not made the surface safe to open, and the rope had not lowered itself because a better story was circulating beside it.

Somebody repeated that the boy had walked.

"Yahaan toh nahi dekha," the worker said.

It was not enough to end the rumour. It was enough to leave one ordinary objection inside it.

The tea was poured. A customer asked for less sugar and received an argument about when he ought to have asked. The cups needed washing again.

No one at the stall knew the private words spoken between a father and son in two distant beds. No old name or ruined birthplace entered the talk. The district had public damage, remembered light and the confidence with which people added a body rising whenever a story seemed to need one.

In Prayagraj, the body was resting after care.

Rudra did not hear the tale. He had not stood, had not healed by learning his lineage, had not used his friends' interest to make himself the appointed leader of an already departing company.

The bed held him. The next question could wait until he had slept.

The evening family call was arranged after that rest, not instead of it.

Rudra had answered the next check and eaten with the help he still needed. In Varanasi, Ram's care was finished for the moment; the phone was settled where speaking would not make him hold an uncomfortable position. Rukmini was resting. Nobody brought her in to make a family tableau out of two people finally managing to speak for themselves.

"Doston se baat hui," Rudra said.

"Haan?"

He gave the practical part: they wanted accurate information from their own teams before anyone tried to make a journey plan. Their separate answers still belonged to them. Vedant would help put the questions to the right people, not announce that four patients were ready to leave.

"Achha," Ram said.

Rudra heard no disappointment that other people had been asked to help with something his father had offered to help prepare. The offer did not have to mean that every answer would come through one man's hands.

"Aapse ek baat aur," he said.

"Kaho."

---

"Aap wahaan se bachche the jab aaye. Apne maa-baap ko chhodne ka faisla nahi kiya tha."

Ram kept looking at the phone.

"Haan."

"Aur yahaan jo zindagi bani, woh unki jagah chori karke nahi bani. Jinhone paala, woh bhi apne the."

The son was not repeating an examination answer. His voice had the slow care of someone placing things beside each other because he had stopped believing that one had to be thrown away to make room for the next.

"Main Pataal ko aapki paidaish ki jagah keh sakta hoon," he said. "Aur Varanasi ko ghar. Ismein kisi ko kam nahi karna."

Ram's good hand moved once on the blanket.

"Nahi karna."

"Jo unke saath hua, galat tha. Aap bach gaye, woh galat nahi tha."

The older man shut his eyes for a moment.

He had told the account as a father, trying to let the son hear what had been kept. Now the son had given something back without offering himself as payment. The infant's life could remain a life saved, rather than the first entry in an obligation which had to pass from one child to another until it was settled in blood.

Rudra waited through the next breath.

"Main aapke liye wahaan ka bachpan laane nahi jaa sakta," he said. "Apna pehla dekhna hoga. Jab jaa sakein."

"Pata hai."

"Bas aapko bhi yaad rahe. Mujhe bhi."

Ram opened his eyes.

"Rahega."

---

They spoke about the corridor window for a while, then the shirt which had become a more difficult identification problem than either boy at the Prayagraj beds had expected. Rudra explained the muddle of colours he had given his mother when trying to describe it. Ram admitted he was unlikely to help distinguish two crooked collars by a colour Rudra had already described differently twice.

"Aapko toh pata hona chahiye," the son said.

"Maine pehna nahi hai."

"Phir bhi ghar mein tha."

"Screw bhi ghar mein tha. Usne bhi meri nahi suni."

Rudra laughed softly, stopped when he needed to and answered the nurse's glance. His father waited without using the interruption to make the laugh into evidence that everything had become easy.

When the line was quiet again, Rudra said, "Pyaar karta hoon aapse."

The words came plainly. He did not add a withdrawal of the anger to make them acceptable.

"Main bhi, beta," Ram said.

Rudra let himself hear it. He had always known something of that love, even when he had not known how to live beside its fear. Naming what had hurt him did not require him to pretend that no love had ever reached him. Accepting the love did not make the hurt disappear.

His father did not ask for the missing pardon.

---

The boy rested back into the support while the nurse checked him once more. The conversation was nearing its end. He said he was tired, that the position was comfortable now, that he needed only a little longer if the team allowed it.

A little was possible. Not an unlimited evening.

The wrist stayed in the brace. The ankle stayed protected. Beyond the window, Prayagraj's lights belonged to streets he could not yet walk. His friends had their own bodies and their own answers; his mother had her rest; the father at the other end was still an injured man, not a doorway which love could force open.

Rudra knew his father's birth name now. He knew why an infant had been sent away, where he had been found, that there had been years of being raised rather than merely years of preparing to fight. He knew the fear had preceded his own birth, that the victory six years before it had not taught his father how to stop being afraid. His training had begun afterwards, when he was one.

Those truths no longer waited outside his life for permission to enter.

He did not need to become the lost child in order to remain this man's son.

He looked at the phone, at the ordinary means by which they could still hear each other across the distance, and asked for what came next.

"Papa…Ab Pataal tak pahunchne ka raasta kaise tay karenge?"

---

# CHAPTER NINETY-THREE — THE WAY THERE AND BACK

---

> *"The first mark on the route was not an arrow pointing in.*
> *It was the place where someone had turned around and come back."*
> *— Ira's later register, before the first account written in Pataal*

---

"Dono taraf se dekhkar," Ram said. "Jahaan se jaayenge, wahin se laut bhi sakte hain—yeh pehle dekhna hoga."

Rudra watched the phone.

"Raasta hai?"

"Ek puraana kudrati guzar hai. Uski jaanch karaayenge. Dhruv aur Sitara se baat hogi. Tumhaare pahunchne se pehle unhe dekhna hoga ki aaj kis haal mein hai."

There was relief in hearing something other than strength named as the first requirement. There was also disappointment. A route which had to be examined was less like the road Rudra had been imagining than the answer which would finally let him stop imagining.

"Aap wahin se aaye the?"

"Uska daava nahi karoonga. Mujhe bachpan mein kaise bheja gaya, uska poora raasta yaad nahi. Yeh aaj jaane ki jaanch hai. Us din ka saboot nahi."

His father let the distinction remain plain.

The infant's arrival would not become a set of directions because the son now needed one. Nor would the broken machine at the ghat be made whole for their convenience. This was a passage to be inspected, not a wound to be forced open again.

"Aur mujhe?" Rudra asked.

"Tumhe apni team ko poora haal bataana hai. Kursi se aage kya mumkin hai, woh unke saath dekhna hoga. Dono kaam saath chal sakte hain. Ek se doosre ki haan nahi milti."

Ram did not promise to meet them at the entrance. His own treatment and movement remained unfinished work in Varanasi. He said that too, without making his inability to leave into something the boy needed to cure by asking less.

"Madad karoonga," he said. "Khud chalne ki baat abhi nahi keh raha."

"Samajh gaya."

They ended soon afterwards. Rudra was tired; his father answered the nurse's next question before resting. The road had entered the conversation without entering either injured body as permission.

---

On day sixty-one, Vedant brought the practical questions to the people treating Rudra.

Not a departure request disguised as an update. The boy wanted to understand what would have to change. He listened while the clinician went through what had actually happened over the recent days: quieter breathing, short supported meals, the corridor visit which had ended when he reported fatigue. Useful progress, and still a long way from managing a difficult journey without asking other people to invent strength for him.

The ankle was examined. So was the wrist. His left knee, which could so easily vanish from a summary concerned with the worst injury, received its own questions.

He answered about all of them.

For the present, the existing support and assistance continued. The next work would be controlled movement, tolerance and repeated review. He was not to stand simply because the possibility had been discussed. There would need to be a workable arrangement for the good hand and the help around him before any later attempt to load the injured leg.

"Aur doosri jagah?" he asked.

The clinician could discuss a body and the conditions it would need. An unfamiliar place could not be certified harmless by putting a medical signature beside its name. They would need honest information about the approach, the effort, the rest and how he could return for care. If the actual conditions exceeded what he could safely manage, a wish would remain a wish for longer.

Rudra listened through the whole answer.

Then he asked what he could begin today.

---

At the post, Veer had a separate version of that conversation.

His shoulder could not be volunteered to carry the other three. The knee would need its own progression; the ribs still made some efforts less simple than they looked from outside. He asked about supported walking and being a passenger, not whether he would be fit to stand in front of an unknown attack.

In Mirzapur, Aadhya asked what her recovering hands could manage through a long day with assistance. Ira asked about travel with the left eye still protected, the right eye's ordinary use and rest. Neither question became an agreement to whatever route somebody else later described.

Sitara heard what each chose to share. She did not need a private medical transcript to know that both answers would matter.

The practical account reached Vedant with their agreement. It contained limits, not four places reserved in a vehicle.

---

In Varanasi, Dhruv received the request after his own review.

The rope-burned hand had closed enough for the ordinary work allowed, though some pressure still hurt. He would not pretend that gripping a support or carrying a person was free merely because he could do other things with light. The care he had agreed to help with was handed over before he travelled.

Sitara could make time for an inspection when the girls' arrangements allowed it. They would go in stages, leave a receiving contact who knew when to expect them back and examine the passage as people who might need to bring tired companions through it.

Rudra heard that plan in the next permitted update.

Nobody asked him to bleed on a threshold to make it possible.

The next week had days in it, not a blank space through which Rudra could arrive repaired.

On sixty-two, the therapist worked through the movements which could be done with the limbs protected. On sixty-three, sitting cost more because he had slept badly; they shortened the interval and checked what hurt instead of treating the lost time as a debt. He had care through both nights, meals which sometimes required more help than he wanted and the unglamorous fatigue of using muscles which had spent too long waiting for permission.

By sixty-five, the easier parts were easier often enough to be useful. He could sit for more of a meal with the right arrangement. The wrist still needed its brace; the ankle still had not been offered the whole weight of him. The team examined both again before altering anything.

He began to understand why recovery could feel like repeating the same thing without being the same day. Yesterday's information mattered. It did not answer today's question on his behalf.

In the following days there were supervised trials, help with washing and dressing, nights in which he slept longer and a morning when he did not. The quieter breathing held through the actual checks. The marks where the kavach had failed were cared for as they healed; no new concealing edge grew over what had been exposed.

He was allowed to ask about standing before he was allowed to stand.

---

On day sixty-nine, after another examination and the preparation the therapist required, the answer changed.

A brief assisted stand, with only the newly permitted part of the load through the protected ankle, the left knee observed and the injured wrist kept out of the work. The good hand had a support placed where he could use it correctly. There were people positioned to help, and a chair behind him which nobody had described as failure.

Rudra looked at the floor.

It had been there through every family revelation, refusing to become less far away because he knew a name for the life beneath his own.

He followed the instructions.

The first lift was mostly other people's work. Then he was upright inside an arrangement of support, effort and fear so ordinary that it almost disappointed him. No great force entered the ankle. The knee felt strange. His head became light after the first interval.

"Baithna hai," he said.

They helped him sit before the words became a fall. The therapist checked him; the nurse stayed until the lightness settled. He answered where it hurt and where it did not.

He wanted to apologize for ending it.

Instead he asked whether he had put weight in the wrong place. They went through what had actually happened. The trial was over for now. A later attempt would depend on how he recovered from this one.

"Aaj ka kaam itna tha," the therapist said.

Rudra rested with the chair beneath him and believed the sentence by degrees.

Standing had not made him ready to leave. Being helped back down had not taken the progress away.

---

The later attempts belonged to later reviewed days.

By seventy-two, he could manage the controlled transfer more easily with help. By seventy-five, the clinician and therapist allowed a few supported steps in the ward within the prescribed load, the aid arranged for the good hand rather than pressed into the injured wrist. He tired, stopped and used the chair for the rest.

The following day was shorter. He had slept poorly and said so.

At the post, Veer was making his own progress: supervised short walking after the knee review, a shoulder still restricted even when it was no longer kept in precisely the earlier position, rib discomfort honestly reported when an effort pulled. He did not rehearse carrying Rudra as a way of finding out whether he could.

The girls' reviews in Mirzapur allowed longer ordinary activity with pauses. Aadhya's dressings could be reduced where healing permitted it, but the altered feeling in the fingers still required protection, and a useful hand was not a recovered grip. Ira's left eye stayed protected. The right tolerated more ordinary viewing in short stretches, then needed rest; the change belonged to recovery, not a new kind of sight.

Their agreed updates reached the others without turning four different bodies into one departure date.

---

While the patients were doing that work, the passage had been examined.

Dhruv travelled to Mirzapur after the Varanasi handover. On separate daylight visits, he and Sitara checked the approach beyond the ordinary road, the stone opening and the bends inside. They went through, stopped at the limited stretch they could examine properly and returned by the same way. The receiving contact knew when they were expected and heard them report back themselves.

The account came to Rudra before anyone asked him to decide.

There was width enough for assistance and the folding chair along the inspected line. A low uneven lip needed help; loose grit at one turn had to be watched. Beyond it was a broad place to turn and rest, not a licence to wander into every broken structure visible farther on. The return had worked in both directions during their checks. It would be checked again before the patients came.

"Tumhaari taaqat se khulne ka sawaal nahi," Dhruv told him in the supported call. "Hum guzre. Waapas aaye. Tumhaare bina."

The words took something heavy out of the journey.

Rudra would have to bring his body, its limits and his own decision. He would not have to arrive as the key.

On day seventy-seven, after the previous short day's fatigue had been heard rather than edited out, Rudra was assessed for regional transport.

The answer concerned a particular journey, not the whole future. He could travel to Mirzapur with the arranged assistance, the injured limbs supported, the planned pauses and the receiving care confirmed. He would not be a useful person for lifting bags or managing someone else's transfer. His current movement permissions, treatment and follow-up would travel with him in the handover.

The longer question about the passage would be reviewed there against the actual route account and how he tolerated the road.

Veer's team reached their own decision after checking the knee, ribs and shoulder. A vehicle seat which could be adjusted, help at either end and rest after arrival were conditions, not optional comforts. His report would go to the receiving people with him.

They did not put the two boys in an ordinary crowded vehicle and call the discomfort training.

Vedant had his forearm reviewed before accompanying them. He could coordinate and give the ordinary help allowed to him. He was not the sole person expected to lift a chair, drive, carry luggage and remain awake through everybody else's night. Two suitable transports were booked with drivers who knew the agreed run and receiving contacts. The costs were settled from what had actually been put aside for travel; keeping vehicles waiting indefinitely was not part of the arrangement.

Rudra listened to these things and felt, absurdly, more eager as the list became less heroic.

There was a journey being made for the body he had, rather than a body being demanded for the journey he wanted.

---

He told his parents before leaving.

Not a farewell from the edge of the world. A short chosen call, after their own care, with the ordinary information about where he would be received, who was travelling and when the next update should come. He told them the remaining limits too.

Rukmini asked whether the ankle would stay supported in the vehicle.

"Check karenge baithne ke baad bhi," he said. "Raaste mein bole toh rukenge."

"Tum bhi bolna."

"Haan."

She and Ram would remain in Varanasi. Their own recovery was progressing, but neither had been cleared for this journey, and neither asked a son to treat their remaining as abandonment. Dadi had her own work and rest beside them; she was not being packed into a vehicle because every person who loved the boy ought to witness him arrive.

"Pahunchke bataana," Ram said.

"Pehle rest karwaayenge. Phir."

"Wahi bataa dena. Pahunch gaye, rest kar rahe ho."

Rudra smiled.

He could send a small true message before he had the strength for a whole conversation.

---

They left on day seventy-eight after the final checks.

Veer saw Rudra properly before the vehicles moved, not through a little bright screen. His first impulse was to get up too quickly. The helper's hand and his own knee reminded him that seeing a friend did not change the transfer plan.

"Abhi bhi itna hi bekaar lagta hai," he said from the supported seat.

Rudra looked at the sling and the careful arrangement of the leg.

"Tumne toh poori kursi ko kapde pehnaa diye."

"Personal comment baad mein. Safar shuru hone do."

The laugh was small. The relief behind it was not.

They travelled with the planned assistance and pauses. Rudra watched Prayagraj move past the window and become road. There were shutters going up, a man arguing beside a loaded cart, cattle which required no interest in a patient's timetable to cross where they wanted. India remained stubbornly full of other people's mornings.

At one stop the ankle position needed changing. He said so before the ache became the only thing he could hear. They checked, adjusted it and gave him the planned rest. Veer's own vehicle stopped as agreed; the boys did not have to compare pain to decide which delay deserved the road.

By Mirzapur, Rudra was tired enough that arriving felt first like work: the receiving check, the careful transfer, the questions he needed to answer before anyone could put the bag down and leave him to sleep.

He answered them.

The handover included what had hurt on the road and what had eased with the stop. Nothing relevant was made private in the name of protecting the story of an exciting departure.

A brief accurate message went to Varanasi and to the girls: they had arrived, been received and would rest before any visit.

---

The next morning had a receiving review before a reunion.

The road had not produced a new breathing problem. The ankle and wrist still needed protection, the knee its limits, the ordinary help its place. Veer was checked separately. Both would have a quiet interval before the others came.

Rudra slept for part of it.

When he woke, the bag was where he had been told it would be. The phone was nearby. Beyond the unfamiliar door he heard a familiar voice complain that somebody had put the chair precisely where she had been told not to stand.

He knew Aadhya's irritation before he saw her.

For a moment, that was enough to make the new room feel less strange.

Aadhya came in with the hand protected and the expression of someone who had been patient for the last three minutes and considered that sufficient.

Ira followed with Sitara, the left eye still covered, the right turning briefly towards the light before she chose the nearer chair. Veer's seat was adjusted beside Rudra's. For a little while there was more arrangement than greeting: a support moved, a bag taken off the place where someone needed to sit, the ordinary delay before four people could look at one another without somebody else's elbow in the way.

Then Aadhya said, "Phone pe kam thake hue lagte the."

"Tum bhi zyaada shareef lagti thi," Rudra answered.

Ira sat down and closed the uncovered eye for a moment.

"Main toh dono jagah pareshaan hoon," she said. "Koi badlaav nahi."

They laughed, carefully and without grace.

Rudra wanted to reach for them all at once. Instead he asked for the chair a little nearer, then put the good hand where Aadhya could touch it lightly without using the fingers she still needed to protect. Ira's hand found his after Sitara described where it was. Veer watched and complained that he had travelled too, in case anyone was keeping an account.

"Tumne raste mein hi shuru kar diya tha," Rudra said.

"Toh credit pehle do."

The room was full of the people whose voices he had been living with. Their bodies made the distance they had crossed more real, not less. He could see the care still needed. They could see the raw colours at his collar without the old joined cover between them and what had been exposed.

No one asked him to make them flare.

---

Dhruv brought the latest route account when they were ready to hear it.

He and Sitara had checked the return again. They described the approach outside Mirzapur, the uneven lip in the passage and the clear turning place beyond it. They showed the ordinary sketch of the inspected line, with the turn marked where they would stop. It was a local working sketch, not a map of all the Lokas or a document which knew who had once lived in every ruin.

There were things they had not examined. Those stayed outside the proposed visit.

Rudra asked about the chair. Aadhya asked about coming back if somebody tired earlier. Ira asked who would give the spoken directions at the uneven edge, so three helpful voices would not tell her different things at once. Veer asked who would be doing the actual lifting where it was necessary.

The answer was not Veer.

Dhruv would help within what his hand could manage, with an able attendant who had agreed to the work and the planned return. Sitara would guide the line she had checked. Vedant would coordinate the Earth-side care and transport arrangements rather than pretend his recovering arm could be everywhere.

The route's existence did not depend on a boy holding a form. Nobody would have to keep a power alive while the others crossed.

---

There was another night before the next decisions.

On day eighty, the treating people considered the route account alongside each traveller's current function. They could advise what a short supported outing would require, what effort was not permitted and what changes meant returning immediately. They did not declare an unfamiliar realm harmless because experienced companions had walked its first stretch.

Rudra's breathing had remained settled through the road recovery. He could manage the limited transfers and protected steps he had practised; the chair would take the longer distance. The wrist would stay clear of lifting and propulsion. Veer had his own supports, walking limits and a place to sit, not a job carrying everyone else.

Aadhya's hands would remain protected from grip and rough contact. Ira would keep the eye cover, use the ordinary right eye only as comfortable and accept spoken guidance. Their care would not stop at the mouth of the passage. The short visit had to fit inside it.

The plans included water, the things each already needed for treatment, rest on the Earth side, a receiving contact, transport back and a return before the agreed care interval was due. The attendant's day and the transport had limits. Those were part of the plan, not people waiting forever beyond the edge of the story.

Each heard the facts before giving an answer.

"Is chhote daure ke liye haan," Aadhya said. "Aage ka aage."

Ira took longer. She asked one more question about the turn, heard Sitara answer and agreed to this visit with the guidance and rest described.

Veer said he wanted to come as himself, with the limits already given.

Rudra looked at them. These were different answers from the first invitations because they had actual information inside them now.

"Main bhi," he said.

He meant the short visit they had discussed, not every dangerous thing which might someday be done in its name.

The update to Varanasi went out that afternoon, before the last rest and the next night's checks.

Rudra spoke to his parents about what had actually been agreed: a short assisted visit, a limited inspected line and a return which had been walked and checked. He told them who would remain on the Earth side, where the receiving care would be and when they should expect the next update. There would be no need to pretend he could call from an unfamiliar place in order to make the arrangement feel safer than it was.

"Jo dekho, wahi bataana," Ram said. "Mere liye pehchaanne ki koshish mat karna."

"Pata hai."

His mother asked whether he wanted to speak to Dadi before she rested. He did. She told him to listen to the people doing the helping, then asked whether the shirt problem had been solved.

"Abhi jo hai wahi pehnoonga," he said.

"Toh theek. Saari duniya dekhke aana, collar hum baad mein seedha kar lenge."

He laughed softly. The collar could wait.

They ended while it was still an ordinary family call. The night which followed contained sleep, care and another morning check before anyone went to the vehicles.

---

On day eighty-one, Mirzapur moved past the window before the road narrowed towards the stone country.

The drive was shorter than the journey from Prayagraj, but it still had its own arrangements. Rudra's ankle remained supported, Veer had room to change position, and the girls were not asked to hold equipment in hands which needed protecting. The attendant checked the unloading place with Vedant before moving either chair.

They rested in the shade on the Earth side. Water was offered, symptoms asked about, the next care interval confirmed with the receiving contact. The drivers knew when the return was expected. Vedant would remain available there, with the practical means of getting someone back, not vanish into the passage because being present at every revelation sounded more important than the task he had accepted.

Dhruv and Sitara checked the short line once more. They went beyond the bend and came back themselves.

"Wahi haal hai," Sitara said. "Jahaan tak tay kiya, wahin tak."

Rudra looked towards the opening.

It was less grand than an entrance to a different world ought to have been. Stone, shade, the place where the ground lost the surface warmth of the road. No name waited above it. Nothing in the rock required the colour at his throat to identify itself.

He had been afraid of being commanded to belong.

Here, before the first step, he was asked whether the arrangement was still comfortable enough to continue.

"Haan," he said. "Abhi theek hai."

The others answered for themselves.

---

Sitara went ahead with Ira, describing the edge once and letting the other voices stay quiet. Aadhya followed within the space shown to her. Veer used his own support and did not turn the procession into a chance to prove he could walk without it.

At the low lip, Rudra made the brief assisted transfer they had planned. The attendant helped him stand within the permitted load while the empty folding chair was brought across. Dhruv helped with the chair rather than taking the boy's weight through the tender hand. They settled him again before the longer stretch.

The work was awkward and slow.

He could hear the scrape of an ordinary wheel, the attendant asking whether the ankle was clear, Aadhya saying that Ira needed a little room on the other side. No one held a transformation open. There was time to correct the position because no one had made haste the price of crossing.

The passage bent.

For a little while the light behind them narrowed and the air cooled against Rudra's face. He felt the old desire to look for a meaning inside every physical change. He could let a cooler place be cooler. He could tell the people around him if it became hard to breathe.

It did not.

At the next bend, Sitara stopped and waited until they had all reached the broad turning place she and Dhruv had described.

Rudra could see beyond her shoulder.

---

Pale light lay across broken stone.

Not a wall close enough for the eye to finish understanding. A road, or what had once allowed people to pass between buildings, continued away from the cleared place. There were fallen edges, gaps where roofs should have completed a shape, a dark rectangle which the eye wanted to make into a window until it found the open space behind it.

He was across.

The passage remained behind them, the checked way back still part of the visit. His body had arrived with its brace, its supported leg and the people whose help he could feel. Nothing had risen in his blood to explain the view for him.

Pataal was there without being remembered.

Rudra had spent days imagining the moment in which he would enter his father's world. Somewhere beneath the words, despite everything he had been told, his mind had kept the simplest shape of entering: a door, a room, somebody's shelter waiting on the other side.

There was a doorway ahead of him.

Nothing behind it was indoors.

---

# CHAPTER NINETY-FOUR — WHAT HAD BEEN A STREET

---

> *"We wrote courtyard. Then crossed it out.*
