> *An absent roof is not an open sky chosen by the people beneath it."*
> *— Ira's later register, correction to the first Pataal entry*

---

The lintel had survived the roof.

Rudra needed a moment to understand the shape. The doorway still divided a wall; it could no longer divide inside from out. Pale light reached a strip of floor which his eye kept putting beneath a ceiling, and behind that strip there was fallen stone, the jagged end of another wall, more light.

He stayed in the chair.

The attendant asked whether the ankle was comfortable. Rudra answered before letting the view become too important for the question. Dhruv stood beside the inspected line. Sitara waited until Ira had found the level place with the guidance she had been given.

Then they were quiet together.

There were no faces in the doorway for Rudra to mistake for people he had inherited the right to remember. There was a shadow beneath one side of the lintel, a line where the lower stone had worn smoother than the rough break above it. It was harder to look at the smooth part.

A break could belong to a terrible moment.

Wear required people to have lived before it.

---

"Aangan tha?" Veer asked.

He had found his own place to sit, keeping the knee in the position which worked. He was looking beyond the doorway at the space between the broken walls.

Aadhya followed his gaze.

"Chhat gayi bhi toh aisa dikhega," she said.

They looked again.

The space had the wrong certainty when named. Courtyard made it sound as though somebody had chosen to let the weather in, arranged a household around an opening to light. A missing room might have the same outline after enough of it fell away.

"Pata nahi," Rudra said.

It was the first account he could honestly give of something in his father's world.

The admission did not send him back through the passage. He could remain here without improving the facts until they were worthy of the distance he had crossed.

Ira asked for a description of the upper edge. Aadhya gave it slowly, one part at a time, while Ira looked only as long as the ordinary eye was comfortable. Aadhya did not turn the guess into a verdict to make the other girl less dependent on words.

"Toh abhi khula hua hissa bolte hain," Ira said. "Aangan kaise tha, woh nahi pata."

Nobody objected.

---

The broad place near the passage gave them room to rest and turn the chair. Beyond it, the inspected line continued a little along what appeared to have been a street. Dhruv showed the part which could be used and the fallen edge which was outside the plan.

They would not go through the doorway. Nobody had examined the structure well enough to put a person beneath that surviving lintel merely because it made a powerful first picture.

Rudra was glad not to be asked to choose between looking brave and looking properly.

From the safe side he could still see the line at the foot of the wall, lower than the threshold, where a shallow channel followed the street. Aadhya noticed it too.

"Paani nikalta hoga," she said.

"Ho sakta hai," Dhruv answered. "Jaisa dikh raha hai, utna hi likhna."

It might have carried water. He did not know who had cleared it, how often it filled or what the people inside had called it. The visible shape offered a possible use, not a memory complete with voices.

Rudra watched a little grit move along the channel in the air.

Somebody might once have been annoyed by that channel, by the work of cleaning it, by someone else putting the swept dirt exactly where it would wash back. He could imagine an ordinary quarrel without claiming to have recovered one.

That was new too: the ruined place making room for an irritation instead of a battle.

---

Ira asked whether she could make a short voice note on her phone.

Her own choice, not a task assigned because the other three wanted their first sight turned into evidence. Sitara checked whether she wanted the phone placed rather than held. She did. The attendant helped settle it once Rudra's chair was secure and everybody had a place to wait.

Ira listened to the recording begin.

"Pehla rukne ka hissa," she said. "Raaste ke paas. Ek darwaaza, uske peeche khula hua floor aur tooti deewaarein. Chhat thi ya nahi—abhi tay nahi kar sakte."

She stopped, heard the last part played back and asked to leave it exactly that way.

There was no name for the family who had used the doorway. No claim that the pale patch on the floor was proof of a particular day. The record contained the place where they were standing and the edge of what they could say about it.

Rudra listened to her voice coming from the small ordinary speaker.

The first account made here did not need an ancient alphabet to matter. It needed the courage to leave a sentence unfinished where knowledge ended, rather than where somebody hoped a better story would begin.

They moved only along the line which had been checked.

Sitara went first, naming an uneven place for Ira before either foot reached it. The attendant brought Rudra's chair slowly over the sound middle, with Dhruv watching the edge where fallen grit had narrowed it. Veer used the aid he had been allowed and kept the knee out of the turn which would have hurt. Aadhya stayed where she could see the ground instead of pushing a wheel with her protected hands.

The distance was small.

From the chair, it felt large enough to change the shape of the first view. What had looked like one broken front became several places divided from one another: a threshold set farther back, a narrow space between walls, another opening beyond the first. The road had joined lives which now appeared separate only because the things between them had fallen.

Rudra watched a patch of floor disappear behind an edge of stone.

There might have been another room there. There might have been a place where somebody put things they were tired of moving. He did not know. The wanting to know was almost painful in its ordinariness.

He had expected the scale of power to meet him first.

Instead there were corners in which people might once have lost ordinary objects and blamed somebody else for putting them away.

---

At the next resting place, Dhruv checked the level stone before Veer sat. The attendant secured Rudra's chair. They gave Ira time to close the uncovered eye and let the descriptions she had heard become a place she could hold without continuing to look.

Rudra asked whether this part had belonged to his father's people.

Dhruv heard the size of the question and answered the part he could.

"Pataal hai. Asuran Kul ka janm yahaan se juda hai. Par is dehleez pe kaun rehta tha, main naam nahi de sakta. Har ghar ka hisaab humaare paas nahi."

The answer had no little locked box waiting at the end of it. There was not a name Dhruv intended to release after the boy endured one more thing. He did not know the household.

Rudra let the unknown household remain.

It was harder than he had expected. If this could become his grandparents' doorway, he could tell his father something shaped like a gift. If it could not, he had crossed all this distance to look at somebody else's loss.

The second thought stopped him.

Somebody else's.

As though that made it small.

He looked again at the smooth lower stone and felt ashamed, then tried not to make the shame the most interesting thing in front of the ruined house. He was not required to identify the people as his own before their having lived here mattered.

---

"Mera Kul Asuran hai," he said.

The words were quiet. They reached the people beside the chair and the ordinary air around them.

Nothing answered from beneath the stone.

He had said it before, in the account of what his father had told him. Here it felt less like repeating a family fact and more like allowing the fact to occupy his own voice in a place which had existed without him.

"Main yahaan paida nahi hua," he added. "Par isse mera kuchh juda hai."

Aadhya looked at him.

"Haan."

She did not tell him what the connection was supposed to make him do next. Veer rested the good hand on his own leg and let the boy have the sentence without turning it into a pledge. Ira listened with the ordinary eye still closed.

Rudra could be related to a place without recognizing it. He could belong to a lineage without being appointed the explanation for every ruin in its world.

The knowledge did not lighten the ankle. It changed the way he was able to look past it.

For the first time since arriving, he stopped searching the doorway for a resemblance to the courtyard in Varanasi.

---

He asked whether they could take one photograph from where they were resting. The attendant passed his phone to Sitara.

Not of the whole group arranged as though it had conquered a view. He wanted the doorway and the open floor, without someone standing in the broken space to give it scale.

Sitara asked which edge he wanted included. He pointed with the good hand from the chair, keeping the wrist which needed protection out of the movement. She took the picture from the inspected line and showed it without asking Ira to strain towards the screen.

It was a poor photograph in the way the first honest ones often were. The pale light flattened some edges. A shadow looked deeper than the place had looked to the eye. The frame cut off the part of the road which made the doorway seem less alone.

"Bas itna aayega?" Rudra asked.

"Is tasveer mein," Sitara said.

He nodded.

He would have to tell his father what stood outside it. A photograph could remember what had been put inside the frame; it could not do the work of admitting what the person holding it had failed to know.

They kept the picture. Nobody improved it into a plan of the lost house.

A sound came through the broken opening above the channel.

All four turned towards it before the sound had finished. Low, then thinner, with a rough catch at the end: close enough to the shape of a voice to make the body begin listening for words.

There were none.

A little dust lifted from the hollow beneath the surviving stone. The sound changed as the air moved through it again. Sitara watched the grit, pointed out the opening and waited through the next change before naming what they could actually hear.

"Hawa. Is khaali hisse se."

Rudra let out the breath he had begun holding.

The explanation did not make the place cheerful. It made it a little more terrible in an ordinary way: stone which had once kept air out now giving it a shape like somebody trying to speak. The people were not required to return as voices for their absence to be heard.

Veer looked towards the broken edge, then down at his own hand.

"Har aawaaz ka jawaab dene ki aadat chhodni padegi," he said.

"Pehle sun toh lo kya hai," Aadhya answered.

"Wahi toh keh raha hoon."

The brief irritation helped. They were four tired people startling at a noise, not four chosen interpreters being addressed by the dead.

---

Ira asked them to wait.

Not because she had seen something beyond their sight. The uncovered eye was watering in the moving air, and the shapes had become less comfortable to hold apart. She said exactly that instead of letting the quiet around her be mistaken for a discovery.

Sitara came nearer, asked about pain and the change in what she could ordinarily see, and followed the care arrangement they had brought. They gave her shelter from the moving air and a place to rest the eye. If the symptoms did not settle as expected, the visit would end. No part of the ruined street was important enough to negotiate with that condition.

Rudra waited.

He wanted to ask whether Ira was all right, then asked once and listened to the answer instead of making her provide reassurance again for every worried face.

"Aankh band karke theek lag raha hai," she said after the rest. "Abhi dekhna nahi. Sun sakti hoon."

She chose to remain seated for the next interval, with Sitara beside her and the way back close. That choice had room inside the limited plan. It was not permission for the others to leave her behind while they pursued a more impressive view.

They stayed together.

The ordinary eye had given them what it could. Nobody asked the covered one to make up the difference.

---

Aadhya looked at the ground between her feet.

A shallow hollow in a fallen piece of stone had gathered a pinch of dust. For one ridiculous instant she wanted something green there. A small thing, nothing grand. A leaf would give the gaze somewhere to go other than towards the next broken edge.

She knew that wanting.

There were times when making a thing live had felt like the only answer she possessed to somebody else's pain. Here the impulse was almost an impatience with the view, a desire to put a kinder ending in it before she had finished looking.

Her hands lay protected in her lap. The altered sensation in the fingers had not changed because the need in front of her seemed larger.

She did not press a palm to the stone.

"Kya?" Rudra asked.

She had been looking down for a while.

"Soch rahi thi kuchh ugaa doon. Phir laga main kya theek karne chali hoon."

He followed her gaze to the little hollow.

"Main bhi aate waqt soch raha tha kuchh lekar jaaoonga," he said. "Pata nahi kya. Bas haath mein ho."

Aadhya nodded.

Neither desire was wicked. Neither became a reason to ask an injured hand for more than it had been allowed, or to call a changed patch of ground the repair of what had happened here.

For now she could sit beside her friend while he looked. She could tell Ira where the air was moving. She could ask for help when her own hand became uncomfortable.

She did those things.

---

When the next little interval ended, Dhruv asked how each was managing.

Veer needed the knee position changed before standing again. Rudra said the ankle support was beginning to press at one place. Aadhya wanted a moment before moving. Ira kept the ordinary eye rested and said she could continue with guidance if the others still wanted the last short part of the inspected line.

They made the adjustments first.

The place was not being taken away while they rested. No gate was closing because they had failed to be strong enough on the first attempt. The return time remained real, and it would come sooner if care needed it, but it was not a supernatural contest with the ruins.

Rudra drank a little water and felt it arrive as water, cool and ordinary in a mouth which had been waiting to taste something more important.

He looked towards the turn where their permitted line ended.

There was enough time to see that last view and come back, if the checked path and their bodies remained as they were. That was the whole offer.

He accepted its size.

The last stretch turned beside a wall too low to hide what lay beyond it once they reached the corner.

There was no climb. Sitara and Dhruv had included this level edge in the line they had checked, and the attendant kept the chair where the surface remained broad enough. Ira took the spoken turn, eyes rested, with Aadhya describing the change in the view only after Sitara had finished directing the feet.

Rudra looked past the end of the wall.

The street did not end with the houses they had seen.

More broken fronts lay beyond the first ones. The pale light found one edge, lost another, reached a farther line which might have been a wall or several walls standing together. There were gaps too wide for him to understand from here, places where the eye tried to finish a building and found another depth instead.

He could not see the end of the damage from this narrow view.

That was not the same as knowing the whole realm. It was enough to make the little house he had been trying to place inside his mind suddenly too small to hold the loss he was looking at.

He had come with the story of a family.

The view contained the ordinary problem of how many families could live beside one another without any one child ever needing to know all their names.

---

Veer reached the resting place and sat before asking anything.

He had once thought of protecting as something which began when a danger appeared in front of him. Here the damage occupied the space after all the moments in which someone might have tried. He could not put his body between the broken walls and what had already happened to them.

He did not know where to put the familiar readiness in his shoulders.

The shoulder which still hurt answered first. He let it settle into the permitted position, told Dhruv what needed changing and took the help offered. It was an answer he could actually use now.

Beside him, Aadhya described the farther view for Ira. She kept the uncertain parts uncertain. She did not count houses or declare the visible emptiness a census of everyone who had ever lived in Pataal.

Ira asked where the line of the nearest street disappeared.

Aadhya told her.

"Uske aage?"

"Yahaan se saaf nahi."

"Wahi rehne do phir."

The words were gentle. Not every distance needed to be conquered by another person's eyes before Ira was allowed to know where she stood.

Rudra listened to the description and felt how much of arriving had been the work of other people making the view possible without pretending to make it complete.

---

He asked whether he could stand briefly in the arrangement already allowed, with help.

The attendant checked the position first. There was space, the same protected load and a sound support for the good hand. The chair remained behind him. Dhruv watched the line rather than asking the injured wrist to do one unnecessary thing in the name of belonging.

Rudra rose with assistance.

The ankle felt the permitted weight through the protection; the knee required attention. He gave the body that attention before looking beyond it. He was not planting his feet as an owner, and no one asked him to kneel.

The altered height changed very little in the distance.

It changed the nearest threshold. From the chair it had been an edge; now he could see the worn strip leading up to it, the little irregularities which a person coming home might once have learned without ever thinking of them as knowledge.

He thought of the wet step outside college. Of the turn by the familiar door. Of feet learning the small dangers of a place until someone could be half asleep and still come through without stumbling.

People had known this ground that way.

He had not.

"Baithna hai," he said when the effort began to spread.

They helped him down. He reported the ache, waited through the check and let the support be made easier. No new injury had been demanded to prove the feeling real.

---

For a while he wanted the view to tell him what his father had looked like before Earth.

The desire arrived as it had in the ward: a face, a room, something particular enough for love to fasten on without having to reach through the whole terrible distance. Then he remembered the infant in the account. There was no childhood walk through this street for him to recover from his father's memory.

There could have been other children here.

The thought did not supply a procession of imagined last moments. Rudra refused that easy cruelty to people whose lives he did not know. He could look at the thresholds without inventing who had fallen on them.

He let the place remain larger than his own need for a picture.

When Ira asked what he would want included in a second photograph, he answered differently from before.

"Raasta bhi," he said. "Sirf ek ghar nahi."

Sitara took the wider view from where they had stopped. It still ended at the frame. He knew that now and kept it anyway.

Dhruv said it was time to begin the return while they could still make it without hurry.

The ordinary words changed the view. A moment earlier Rudra had been looking as though he could remain until the missing part became visible. Now the broken street would have to go on existing while he was somewhere else.

He wanted another minute.

He said so. Dhruv looked at the group, the way they were resting, the time left within the arrangement, and said they could have a short one here before turning. If anyone needed to leave sooner, they would. The answer had no bargain hidden in it: the minute was not being exchanged for a longer walk back.

Rudra stayed seated.

He looked at the farther line of walls and tried to stop fixing it in his mind like a photograph which would fail if he blinked. There would be things he forgot. Forgetting the exact shape of a broken edge would not make the people less real. He could write later what he actually remembered, with the gaps left in it.

Ira asked for one addition to her voice note before the phone was put away.

"Doosre rukne ke hisse se aur tooti imaaratein dikhti hain," she dictated. "Jahaan tak hum gaye, usse aage bhi. Poora hissa dekha nahi."

She had heard the descriptions and checked the wording with the people who had given them. The account named its source instead of borrowing an eye which had been resting.

Then she asked to stop recording.

---

A small piece of grit had worked into the edge of the cloth supporting Rudra's ankle.

The attendant noticed it while making the position ready for the return. He lifted it clear before settling the cloth again. Rudra said the support was easier and watched the little rough piece of stone in the man's hand.

"Dena zara," he said.

The attendant put it in the good palm once the chair was secure.

It was unmarked. A broken piece, no bigger than something he might have shaken from a shoe on Earth, grey on one face and rougher along the broken edge. There was no way of telling when that had happened. Nothing in it named the wall it might have come from.

Rudra held it loosely.

The weight was almost nothing.

After all the effort of crossing, there was a terrible convenience in that. A thing which could go into a pocket. A thing he could put before his father and say, there, I went. It existed outside what either of us had been told.

He knew it could prove no more than that somebody had carried a stone. He could have picked one up beside the road to Mirzapur and given it the same importance with his voice.

Knowing this did not immediately loosen the wanting.

---

Aadhya noticed the hand.

"Kya hai?"

"Pathar. Support mein aa gaya tha."

She looked, then looked back towards the street. There was nothing in her expression which could decide for him whether a loose fragment belonged in a pocket. She had wanted a leaf in the dust a little earlier. Wanting a small thing to carry more than it could was not an error she had earned the right to find amusing.

"Rakhna chaahte ho?" she asked.

"Papa ko dikhaaoon soch raha tha."

"Tasveerein bhi hain."

"Haan."

The answer was not enough for what he was trying to say. A picture was light caught in a device. This could touch another hand. The difference felt important even while he knew it did not make the grey fragment an heirloom or an answer about the people who had died.

He remembered his father's instruction before leaving: tell what you see; do not try to recognize it for me.

He had seen so much and understood so little of it. The stone offered the relief of looking certain.

---

Sitara helped Ira prepare to stand with the guidance she had chosen. Veer checked the position of his aid before rising. The attendant waited by Rudra's chair. The way back had not changed into a test while the boy looked at his palm.

There would be water on the Earth side, care due at an ordinary time, a message to people who would worry until it came. The ruins would not become less ruined because he remained one minute longer than the body could manage.

He could leave.

He could also bring something which he had no right to call a recovered piece of his father's childhood. He knew that already. What he had not settled was why the almost weightless thing still felt easier to carry than the view.

Ira heard the conversation about the fragment. She kept the ordinary eye rested and asked from beside Sitara, not from some sight which reached through the closed lid.

"Isse unhe kya bataaoge?"

---

# CHAPTER NINETY-FIVE — WHAT HE COULD BRING BACK

---

> *"He brought back two pictures. Neither was a home.*
> *For the rest, we had to let him speak."*
> *— Ira's later register, beside the first return from Pataal*

---

"Ki main yahaan aaya tha," Rudra said.

The answer sounded small after he had given it.

Ira waited. She was beside Sitara, ready for the way back, with the ordinary eye rested and no wish to make him produce a better thought quickly because the question had been hers.

Rudra looked at the unmarked piece in his good palm.

"Aur jo dekha," he added, then stopped.

The fragment could not show a street. It could not show the difference between a courtyard and a room which had lost its roof, the movement of grit inside a hollow, the way another person's voice became important when an eye needed rest. He could give it those meanings only by telling them.

The telling would have to happen anyway.

"Bas pakadke dikhaana aasaan lag raha hai," he said.

"Haan," Aadhya answered.

She had not asked him to be ashamed of the wanting. That made it easier to see what the wanting could not do.

He asked the attendant to help him put the fragment down clear of feet and wheels. It went beside other loose stone at the edge of the inspected line. He did not set it upright, make a mark around it or tell himself that the gesture had become a ceremony for people he could not name.

It was a little piece of grit removed from a support.

His palm was empty again.

"Do tasveerein hain," he said. "Baaki bolna padega."

---

They began the return.

The street changed when approached from the other side. A broken edge which had hidden the first doorway now revealed how close the next opening had been to it. Rudra had mistaken the first sight for something solitary because that was how it had entered his eye.

From here there were neighbours in the shapes, even without people visible between them.

He did not know which household had borrowed from which, who had failed to bring something back, who had disliked the dust being swept towards the shared way. He could let the possibility of those lives remain ordinary instead of dressing every unknown person for a final battle.

At the first resting place, he looked once more towards the roofless opening.

He did not feel that he had finished with it.

That was not a reason to stay until the body or the receiving people had to pay for his inability to find an ending.

"Abhi chal sakte ho?" Dhruv asked the group.

They answered separately. Ira wanted the same single voice for the uneven turn. Veer needed a short pause before the next stretch. Aadhya said the hand was comfortable enough if she did not use it for support. Rudra gave the actual answer about the ankle and tiredness.

The adjustments were made before they went on.

The way back remained part of what they were doing, not an admission that they had failed to go far enough.

---

For a little while Rudra tried to keep looking over his shoulder.

It pulled at the position which had been made comfortable. He noticed, stopped and asked whether they could turn the chair once at the broad place rather than make the whole return an effort to watch behind him.

There was room. The attendant turned him safely, waited through the look and brought the chair round again.

It was such a small accommodation that the feeling which followed embarrassed him. He had not needed to earn the last look by hiding where it hurt. Somebody had simply helped him have it without making it more dangerous.

The pale road lay in his mind after the actual road left the direct line of sight.

He could carry an incomplete view. He did not have to carry the stone to make it solid enough to count.

Ahead, Sitara described the next change in the ground. Ira listened and placed the foot where the sound led her, with her own consent and the help close enough to be useful. Veer waited until the space cleared. Dhruv checked the turn before the chair followed.

The movement had a rhythm without becoming a count which kept a power alive.

---

Near the low lip, a wheel stopped against a piece of loose grit.

The attendant stopped pushing at once. Rudra felt the chair resist and began moving the good hand towards the side as though he could solve it by helping the body lean. Then he saw where the injured wrist would have to go if the movement continued.

He left it clear.

"Ruko," he said, before the ordinary hesitation could become everybody trying to correct it at once.

Dhruv came to the wheel. Sitara kept the other three in the broad place behind the turn rather than send them round the obstruction. Veer had already started to rise farther than his knee liked; he stopped when the attendant said he had room to work.

They looked at what had actually caught.

It was a small problem, and they had brought enough ordinary help to let it remain one.

Dhruv found the grit caught where the wheel needed clear ground to turn. He moved it aside while the attendant kept the chair steady. They checked the position, eased the wheel back the little it needed and brought it straight again before trying the same stretch.

Nobody struck the stone. Nobody made the difficulty into a warning from the realm.

The chair moved.

At the lip, they paused for the planned transfer. Rudra was more tired than on the way in. He said so. They gave him rest, checked the support and asked whether the assisted movement still felt manageable within what had been allowed. He waited before answering; wanting to be out was not a reason to give the quick answer.

"Ab kar sakte hain," he said when ready.

The attendant helped him through the brief stand. The good hand found the support; the injured wrist stayed clear. Dhruv moved the empty chair with help rather than carrying a person's full weight through his tender hand. They settled Rudra before anyone began the next part.

He told them where the ankle needed easing. The change was made.

There was enough time to do that because they had started back before the last possible minute.

---

The light changed as they followed the final bend.

Earth did not announce itself with a voice. There was a warmer patch of air, the smell of ordinary dust and something growing beyond the stone, the familiar uneven sound of traffic farther away. The passage remained behind them as a route they had used, not an event which required shutting inside someone's body.

Sitara came out with Ira first and took her to the shaded resting place. Aadhya followed. Veer used the last stretch of his own permitted effort and sat where the chair had been kept ready on this side. He said the knee was aching before anyone asked him to stand again.

Rudra came through with the attendant.

Vedant was there.

Not appearing from nowhere, not returning from an unseen battle. He had remained with the transport and care arrangements he had promised to keep. He asked the ordinary questions while the boy was settled in the shade and let the answers come before asking what the place had been like.

Rudra told him about the tiredness, the supported stand at the outlook and the little ankle pressure which had eased after adjustment. Sitara gave the account of Ira's eye watering, the shelter and rest, the continuing comfort with the eye closed. Aadhya described her own hand. Veer repeated what the knee and shoulder needed.

The return had brought four patients back, not four clean pages on which a heroic outing could be written without the inconvenient parts.

Their receiving people would hear the same account.

---

For a while, no one asked about the ruins.

Water was offered. The things already needed for care were attended to within the arrangement, and anything requiring the treating team was kept for that actual review rather than answered by a mentor's confidence. Ira could remain in the shade with the ordinary eye rested. Aadhya did not have to use the hand to help hold somebody else's drink. Veer found the position which made the knee less intrusive.

Rudra looked towards the Earth-side opening.

It did not look large enough to contain the difference between the two views. He did not need to make it larger by deciding that every ordinary stone around it had acquired a secret name.

His phone was handed back from Sitara once the photographs had been checked as saved. He asked the attendant to put it where he could reach it without disturbing the wrist. The two images were still ordinary images, too flat in places and unable to include what the person taking them had not put in the frame.

They were his to describe, not his to improve into certainty.

Ira kept her own short recordings. She had not offered them to a group or a public archive merely by making them. When she wanted to hear them later, they could find a quiet interval and decide what to do next.

For now, all four needed rest more than an audience.

---

The brief arrival message went to Varanasi from the Earth side as agreed.

They had returned. They had reported the actual symptoms, were resting before transport and would speak after the receiving checks if both sides could manage a call. There was no claim that every body was unchanged and no alarming half-sentence left for a mother to complete alone.

The answer came back: message received; rest first.

Rudra listened to it and felt something in his chest settle which had nothing to do with a new power. Somebody knew he was back. He did not have to use the first safe moment proving what he had seen.

When the agreed rest was finished, the drivers and attendant checked the next move. There was help for the transfer, room for the supports and the ordinary road to receiving care. The people who had worked this part of the day would finish their agreed work and go to their own lives afterwards.

Rudra let himself be helped into the vehicle.

As Mirzapur's roads returned around him, he watched a man carrying vegetables argue with a driver about whose turn it was to move. The sound made him unexpectedly glad.

He had come back to a place which had not stopped needing ordinary things while he was away.

The receiving checks broke the group back into four separate accounts.

Rudra told the clinician about the stand at the outlook, the return fatigue, the pressure at the ankle cloth and the transfer over the lip. He included the moment when he had almost helped the stuck wheel with a movement the injured wrist should not make. He had stopped before putting the wrist into it; that was useful information too, not a reason to hide the impulse.

The limbs were examined. His breathing had remained settled through the outing and road return. The tiredness was real, and the plan for the rest of the day remained care, food and sleep, not a second test to see what the successful first visit had earned.

The existing protections stayed. The next activity would depend on the next review.

He was relieved and disappointed, which was becoming a more familiar pair of feelings than he had ever expected to live with.

Veer gave his own account of the shoulder, knee and ribs. The little rise towards helping the chair had not become a lift, and the knee's objection had been heard. He did not ask whether he could make up for the rest by carrying something later.

Aadhya's hands were checked as hands, not praised into recovery because she had managed to keep them out of the roughest work. The altered feeling still mattered. The cloth and protection were attended to where needed.

Ira told her treating people about the watering, what had changed, what had eased with shelter and rest and what she had chosen not to look at afterwards. They examined her, continued the necessary care and kept the left eye protected. Comfort after resting the ordinary eye did not mean either eye could be asked for more on demand.

Each person had gone to Pataal. Each still needed an individual answer about tomorrow.

---

Rudra was helped into the position which would let the ankle rest without pulling at the knee.

The room looked smaller after the view. It also looked wonderfully finished: a ceiling which kept being a ceiling, a door which closed into its frame, a floor somebody could clean without first deciding which pieces of a wall would fall if moved.

He lay looking up until the nurse asked whether the light was uncomfortable.

"Nahi," he said. "Bas dekh raha hoon."

She left the light where he wanted it and checked that the ordinary means of calling for help was within reach. There was no hidden condition that a person who had travelled so far ought to stop needing small things done for him.

He ate with the help and pauses he needed.

The first mouthful tasted strongly of something ordinary, and for a moment he felt guilty about being glad of it. Then he remembered the people whose lives he had been trying not to reduce to their deaths. They might have wanted a meal without being asked to explain how it honoured everybody who had ever gone hungry.

He could eat.

The thought was not a lesson received from the dead. It was a permission he could stop refusing the living body in front of him.

---

The second update to Varanasi carried the result of the receiving care.

All four had been seen. There were aches and tiredness, the eye symptoms had been reported and assessed, and the agreed rest was being followed. They had not been sent back into the passage to make the first trip look easier. A longer conversation could be offered after sleep, or the next morning if that was what both sides needed.

Rukmini asked for a short message from him when he could give it without being woken.

He recorded one before sleeping.

"Aa gaya hoon. Check ho gaya. Abhi thak gaya hoon, so raha hoon. Kal aaraam se baat karenge."

He listened once, decided it sounded exactly as tired as he was and let it be sent.

Her answer came before the phone was put aside: sleep; they would speak in the morning after care. Ram had heard the update too. No one required an account of the ruins that evening in order to believe he had returned.

Rudra let the phone go.

---

He slept for a while without an important dream.

When he woke, the daylight had changed. Someone had left water close enough for the permitted reach. The bag was in its ordinary place. The injured wrist remained inside the brace, and the first thing he wanted to do was scratch an awkward place he could not reach without moving it badly.

He asked for help.

Afterwards he lay smiling at the ridiculousness of that being the first problem after sleep. A world of ruins had entered his life, and the body had kept an itch available in case he became too impressed with his ability to think about it.

The nurse asked whether anything else was uncomfortable.

He answered, let her finish and waited until the rest interval was properly over before asking about the photographs.

There were two of them.

He remembered the street outside both frames. That was the part he would have to keep beside whatever he chose to send.

Later, when their separate care and rest allowed a short time together, the four looked and listened without turning the room into a meeting.

Sitara helped with the phone so that Rudra did not have to hold it in a position which tired the good hand. Ira chose not to spend her rested eye on the images. They described them while she listened, and the descriptions kept the difference between what was visible and what the person speaking remembered.

In the first photograph, the doorway looked almost complete.

Rudra had to say that aloud before he could explain the problem. The frame made the missing roof less obvious than it had been where he sat. A patch of light beyond the opening could be mistaken for a room farther back. Somebody who had never stood there might decide that the doorway protected an inside.

"Beech ka hissa dikh raha hai," Aadhya said. "Baaki ki jagah nahi."

The second photograph included more of the way. It gave a better sense of how one broken front followed another, though it could not show the turn which had hidden the first view on their return.

Veer asked to see that one again.

"Isme road hai," he said. "Sirf ghar nahi."

They still did not know whether that opening had belonged to a house. He corrected himself before anybody else had to.

Rudra was grateful without saying so. The corrections no longer felt like people taking the place away from him. They were helping him leave enough space inside the account for the place to have existed differently from his need for it.

---

Ira played the short recordings she chose to share with the people who had been there.

Her first voice sounded more certain to Rudra than its actual words were. He had heard those words made in the open air. In the quiet room, with the small speaker giving every hesitation an edge, the record seemed liable to become an account which somebody might later quote without the hesitation.

Ira stopped it herself.

"Likhenge toh correction saath mein," she said.

The courtyard that had become an unknown open area mattered. So did the difference between what she had looked at and what the others had described after her eye began watering. She left those qualifications attached, including the limits of the far view. She did not turn a difficult hour for the ordinary eye into a claim that another kind of sight had filled the gaps.

There would be time to put the account in order. Not all of it had to be completed that evening, and recording it had not created a promise to release every word to anyone who asked.

Rudra wanted the first return to remain something they could correct.

The wish felt less grand than wanting never to forget. It also felt more possible.

---

He asked whether there was anything they did not want him to give his parents from their part of the visit.

The question changed the room a little.

The family updates and care reports had already carried the symptoms which needed reporting. That did not make every private sentence said in a ruined street part of a family message. Veer did not offer his old conversations for Rudra to explain. Aadhya could speak about her own thoughts if and when she wanted. Ira's recordings remained hers; she was comfortable with him describing how they had kept correcting the account, not forwarding the files on her behalf.

He would tell what he had seen and what they agreed he could tell about being there together.

It was enough to make a beginning.

"Tumne kya socha, woh bhi bolna," Aadhya said.

"Woh toh clear nahi hai."

"Toh clear nahi hai."

She adjusted the protected hand on the support rather than reach across him. He looked at the space between them, close enough to be company without either person needing to prove it with a movement which would hurt.

"Ajeeb lagega," he said.

"Tumhari baat hai. Report nahi."

---

The evening ended before he became good at explaining any of it.

Veer needed the knee settled. Ira had used enough attention on listening and wanted the quiet which did not require following another person's voice. Aadhya went when it was time for her own care. The goodbyes were small and specific; the four had not failed to share a day because they could not remain in the same room until sleep.

Rudra lay with the phone put aside.

He had brought back no fragment to place in his father's hand. There were two photographs he could offer, neither a claim that he had found a home. There was the knowledge that an entire street could be larger than the family he had first looked for inside it.

He had not found an answer which ended the relationship.

That was different from finding nothing.

When the nurse came, he was ready to say where the ache was without worrying that it would be taken as a verdict on whether he should ever have gone.

The next morning could keep its own questions until he had slept.

By morning, the body had made its own account of the journey.

There was tiredness, less sharp after sleep but not gone. There were the familiar places which needed checking before he was moved. The day's care came before the call, and no one used the arrival of a new morning to take yesterday's protections away.

The four would each hear what could be asked of them next. There was no second trip hidden inside the fact that the first had ended safely.

Rudra asked for the call after breakfast and his checks, at the time his parents had said would work with theirs. A member of staff helped him settle the phone so the wrist could stay rested. He could stop if tired. He did not have to fill the interval merely because everybody had made room for it.

When the connection held, his mother was adjusting something outside the frame.

For a moment he could see only the edge of his father's shoulder and a patch of wall. Then the phone steadied and there were both their faces, his father supported comfortably, his mother looking at the screen rather than the small place where the camera wanted her eyes.

"So liye?" she asked.

"Haan."

"Khaana?"

"Woh bhi."

She studied the answer through the face which had given it. He had sent a message saying he was back; she could still need this small, unrecorded proof that the person who ate and slept had come back too.

"Tum logon ka?" he asked.

His father answered about the morning's care. His mother had eaten. Dadi had received the return message and could be spoken to later when she had rested. The lives on that side had not been suspended inside a single question about the passage.

Rudra was glad they had waited until morning.

---

He told them how he felt now, including where the outing still made itself known. His mother asked what the receiving people had said. He gave the answer without shortening it to the part which sounded like permission.

"Aaj aage ka review hoga," he said. "Kal waali haan har cheez ke liye nahi hai."

"Theek," Ram said.

It was not an invitation to argue for more. His father shifted slightly, found the better position and remained in it. Rudra saw the movement and did not turn it into a reason to conceal his own ache.

They could both need help during the same conversation.

"Do photo liye," he said after a pause. "Dikhaa sakta hoon. Par pehle bolna padega ki kya nahi dikh raha."

His mother nodded. His father waited.

Rudra found himself explaining that the pictures did not establish whose doorway it was. He had not found a family house. They had gone only as far as the inspected line, and the road continued beyond what they could see. The little part he had reached could not tell them how the whole realm stood.

He did not forward Ira's recordings. He did not borrow his friends' private words to make his own account sound less unfinished.

There was enough that belonged to his telling.

"Main aapke liye kuchh pehchaan ke nahi laaya," he said.

The sentence came out closer to an apology than he intended.

Ram looked at him for a moment. Whatever the sight of those photographs might ask of his father later, it had not yet been asked. Rudra did not have to decide the answer on his behalf.

"Woh tumhaara kaam nahi tha," Ram said.

---

His mother reached to steady the phone on their side. The picture tilted, found the wall again and returned to their faces. Nobody tried to make the interruption disappear. It gave Rudra time to put his good hand down instead of holding it ready for an object which was not there.

He could have brought the fragment. He could have made a story cling to its unmarked sides and asked his father to receive that story as though it had arrived already true.

There was no fragment between them now.

The doorway remained unknown. The threshold had been worn by feet he could not name. He had seen it from a chair, with a tired ankle and his friends beside him, and the sight did not become less his because another person would have seen more, or differently.

He had gone to a place his father had lost before he could remember it. He had not gone in order to give his father a childhood back.

On the screen, Ram let the explanation end. His mother's hand settled away from the phone.

Then his father asked for the part which neither photograph could bring into the room.

"Aur tum, beta—tumhe wahaan apne liye kya mila?"

# CHAPTER NINETY-SIX — WHAT WAS HIS

---

> *"The picture reached them before the story was finished.*
> *It did not finish it for them."*
> *— Ira's later register, on the first photographs brought from Pataal*

---

"Apna laga," Rudra said. "Sirf mera nahi."

He had not known that was the answer until he heard himself give it.

On the screen, his father waited. His mother looked towards Ram and back, leaving the small silence alone. There was no ruin behind either of them. There was a wall he knew, the edge of a pillow, the particular concentration with which she kept the phone from leaning while trying not to seem occupied by it.

Rudra could belong to that view too.

"Pehle ghar dhoondh raha tha," he said. "Aapka. Dada-Dadi ka. Kaunsa hoga. Phir laga, paas waale mein bhi toh koi rehta hoga."

He meant the people he could not identify, not the beginning of an account in which every opening acquired a name because he needed one. He told them about the worn threshold. The broken part had first held his eye; then the smooth place had become harder to look at. Someone had crossed it often enough for stone to carry the difference.

He had looked for the grandness of loss and found a place where people had put their feet.

"Woh pehchaana nahi," he said. "Bas dekha."

Ram's face moved a little, not towards recognition. Rudra could not know everything inside the movement. He had spent enough of the journey resisting the wish to make stone answer him; he could resist making his father's expression answer too quickly.

"Aur?" Ram asked.

"Dukh hua. Par sirf dukh nahi."

He stopped because the rest sounded wrong before he said it. He had been glad to get there. He had sat looking at destruction and felt something loosen, the long uncertainty of whether the place existed for him only as a father's account. Gladness beside those ruins seemed an ugly thing to offer the man who had lost people there.

His mother asked whether the ankle needed changing.

It did not. He told her so.

The interruption gave him somewhere to begin again.

"Pahunchke achha bhi laga," he said. "Yeh bolna ajeeb lag raha hai."

Ram took a breath before answering.

"Pahunchna aur jo wahaan hua, dono ek baat nahi hain."

There was no triumph in the distinction. It did not ask him to be grateful for the massacre because it had eventually produced this journey. It made room for the son who had wanted to see and the people whose lives had been taken without wanting anything of the kind.

Rudra let the relief be small enough to bear.

---

His mother asked whether he still wanted to send the photographs.

"Aap dekhna chaahte ho?" he asked them both.

She said yes. Ram looked away from the screen for a moment, found the position which eased his side and answered for himself.

"Ek-ek karke."

The nurse helped Rudra change from the video to the image while their voices remained on the call. He did not try to keep the wrist hovering over the screen to prove how little assistance the task needed. His mother settled their phone where both could see it without her holding it up.

The first photograph went.

It was strange to watch the little sign that the file had arrived. Something made in another realm had become a small rectangle inside a familiar conversation. The machine had done nothing to distinguish it from a picture of a meal or a crooked shelf. He was grateful that the phone did not know enough to turn the sending into a ceremony.

"Khul gayi," his mother said.

They were quiet.

He could no longer see their faces while looking at the image on his own screen. The absence made him want to explain everything at once: the roof, the missing wall, the way the light had entered from above rather than from some farther room.

He said only the first necessary thing.

"Iske peechhe chhat nahi hai. Photo mein lag sakta hai ki andar aur kamra hai."

His mother made the small sound she used when she had understood a direction and was looking again. Ram asked which side the road had been on. Rudra described his position in the chair and where Sitara had stood to take the picture. The answer stayed on that little stretch; it did not become a route for someone who had never walked it.

"Darwaaze ke neeche jo halka hai," Ram said after a while. "Wahi ghisa hua hissa?"

"Haan."

There was a slight grain to his father's voice which the connection had not put there. Rudra kept the rest of his explanation behind his teeth until Ram had finished looking.

He had brought the view back. He could not decide how quickly another person ought to receive it.

Ram did not say that he knew the doorway.

He asked Rudra to leave the picture where it was for a little longer. A sound came through the call: cloth moving, his mother asking quietly if the position was comfortable, his father's answer. Then there was the silence of two people looking at something which neither could put back into its former shape.

Rudra had expected a question he could not answer. Whose house, how old, how much farther. He had prepared his honest limits as though they would be the difficult part.

It was harder to let the image be looked at without offering an explanation which could make looking hurt less.

"Bas us taraf ka thoda sa dekha," he said eventually. "Poora nahi."

"Samajh gaya," Ram answered.

Not a dismissal. A boundary accepted without being bargained down.

"Doosri bhejo."

Rudra asked for the next file to be sent. This one held more of the way between the broken fronts. His mother said that she could see why he had changed his description from one building to a street. There were edges beyond edges. Even the larger picture stopped before the place did.

"Iske aage hum nahi gaye," Rudra said.

His father asked where they had turned back. Rudra told him about the inspected outlook, the rest and the return before the care interval, not a farther promise which he would now have to make true. He said that Veer had wanted a pause, that Ira had used descriptions when looking became uncomfortable, that Aadhya had kept her hands protected. Those were the functional parts they had agreed could travel with his account.

He did not give their private sentences away.

"Saath the," his mother said.

"Haan. Par sab alag-alag cheez dekh rahe the."

It had not felt like a disagreement while they were there. Only now, telling it, did he understand how much the visit had depended on not making four bodies and four kinds of attention into one witness.

---

Ram said he was glad Rudra had come back when he needed to.

The words landed beside the road in the photograph. Not on top of it. They did not claim the road had become unimportant because a boy's ankle tired on it.

"Aapko kaisa laga?" Rudra asked.

His father took time.

"Dekhke dukh hua. Aur tumne dekha, yeh jaankar kuchh aur bhi laga. Abhi uska shabd nahi hai."

Rudra had wanted a clearer answer. He heard his own unfinishedness inside the one he received and stopped wishing to have it cleared away for him alone.

They could be related without arriving at the same sentence together.

"Mujhe bhi nahi mil raha," he said.

"Toh abhi rehne do."

His mother's voice came closer to the phone. She asked whether there had been anything he had wanted to bring besides pictures.

He told her about the loose grit in the ankle cloth, the moment in his good palm and the choice to leave it clear of the wheels. He did not make the leaving sound wiser than the wanting had been. It had been easier to imagine placing something in his father's hand than saying he had seen a place he still could not properly explain.

"Yeh bhi keh sakte ho," she said.

"Abhi kaha."

"Haan. Sun liya."

He smiled. She had given the sentence somewhere to arrive without turning it into advice about how he should feel next.

---

The nurse came to check whether they were ready for the next part of the morning's care. Rudra noticed his good hand had begun curling against the sheet as though it still had to hold the conversation upright.

He let it rest.

"Abhi thoda thak gaya," he told his parents. "Baad mein baat karenge."

There was more he could say. That did not make the tiredness an inconvenient lie.

They agreed to stop. He asked that the photographs stay with them for now. His mother could ask Dadi later whether she wanted to see; no one needed to pass the files farther merely because they could be passed. Ram said he would look again after his own rest, not keep the son on the call as a guide beside every broken edge.

Before ending, his mother asked if he had wanted them to see something of him in the pictures.

He knew what she meant. Neither showed the chair, his face or the people around him. He had chosen the view rather than proof of a boy beside it.

"Main bhi tha," he said. "Photo mein nahi hoon, par tha."

"Pata hai."

The call ended without a new family secret.

Rudra looked at the darkened screen until it reflected only a piece of the room. He was still angry about things which had happened long before the journey. He was still glad his father had looked. Neither feeling had to be removed to keep the other honest.

When the nurse asked about the support, he turned towards her and answered the present question.

The morning review was not interested in whether the photographs had been received well.

It was interested in the ankle, the knee, the wrist and the work the outing had asked of the rest of him. Rudra described the ache after waking, the easier part after breakfast and the tiredness during the call. When asked what he wanted to try that day, he said he had been thinking of the few ward steps he had managed before the journey.

The clinician examined him before answering. The therapist asked about the transfers and how the support had sat on the return. There would be no increase in walking that morning. They could do the necessary assisted movement, settle him again and see how the day went; tomorrow's review would consider tomorrow's function.

For a moment the answer made the trip feel like something he had spent which he should have saved.

"Kal nahi jaata toh aaj kar paata?" he asked.

The clinician would not invent the other day for him. Yesterday's effort might be part of the tiredness. So might the rest of his recovery. They had today's body to examine, not a second Rudra who had stayed behind for comparison.

The visit had carried a cost. That did not make every later ache proof that choosing it had been wrong.

He asked what they needed him to report if it changed. They told him. He repeated the part he was unsure about instead of nodding as though understanding were a favour he owed them.

During the assisted movement, he let the good hand take only the work intended for it. The ankle was protected. The injured wrist remained clear. When the knee pulled, he said so before the position was held too long.

They changed it.

Afterwards there was no mark entered against Pataal for the ordinary fact that he still needed help.

---

Vedant brought the practical account of the outing only after the care was finished.

The receiving people had what they needed. The drivers and attendant had completed the agreed return work; they were not still waiting somewhere for a command to take the four out again. The route sketch remained an account of the inspected line, not permission to add another turn to it because the first journey had succeeded.

Rudra asked if he could keep a copy of the few practical points concerning his own movement. Vedant said they could set that down in plain words, with the current limits, not draw a bold line through every difficulty now that they were on this side.

"Agli baar ka plan?" Rudra asked.

"Kal ke kaam ka hisaab. Agli baar abhi tay nahi hai."

He accepted the distinction more slowly than the sentence deserved. Part of him had wanted a next visit waiting at once, so leaving could not be mistaken for the end of his connection. He looked at the chair beside the bed. He had not mistaken sleeping for ceasing to be a son. Perhaps a place could remain part of his life through an interval in which he was not travelling towards it.

Vedant asked whether he needed anything within the few minutes before his own forearm review.

Rudra wanted to add the words which belonged beside the photographs.

"Baad mein kar loonga," he said, seeing the time.

Vedant put the phone within the permitted reach and went when he needed to go. He had not become less useful by having a body whose care could not be postponed around every other person's request.

---

After lunch and rest, Sitara had time to help with a short note.

Rudra wanted his parents to have it with the two images, not a label left loose in a conversation which might later be remembered differently. He dictated where he had been sitting, who had taken the pictures, which edges the first frame concealed and that the second stopped before the whole view did. He did not number the unknown buildings as if numbering them had established what they were.

At the end he added that no household had been identified.

Then he asked her to read it back.

The note sounded careful and strangely absent of him. It could have accompanied images handed to a stranger by someone paid to describe a route.

"Ek line aur," he said.

Sitara waited.

He wanted to write that he had felt at home, but it had not been that simple. He had felt lost, glad, ashamed of the gladness, connected, tired. The neat phrase would have asked his father to receive an ease which the son had not actually experienced.

"Likho…Wahaan baithke laga ki main in logon ko jaanta nahi. Par anjaan rehne se unka mujhse rishta khatam nahi ho jaata."

When Sitara read it back, he let the imperfect words stay.

She helped send the note to his parents with the images already in their conversation. Nothing from Ira's phone went with it. He thanked Sitara, put the screen aside and asked for the water to be moved a little nearer before she left.

The message received a brief answer: they would keep it with the pictures.

He did not have to watch the phone until another response arrived.

Outside, a delivery worker argued mildly with someone about the right entrance for a carton. A trolley rattled over a join in the floor. The room remained a place in which other people's days crossed his without belonging to his story.

He closed his eyes and let them go on.

The short time together that afternoon was allowed to begin without the phones.

Veer had a complaint about a pillow which seemed determined to put his shoulder in the one position it disliked. Aadhya said a pillow was a poor opponent to accuse of strategy. Ira asked whether anyone had tried turning it before deciding it had chosen a side.

They had. The pillow was cleared of intention, though not of being uncomfortable.

Rudra laughed, then shifted because laughing had changed the way he was resting. No one made a lesson of that either.

The staff had helped him into the chair for the move. Veer had come with his own aid and a seat arranged. Aadhya kept the protected hands supported. Ira had chosen the shaded side, using the ordinary eye briefly before letting it rest. The time would end when care or tiredness required it, not when all four had produced a useful conclusion.

Rudra told them he had sent the photographs to his parents and added his own note. He described what he had chosen to tell about himself. He did not repeat his father's pauses or offer the family's half-formed reactions for the others to interpret.

"Theek laga?" Aadhya asked.

"Bolna? Haan. Khatam nahi hua."

"Khatam karna tha?"

He looked at her. She was turning the edge of a cloth into a shape which did not require a hard pinch. She left it unfinished when the fingers began to tire.

"Pata nahi," he said. "Shayad."

---

Veer said the road had surprised him most.

Rudra had expected him to say the size of the damage. Veer meant the ordinary possibility of going from one opening to the next. A place which could be fought over was one sort of picture. A place where someone might simply have walked out to find another person was different.

"Har jagah khada hone ki jagah nahi hoti," he said. "Kabhi bas guzarna hota hai."

He adjusted the aid beside his chair so it would be where he needed it when he rose. The sentence did not turn him into a man who had stopped wanting to protect anyone. It made the wish less likely to occupy every bit of ground before him.

Ira said that she had been angry when the ordinary eye began watering.

"Tumse nahi," she added before anyone could apologize for the air.

She had wanted to keep looking. Asking for rest had felt, for a moment, like accepting that the visit belonged more fully to whoever could see through all of it.

"Phir jo tum log bataa rahe the, usse alag cheez mili," she said. "Zyaada nahi. Alag."

She had not acquired a hidden view beyond the closed lid. She had heard where each voice slowed, which detail was difficult to describe, when someone corrected the shape of something first called a room. Her account contained those voices as sources, not replacements for eyesight or proof she could dispense with care.

Rudra had watched her rest. He had not known she was also giving up the particular picture of the visit she had wanted.

She had told them now. That was how he knew.

---

Aadhya said she had wanted something green in the hollow where the wind made its noise.

She looked at her hands rather than the little plant near the window in the room. It had no connection with Pataal, no duty to become an emblem because it happened to be within reach of the thought.

"Ek patta bhi," she said. "Bas itna."

"Kiya nahi," Rudra said.

"Nahi."

She let him hear both the wanting and the decision. No one had asked the place what it needed. No one had examined the cost to her hands and declared it insignificant because the act could be described as small. She had gone as a person allowed to look, not a promise that living things would spring up to repair whatever the four could bear to notice.

"Wahaan hona bhi kuchh tha," she said.

Rudra understood that she was not offering him a prize for sitting in a chair. She was resisting the same bargain on her own behalf: the idea that being present counted only if the body produced something to leave behind.

"Tha," he answered.

She glanced at him and smiled a little. It did not require either of them to reach across the space.

---

There was no single sentence which could contain all four visits.

They had been together in one place. It was still possible for Veer to remember a road, Ira the work of receiving other people's descriptions, Aadhya the leaf she had chosen not to grow, and Rudra the worn edge of an unknown threshold. One account did not have to defeat the others to deserve being kept.

When Sitara came to see whether they needed anything before separating for care, Rudra asked if they could leave the conversations there for now.

They could.

Ira wanted quiet. Veer needed to change the knee's position. Aadhya had her own treatment waiting. The staff helped Rudra back without requiring his friends to become the necessary lifting team.

His phone stayed dark during the move.

There was nothing on it which needed him more urgently than the ankle did.

That evening, after food and the next checks, Rudra read the answer his parents had sent to the note.

His father had added one sentence beneath the acknowledgement: he would keep the words with the pictures, not turn them into names the son had not found. His mother wanted him to sleep before trying to write anything more for them.

He did not answer with another paragraph. He sent that he had eaten and was following the rest already agreed.

The phone was put aside.

Near the collar, the visible colour remained part of the body the journey had brought back. There was no new layer between him and whatever had already been exposed beyond the people who loved him. Nor had the visit supplied a set of instructions for the colours. He had looked at a threshold; the threshold had not explained his blood.

He pulled the sheet into the comfortable place with help and let that be the last useful thing done that evening.

The night contained waking, a position changed because it had begun to pull, water, then more sleep. Nobody had to call the view back in order to make the darkness significant.

---

On day eighty-three, the therapist asked about the night before asking him to stand.

The examination and review came first. A brief assisted stand for the necessary transfer was manageable again; more walking was not added simply because he wanted it. The support would stay, the protected load remain limited, and the chair would take him where standing was not the task.

Rudra heard the difference between an invitation to try the allowed movement and a challenge to make it look easy.

He made the transfer with help. The good hand found the support, the wrist remained clear and the ankle accepted only what had been agreed. When the knee needed an adjustment before sitting, he asked for it. The therapist gave him time instead of making the next instruction louder.

Afterwards he was tired, but not in the same way as the morning before. The distinction went into the account with the ache, not into a private verdict that he had recovered everything which mattered.

The chair took him to the place near the window.

There were people on the road outside whose destinations he did not know. One boy was holding a second helmet while the rider searched a pocket. A woman waited beside a sack with a tear in one corner, keeping her foot near the escaping grain until somebody brought a bag. They were inconveniences small enough to remain inconveniences because other people had stopped beside them.

Rudra watched until the sack was moved.

He had wanted the whole view of a lost place. Here too, even with a window and a road he could see, he knew almost none of the lives crossing it.

That did not leave the road empty.

---

Vedant came after his own review with the plain account Rudra had asked for.

It was no new chart of the passage. It set down the limited movement the boy had actually needed help with, the places where they had rested and the symptoms to carry back to the treating people. Yesterday's successful return was not written as next week's clearance.

Vedant read it aloud. Rudra corrected one point: the pressure at the ankle had begun before the final outlook, not during the return. Vedant changed the line without defending the version he had brought.

"Aur?" he asked.

Rudra wanted to add where he had kept turning to look back. The words became tangled. He began again, trying to find the exact place in the turn, then noticed that the good hand had tightened on the edge of the sheet.

Vedant asked whether they should leave the remaining sentence until later.

"Bas ek line hai."

"Toh baad mein bhi ek hi rahegi."

He had not raised his voice. He was not taking the page away as a punishment for wanting to finish. He set it where Rudra could hear the next reading when there was room for one, then asked if the support needed changing before he went.

Rudra said it was comfortable. He was simply tired.

Vedant accepted the answer.

---

The boy looked at the unfinished account.

A few weeks earlier, he might have thought stopping at one line short was a particularly quiet sort of defeat. He still disliked it. That part had not been educated out of him by a single journey.

But he had seen the wheel freed because nobody forced it. He had seen a road by turning the chair instead of straining the body. He had come back from a place he had wanted badly to stay in, and the leaving had not erased his arrival.

Vedant was putting the pen away. There was no secret diagram on the page, no technique withheld until the student proved he could endure the pain of receiving it. There was one incomplete sentence and a man willing to let it remain incomplete.

Rudra asked before the mentor reached the door.

"Aapne kab seekha ki rokna bhi sikhaana hota hai?"

# CHAPTER NINETY-SEVEN — WHERE THE WATER FELL

---

> *"He did not begin with the lesson he taught.*
> *He began with the water he put in the wrong place."*
> *— Ira's later register, opening of Vedant's account*

---

"Jab main galat jagah dekh raha tha," Vedant said.

He took his hand from the door and came back far enough to ask whether Rudra wanted the short answer now or the rest when he had rested. There was no penalty hidden in choosing later.

"Abhi bataaiye. Thoda," Rudra said.

Vedant drew up a chair. He placed his own forearm comfortably before beginning. The unfinished account of the journey stayed beside them; he did not turn it over to draw a diagram which would make a story into the next exercise.

"Tumhaare pitaji se seekh raha tha tab ki baat hai. Paani chalaa leta tha. Kisne dekha, kaise dikha, uska hisaab kaafi tha. Paani kiske liye tha, woh chhoot gaya."

Rudra looked at the water by the bed, then at him.

"Koi ghaayal tha?"

"Nahi. Pyaasa tha. Aur main bahut prabhaavit karna chaahta tha."

There was a wryness in the answer which kept the memory from dressing itself for a battle it had never been. No one had died because the younger Vedant wanted to be impressive. The mistake was allowed to matter without being given a body to justify its place in the telling.

---

There had been a basin of water and an ordinary metal tumbler.

Vedant remembered the shine on one side of the tumbler more clearly than the rest of the place. It had stood near the things Ram was washing after food. There was wet stone where the washing had been done, dry ground a little farther off, a cloth which had been folded and put out of the splash.

Ram asked him to fill the tumbler.

Vedant could already move water from the basin. He was not remembering the discovery of a gift or the day his teacher had given him one. He was remembering the less flattering interval after he could do something, when he wanted every use of it to testify that he could do it.

He brought up a narrow curve.

The water held together above the rim, caught the light and went where he intended. He remembered being pleased before it had arrived. The pleasure made him lengthen the path a little. There was room for a smoother turn, a cleaner fall, a last small flourish before the stream reached the cup.

Ram was still occupied with what he had been washing.

Vedant had decided that did not matter. In another moment Ram would see. The cup was there. The water was there. The skill was his.

It had seemed an almost complete account.

---

"Aap dekh rahe the ki unhone dekha ya nahi?" Rudra asked.

"Haan. Aur soch raha tha main paani dekh raha hoon."

Vedant stopped to move the chair a little. He did it with the unhurt hand, without making the movement part of the remembered performance. Rudra watched the present man settle again and found it easier to imagine that a younger version had once wanted praise badly enough to mistake wanting for attention.

He had known Vedant could be wrong about an ankle's timing on a page. That was a small correction between two people who agreed on what the correction was for. He had not often thought of the mentor before the assurance had settled into him, at a time when the opinion of the person watching could crowd out the actual work.

"Phir?" he asked.

"Unhone haath badhaaya."

---

Ram reached for the tumbler before the stream arrived.

There was nothing deceptive about the movement. He had asked for a drink. He was taking the cup he intended to drink from. Its position changed as an object changes when the person who needs it begins to use it.

Vedant's elegant curve went to the place where it had been.

The first water struck stone. A little splashed Ram's trouser hem. Vedant saw the miss, felt the hot stupidity of it and tried to correct the fall without first letting the stream stop.

That was the part he remembered most clearly: not the spill, but the wish to repair it quickly enough that the other man would never have to consider the mistake complete.

He pulled the curve round.

Now there was more water in motion near a person whose attention had been on receiving a drink. The younger Vedant could still tell himself he was fixing the first error. The water at his feet had already made another fact of the ground.

Ram set the tumbler down, away from the spreading wet.

"Roko," he said.

The command was not difficult to understand. It was difficult to obey before the demonstration looked right.

Vedant stopped the stream.

Some of it fell back into the basin. Some had already fallen elsewhere and remained there. The distinction had irritated him, though he would not have admitted it then. If he could gather the spill, make one clean movement and finish with the tumbler full, the attempt might still be remembered as a correction accomplished rather than an error seen.

He asked to try again.

Ram looked at the wet stone between them.

"Pehle yeh."

Vedant looked too. He had thought he was waiting to be told how to improve the curve. There was no curve now. There was a place where someone could put a foot without knowing that the ground had changed.

"Utha deta hoon," he said.

He meant the water, with the same skill which had put it there.

Ram asked how the arm felt.

The question was unwelcome because it had an answer. Holding the narrow stream while trying to correct it had left an ache which Vedant was pretending belonged only to concentration. He could still make another attempt. He was less certain that he could explain why another attempt was necessary before anyone was allowed to want a drink.

He answered about the ache.

Ram put the cloth down within reach.

It was a cloth. Not a sign that water had rejected him, not a lesser instrument awarded to a disappointing student. It had been folded before either of them knew there would be a spill. Somebody had expected that wet ground might need drying.

The younger Vedant hated how ordinary the answer was.

---

"Aapko laga sazaa hai?" Rudra asked.

"Laga tha."

"Thi?"

Vedant considered the question rather than borrowing certainty from the fact that he was older now.

"Us din? Nahi. Paani gira tha. Raasta sookhna tha. Par mujhe lag raha tha meri vidya chheen li."

He glanced towards the present glass. There was no water moving around it. Rudra did not find that lack disappointing. The room had enough things which needed placing carefully without a demonstration being added to them.

"Gilaas toh bhar sakte the," the boy said.

"Haath se."

The answer surprised him into a smile.

Vedant smiled back, briefly. The younger man in the memory had taken longer to discover the possibility.

---

Ram filled the tumbler from the basin without asking the water to do anything remarkable.

He drank, then put it where a hand could reach it again. The person who had asked for water had received it. That part of the task was finished, though it did nothing to repair Vedant's feeling about the attempt.

Vedant dried the wet place. The cloth did not make every edge dry at once. He had to look at the ground instead of at the man whose opinion he wanted to regain. He found a thin run of water behind the basin and another near the place where his own foot had been.

There had been more of the spill than his first ashamed glance had admitted.
