Ram moved the washing away from the remaining damp. He did not stand over the work reciting what the younger man ought to understand. That absence of a speech was almost more uncomfortable. Vedant could not prove he was learning by repeating the right words while failing to see the next wet patch.

When the way was dry enough to use, they left it alone.

There was no immediate second performance.

---

"Bas?" Rudra asked.

"Us waqt bas. Haath aaraam maang raha tha. Unka kaam bhi tha."

The fact had the ungenerous shape which real life sometimes gave to a lesson. The teacher did not exist only to wait beside the student's unfinished feeling. There had been food to clear away, washing to finish, people whose day was not an audience for the young man's progress.

"Agle din phir mauka mila?"

"Mila. Par us tak main jo soch raha tha, woh bhi kahaani ka hissa hai."

Vedant looked at him, not at the clock alone.

Rudra had begun holding his head in the position which made listening seem easier while the rest of him tired underneath it. He noticed when the mentor asked about comfort. The ankle wanted easing. The room, which had briefly contained the wet stone of another day, contained his own supported legs again.

"Yahin rokte hain," he said.

Vedant did not make the unspoken remainder precious by refusing to leave. He helped him call the staff member needed for the adjustment and waited until help had come. Then he left the boy to rest.

The old spill did not continue spreading because the account had been paused.

Rudra lay looking at the ceiling. He had asked when a teacher learned to stop. He had heard about a student who had first wanted very much not to.

It was an answer. The part he had not expected was how familiar that student had felt.

After the rest, the nurse helped him change into a clean shirt without making the protected arm do the work of getting through it.

The collar sat badly on one side. Rudra saw it in the dark face of the phone when it was put back within reach and thought of Dadi's promise to deal with it after he had seen the world. He could have asked for it to be straightened. For the moment he left it.

There were other things he wanted to put in order first.

He asked Sitara, when she had a free interval later, to carry a question to Vedant: could the account he had just heard be shared with the other three, or did he want it kept between them?

The answer returned with her. Vedant was willing to tell them himself at a time which fitted their care. Rudra did not have to become the sole keeper of a teacher's remembered embarrassment. Nor did he have to turn it into common property before asking.

He put the phone down.

He was less settled by the story than he had expected.

---

When Aadhya came for a brief visit after her own rest, she noticed him looking at the collar.

"Dadi ko mat bhejna," she said. "Photo."

"Woh bina photo ke jaan lengi."

"Phir bachne ka kya fayda."

She sat where the hands could remain supported. The joke left the room a little easier without requiring the thing underneath it to become easy too.

Rudra told her that he had heard something about his father as a teacher. Vedant would tell the actual account himself if they wanted to hear. He was not going to repeat it while the teller was absent.

What he wanted to say was about the discomfort it had left in him.

"Achhi baat thi," he said. "Sunke achha bhi laga. Phir gussa aaya ki achha kyun laga."

Aadhya did not answer at once. Her ordinary expression made room for an answer which had not been rehearsed around the correct way to comfort him.

"Achha laga toh jo bura laga tha, woh kat gaya?"

"Nahi."

"Toh?"

He looked towards the window. The little question was not an instruction to stop feeling the difficulty. It asked whether he was trying to make one true thing cancel another because carrying both was untidy.

"Lagta hai kisi ko choose karna padega," he said. "Kaunse waale Papa sahi hain."

"Dono mein se koi photo nahi hai na. Crop karke rakh do."

He laughed once, unwillingly, then went quiet.

The father who could give one student a useful lesson did not prove that the son had been wrong about the costs of his own childhood. The childhood did not mean every useful thing learned from the father had to be declared false. It was a harder arrangement than choosing one man and throwing the other version away.

He did not need to solve it while Aadhya's hands waited for their next treatment.

"Theek hai," he said. "Abhi dono."

"Abhi jitna hai."

She did not reach to fix the collar. After a while he asked the nurse for help with it, and Aadhya looked away to give the small task its privacy.

---

Later he returned to his own account of Pataal.

Not the account of the assisted movement. That had a remaining sentence which could wait until the next reading with Vedant. This was the part he wanted kept beside the photographs: what he could say about having been there, without demanding the pictures say it for him.

A short quiet interval was available. Sitara helped him put the words down and stopped when he needed the support changed. The account did not have to be long enough to justify the work of making it.

He named what was known. A threshold, worn smooth. An opening without a roof behind it. A way between broken fronts. The sound of air passing through hollow stone. Four friends who had taken different things from the same short line.

He left the unknowns in place.

He had not identified a household, found an heirloom or learned the view beyond the inspected turn. He could not say how every person in the realm had lived or where every survivor might be. Those absences did not have to be disguised as mysteries the stone had promised him a later right to solve.

Then he dictated what he had almost omitted.

"Mera Kul Asuran hai. Isliye yahaan aana mere liye alag tha. Par yahaan jo log rahe, woh sirf meri kahaani ke liye nahi rahe the."

Sitara read it back without making the word Kul louder than the rest.

The line did not command a future battle. It did not certify that he was now a different sort of body, cured of fear and injury by finding a word to stand behind. It was a fact of relation and an attempt to leave other lives their own size.

He asked for one final sentence beneath it.

"Main wahaan gaya tha. Laut aaya hoon. Rishta wahin chhodke nahi aaya."

When it was read to him, he did not ask for it to sound grander.

He asked for his name to be put with it: Rudra Vashisht. The name he had answered to before he knew where his father's family had come from. He did not have to leave that name on the Earth side of a passage in order to make the relation honest.

Sitara added it without a title. No handwriting was demanded from the braced wrist. Hearing his name beside the imperfect account was enough to make him responsible for having said it, not an authority on all the lives it could not describe.

Ira heard the account during a later quiet interval which she chose for listening.

Sitara read it with Rudra's permission. The two photographs stayed on his phone; Ira did not need to look at them again to certify that she had been present. She asked for the sentence about the return to be read once more.

Rudra watched the ordinary eye remain closed against the light. He was learning not to take the absence of a visible response as an absence of attention.

"Rakhna hai?" he asked.

"Tumhaare liye?"

"Apne paas. Mere naam se. Jo maine kaha."

She said she would keep a copy with his permission, beside her own qualified notes, not as a correction which replaced them. She would ask before passing it farther. Her name on a register would not make his words more certain than they were.

Then she asked why the chair had almost disappeared from his account.

He looked down at it.

The first reaction was irritation. He had spent the visit refusing to make the body less injured for the sake of being there. Must the first thing anyone learned about his relationship to a realm be how he had been helped across a lip?

Ira did not say it must.

"Bas kahaan se dekha, woh tumhaari baat ka hissa hai," she said. "Maine jahaan nahi dekha, woh meri baat ka hissa hai. Dono ko chhupaana kyun?"

He sat with the objection until it no longer sounded like pity.

They added that he had viewed the short line from the assisted chair, with the brief supported stand at the outlook. Not a medical inventory. A position in the world from which the seeing had happened.

The addition did not make the threshold less worn or the unknown lives smaller. It made the boy telling the account less likely to vanish behind a version of himself who had needed nothing.

---

Veer came when his own care allowed, in time to hear Rudra say that the account was finished for now.

"Bas?" he asked.

"Haan. Aaj ke liye."

"Achha hai. Socha ab poori sadak ka tender niklega."

Ira smiled without opening the eye. Rudra made a rude gesture with the good hand which remained safely brief and close to his lap.

The joke held a little of the relief he had not found a serious way to admit. Nothing had to be commissioned because they had reached the place. No next expedition had to be promised so the first could count. A relationship with lost people was not a contract for four recovering bodies to undertake every task left undone.

There would be questions later. They had not run out of things to learn. But the unknown beyond the turn could remain unknown through a meal, a treatment, a night.

Veer moved the aid closer before the knee became uncomfortable enough to make the reaching awkward. Ira said she wanted quiet soon. Rudra asked Sitara whether the copy could be made later rather than using the rest of the interval merely because a phone was available.

It could.

The three left the account where it was.

---

Before the next rest, Rudra asked whether the others wanted to hear Vedant's story when a suitable time could be found.

He gave only what he had permission to offer: an ordinary incident from when the mentor was learning from Ram, not a promised secret about any of their bloodlines. There had been water, a mistake and a useful reason to stop.

Veer wanted to hear it. Ira did too, if listening could be fitted around the eye's rest rather than made its replacement. Aadhya's answer came through Sitara after she was asked separately: yes, after her own care, without turning it into a practical test for the hands.

Those were not four people agreeing to a lesson because a name had appeared above them in a family chart.

They were different people choosing a question they could bring to a teacher.

Rudra realized he had imagined the story of a mentor as something received once one had become strong enough to deserve the rest of the training. This one had begun with a cup which the mentor failed to fill. There had been no demand that he stand unaided to hear the answer.

He wanted to know more about how a person learned to live after being seen making a small mistake, before every mistake had an audience large enough to turn it into a legend.

He could ask.

---

The account went to his parents later with his own permission and a plain message: this was as much as he could put into words now. Ira received the private copy he had offered her when she was ready to keep it. Neither sending included a request to make it public.

Rudra listened to the last sentence once more before the phone was put away.

He had gone. He had returned. The connection had not been left behind.

The words did not close Pataal around his father's past or his own new experience. They closed the need to keep proving that the first visit had actually happened.

For the first time since the journey began, he could let an account be finished without mistaking it for the end of the place.

In Varanasi, the story of the light was still being improved by people who had not agreed on what they had seen.

At a tea stall, a man said the golden colour meant the boy could no longer be hurt. Another asked why, if light settled such matters, their lane still needed a mason. The first said he was speaking of bigger things. The second pointed out that the hole outside his shop was big enough.

Someone remembered the tournament differently from both of them. Someone else supplied a result which belonged to another bout. The cups emptied while the accounts grew more assured.

A woman collecting tea for the people repairing a shutter waited through the argument and asked for the change.

The stallholder gave it to her. One coin had rolled beneath a bench; a child fetched it before the talk could make the missing amount symbolic.

There had been damage. There had been a tournament. People had seen an alarming colour and found explanations with more confidence than evidence. At that stall, none of the speaking men had a first-hand account of the recovering boy's present day.

The mason still needed paying. The shutter still needed its last hinge. The city kept a little of the light in its mouth and went on with work which light had not finished for it.

---

Elsewhere in Varanasi, Rukmini read her son's account after her own care.

She left the screen on the sentence about returning. Ram was resting, and she did not wake him to make the moment complete. The copy would be there when he wanted to read it.

For a while her fingers rested against her own pulse. She had not known, before he went, how often she would want an update he could not safely send from wherever he was. Now there were words made after his return, his chosen words, not a promise delivered to quiet her before leaving.

She let the phone rest beside her.

When Ram stirred later, she asked if he wanted the message now or after water. He asked for water first. She called for the help they needed, and the account waited without being lost.

---

In Mirzapur, Vedant came back for the short reading they had left unfinished.

Rudra had eaten. The support had been checked, and there was time before the next rest. The mentor asked whether that time was still available for this. Rudra said yes.

They finished the sentence about the last look.

The chair had been turned at the broad place. Rudra had not been expected to keep twisting to look behind him. The little change had let him see without spending a movement the injured body could not comfortably give.

Vedant read the whole practical account once more. It did not become an invitation to repeat the trip tomorrow. Rudra heard the parts which belonged to the people doing the assisting as well as the patient being assisted. He said it was accurate now.

The pen stopped moving.

For a moment both sheets lay beside each other: the plain account of needed help, and the boy's attempt to say what the place had meant. Neither could do the other's work. He was glad he had not tried to keep only the more beautiful one.

"Baaki log sunna chaahte hain," he told Vedant.

"Kal apne-apne kaam ke baad dekh lenge. Jitni der theek ho."

"Kahaani?"

"Kahaani. Haath se kuchh karwaa nahi rahe."

There was warmth in the answer. It left room for a roomful of students who were not ready to perform.

Rudra looked at the hands which had corrected the page. He had learned a little about the person before they became a mentor's hands in his mind. Not a hidden origin, not the answer to every old question about the man, but a younger person's wish to make an error disappear before anybody could see how much wanting had helped make it.

He recognized the wish.

---

"Aapko tab laga tha ki unhone jaldi rok diya?" he asked.

"Haan," Vedant said. "Baad mein jo samajh aaya, woh us waqt nahi aaya tha."

The honesty kept the younger man in the story alive. He had not stood on the wet ground already possessing the wisdom of the man who could tell it now. He had been cross, ashamed, eager to try again before the arm's ache became part of the account.

Rudra did not have to become a grateful student before he was permitted to say that stopping hurt his pride.

He looked from the finished line to the mentor.

The man had listened when the ankle's timing was wrong. He had left a sentence unfinished because the boy was tired, then returned when there was room to finish it. These were things Rudra had seen, not qualities he had to accept because someone older had been appointed above him.

But a useful story was not a promise that its teller could never repeat the mistake in another form. The student who wanted a beautiful result could grow into a teacher who wanted one too.

Rudra asked the question which remained when admiration had stopped doing the work of judgment.

"Aur aap galat ho rahe hon, toh aapko kaun rokega?"

# CHAPTER NINETY-EIGHT — BEFORE ANOTHER ATTEMPT

---

> *"We asked about the water he learned to hold.*
> *He told us what he put down before trying again."*
> *— Ira's later register, after Vedant's account*

---

"Sabse pehle main," Vedant said.

Rudra kept looking at him.

The mentor let the answer remain where it was before adding the part which prevented it from becoming an easy promise.

"Aur tum roko, toh sunna hoga. Par meri har galti pakadna tumhaara homework nahi hai."

Rudra smiled a little, then asked what happened if he could not find the words quickly enough. He did not mean only the cup in the story. Some instructions arrived inside a voice one had spent years learning to obey; understanding an objection and saying it were not always the same movement.

Vedant did not ask him to identify every voice he meant.

"Tumhara chup rehna meri jaanch khatam nahi karta," he said. "Mujhe dekhna hai ki kya maang raha hoon. Aur jiska kaam ilaaj hai, uski baat main apni class ke neeche nahi rakh sakta."

There would still be mistakes. He did not promise a room in which every person always knew enough to prevent them. He was saying that a teacher could not hand the work of remaining responsible to the person least free to interrupt him.

Rudra heard the difference.

"Aap bura maan gaye toh?"

"Maan bhi gaya toh pehle kaam rok sakta hoon. Bura lagna koi hukm nahi hai."

The answer was less comforting than a claim that Vedant would never feel offended. It was also harder to mistake for a claim of perfection. He could imagine a person having the feeling and still being required to choose what happened next.

He could imagine himself having to do the same.

"Sirf yeh boloon ki samajh nahi aa raha? Behtar tareeka na pata ho?"

"Toh pehle wahi sunenge. Sawaal poochhne ke liye mujhse zyaada jaanna zaroori nahi hai."

Rudra let the words settle. The student did not need to arrive with a complete replacement for the lesson before he could say something inside it was wrong for him. Not knowing yet could be the beginning of asking, rather than the reason he must remain quiet.

---

Vedant asked if the support remained comfortable and whether the call for the next care was within reach. Then he left when it was time to leave.

The boy did not test the promise by keeping him there.

He lay with the two finished accounts put aside, one about the help needed for the outing, one about the view and the people whose lives had existed before it broke. The questions which came after them did not have to reopen the first visit as unfinished business.

There were new things he wanted to ask the teachers. There were old things about his father which did not become simple because one teacher could tell a useful story about him. They could stay different things.

During the night the ankle began pressing against a fold which had been comfortable earlier. Rudra called before deciding how long he could tolerate it. A member of staff helped change the position, checked what needed checking and left him settled again.

He slept afterwards.

The answer to the evening's question did not heal the joint. It made asking for the ordinary adjustment a little less crowded by the idea that he ought to have managed alone.

---

On day eighty-four, the separate care came before any gathering.

Rudra's wrist and leg protections remained. Current examination and function allowed the supported movement already being used, not a longer walk awarded for a conversation well heard. Veer had his own shoulder, ribs and knee reviewed and his own limit on the move to another room. Aadhya's hands still needed their treatment and protection; Ira's left eye remained covered and the ordinary eye's use stayed a matter of comfort and the treating plan, not how interesting the next story might be.

The proposed listening could be short and could end early. There would be no task to perform while listening, no water moved by a student and no demonstration to make pain worth mentioning.

Each was asked again whether the arrangement still worked.

Ira wanted the shaded place and no expectation that she look at the teller throughout. Aadhya wanted the hands supported rather than a notebook placed in them. Veer wanted the aid and a place to sit before anybody began. Rudra needed help with the chair and the transfer.

The arrangements were made as arrangements, not interpreted as four apologies for coming.

---

Before the appointed interval, Vedant checked that the room was free for the agreed time and that nobody else's care had been moved aside to provide him an audience.

He had his own forearm comfortably supported. He did not bring a basin. The drinking water already in the room remained drinking water, available to anyone who wanted it without having to become material for a lesson first.

Sitara helped with the settling and would remain within call rather than occupy every place needed by the people listening. Hospital staff did the assistance which required them. Veer was not recruited to lift the chair. The attendant from the passage had not been summoned back merely because the four were together again.

Rudra looked at the ordinary room when he arrived: chairs, a table with a faint ring where a wet glass had been, light cut into a softer shape by the curtain. There was nothing in it which required a power to begin.

For once, knowing that did not make him feel they had come to the lesser part of the training.

Veer was sitting before the story began.

It ought not to have felt like a contribution. The seat was there because the knee needed it; the aid was close because getting up later would require it. Still, he caught himself watching the other arrivals for some work he could take from them before he noticed that the necessary work had already been given to people able to do it.

He kept the place he had been helped to reach.

Rudra told them the question he had chosen to ask the evening before. Not only when Vedant learned to stop, but who would stop the mentor when the mentor was wrong.

"Meri taraf se jawaab abhi bhi wahi hai," Vedant said. "Apni zimmedaari tum par nahi daal sakta."

Then he told the story from the beginning, so nobody had to borrow a friend's partial memory to follow it.

---

There had been no flood.

Veer found himself almost disappointed by that, then amused by the disappointment. Vedant was describing a basin after a meal. Ram wanted water to drink. The younger man wanted his already-practised skill to be noticed.

The curve was elegant. The cup moved because a person reached for it. The water continued towards a position which had become empty.

The simplicity made the error more difficult to put away as something which belonged only to an inexperienced fool. Veer knew the urge to solve a problem before the person inside it had finished moving. He knew how certain a body could feel about a response which another body had not asked for.

Vedant spoke about the attempted correction, the growing wet place, the stop he did not want and the ache he had hoped another attempt would let him forget to mention.

He did not add danger to make the memory worth their time. The wet ground was enough. So was the person who had asked for a drink and still needed one while the display was being repaired.

"Unhone jaan-boojhke gilaas hataaya tha?" Aadhya asked.

"Peene ke liye uthaaya tha. Usko meri pareeksha banaane ka mann mera tha."

She nodded. The distinction seemed to matter to her.

Ira asked whether the ground had been wet before he began.

Part had. The washing had made it so. His spill had carried water to a place previously dry, and he had first looked only at the obvious splash, not at the thin run near his own foot. The cloth had made him pay attention to what had actually changed.

Veer looked at the faint ring on the table. No lesson was concealed in it for him to discover. Someone had put a damp glass there. He could let that be the whole fact instead of turning every ordinary trace into an examiner's trick.

---

Vedant told them that Ram had filled the tumbler by hand.

Veer laughed softly. It had not occurred to him either, though the ordinary answer had been available from the beginning of the story.

"Pehle hi kar dete," he said.

"Main? Kar sakta tha. Par phir kaun dekhta ki paani chalaa sakta hoon."

There was no self-disgust in the older man's voice. He had not made the younger one ridiculous in order to prove how far above him he now stood. He described a desire which still sounded human, including in a room where four people had reasons to want their own bodies to become impressive again.

Veer shifted to ease the shoulder and did not make the movement discreet enough to become painful.

He had wanted to hear where the mentor's assurance came from. He was hearing about a time before it could protect the mentor from the embarrassment of wanting praise.

That was not the same as hearing that assurance was false.

---

When the first part was finished, Ira asked if the second attempt had succeeded.

Vedant said he had asked himself the same question before the next day began. He had imagined the better arc of water. He had made the receiving hand still in his imagination, the cup exactly where it should be, the teacher looking at the right moment.

"Is baar sabko aapki tasveer mein rehna tha," she said.

"Haan. Sabse pehle apne aapko. Haath kaisa hai, woh bhi tay kar liya tha."

Rudra was quiet beside the window. Veer did not know every part of what his friend was thinking, and the account did not demand that he guess. He had enough of his own response to attend to.

"Phir gaye?" he asked.

"Gaya."

"Paani uthaaya?"

"Usse pehle kehna pada ki haath abhi bhi dukh raha hai."

The sentence altered the imagined next day. Veer had been waiting for a performance repaired. The person who went back had taken the same arm with him.

He wanted to hear what became possible once that fact was allowed to arrive before the water.

The younger Vedant had gone back with the improvement already arranged in his head.

He knew where he would stand. He knew the point at which the curve would narrow, the shorter path it would take, the quicker end which would prevent the cup from moving beyond it. The imagined attempt had no ache in it. He had placed that fact outside the picture as carefully as he had placed the tumbler inside.

Then Ram asked how the arm was.

For a moment he considered saying it was better. It was, a little. Better could be made to sound like enough if he spoke quickly and began before the next question.

He remembered the wet ground near his own foot.

"Abhi dukh raha hai," he said.

Ram put aside what he had been doing and asked what he had noticed overnight. Vedant told him. There was no new trial with the water that day. The arm could be rested and attended to; an exercise was not the proper means of finding out how much hurt could be concealed inside a successful result.

Vedant had returned ready to repair a demonstration. Instead he found himself sitting with the fact which the demonstration had been meant to cover.

---

"Aap naraaz hue?" Aadhya asked.

"Apne aap se. Unse bhi. Us waqt farq nahi kar raha tha."

"Bola?"

"Pehle nahi. Phir keh diya ki agar har baar rukenge toh seekhenge kab."

Veer felt the question find a familiar place. He had asked versions of it without always using words. If the body could not be trusted today, when did trusting it begin? If another person could do the work more safely, when did a person become useful enough not to be asked to sit?

Vedant said Ram had asked what he thought there was to learn from the previous day.

"Sahi jagah paani giraana," the younger man had answered.

It had been the obvious thing. He had missed. The mistake needed better aim. He wanted to be allowed to work on the skill rather than be congratulated for talking about the ache which prevented work.

Ram asked whether the person receiving the water had become less thirsty because the curve was beautiful.

The question annoyed him. Of course not. He knew that. He was not a child who needed the use of a cup explained.

What he had not wanted to know was how completely the receiver had become scenery inside an attempt made for the receiver.

He had put the person's hand where it needed to be for him to succeed. He had made the ground dry by forgetting the spill. He had made the arm ready by giving the imagined arm no answer of its own.

There was no better curve which could correct all that before he looked at it.

---

"Toh us din baat hi hui?" Ira asked.

"Hui. Aur paani haath se diya gaya. Jise peena tha usne piya."

"Aapne kya kiya?"

"Pehle poochha ki baith sakta hoon ya nahi. Khada rehke zyaada yogya dikhne ki koshish kar raha tha."

This time the four laughed together.

Vedant let them. The joke belonged to the younger man's stubbornness, not to an arm which had hurt. He had been allowed to sit. The disappointment did not vanish as soon as the correct words were spoken. He had stayed disappointed and listened anyway.

There had been no grand declaration that praise no longer mattered to him. He still wanted to make the water do what he intended. He still wanted the person teaching him to see and be pleased. Those desires could remain without being permitted to decide whether the arm was ready.

"Agli baar kab hua?" Veer asked.

Vedant said the next practice came after rest and after the arm was fit for the effort again. He did not supply a miraculous interval or turn the return to practice into a new name for a power.

Before beginning, he had asked whether the other person was ready to receive. That had felt embarrassingly small beside the skill he had hoped to display.

It had been useful.

---

Veer looked at his own aid.

He had heard a story with no dead friend inside it, no last command from a battlefield, no hidden inheritance which made the teacher's present ability inevitable. The person telling it had learned something before disaster made learning dramatic.

That did not make the learning less available to those who had already been hurt.

"Aapko ab bhi prabhaavit karna hota hai?" he asked.

"Hota hai," Vedant said. "Kabhi tum logon ko bhi."

Veer had not expected the last part. The room grew quieter around it.

"Isliye pehle waala sawaal zaroori tha," the mentor added. "Main achha dikhna chaahoon, uska kharcha tumhare haath-pair kyun dein?"

Rudra was looking at him now. Aadhya's hands remained on their supports. Ira listened without reaching for a notebook or opening the eye to reward the confession with attention she had not offered.

The story had brought them to a man who could still want the wrong kind of success.

It had not left them alone with the responsibility for giving it to him.

Veer asked for water.

It was not a way of extending the story. His mouth was dry. Vedant reached for the glass with the unhurt hand and began to put it where Veer could see it clearly on the table.

Then he stopped.

Veer had started to lean. The glass was visible, but the comfortable reach was on the other side, closer to the hand he could use without drawing the shoulder into the movement. Vedant had noticed the body preparing to accommodate his placement.

He moved the glass nearer the right place before Veer had to finish the reach. Then he asked whether that position worked or whether help was needed.

Veer gave the answer. The water reached him without becoming a task in which he had to prove the injured shoulder was less troublesome than expected.

"Yeh bhi plan tha?" Ira asked.

"Nahi," Vedant said. "Jahaan mujhe seedha laga, wahaan rakh raha tha. Uske haath se nahi dekha."

He did not make the small correction into a second story about his wisdom. Nor did he call it too small to admit because the first account had just made him sound careful. He had put a glass down badly and changed it when he saw.

Veer drank.

The useful part was not that somebody had nearly made a mistake for him to catch. It was that he had not been required to pay for the mistake with a painful reach while the older person finished looking right.

---

Aadhya asked whether, when they were ready to practise again, asking for a pause would end the lesson.

"Kabhi us din ke liye khatam ho sakti hai," Vedant said. "Jitna sach hai, utna hi."

He would not promise that the body always got to stop for one minute and return unchanged to everything it wanted. Sometimes stopping meant treatment, reassessment, another day. That was not a punishment the teacher could remove by agreeing to call it a break instead.

"Par phir mauka milega ya nahi, woh dard chhupaane ka inaam nahi hoga," he added.

Aadhya looked at her protected hands. She did not offer them for examination by the story. Their treating people already had that work, and the next permitted effort would need their actual answer.

Veer understood why she had asked. He had heard the question; he did not have to decide what every past silence of hers had meant.

Ira asked if they could keep their own account of what they had heard, without trying to take down every word before the interval ended.

Vedant agreed. He would rather answer a later question about what he meant than let an inaccurate sentence remain because somebody had written it neatly. Their records could name him as the teller of his memory, not turn a remembered cup into independent proof of every part of his life.

The story gave them something to ask about. It did not give them access to the things he had not told.

---

When the staff member came for the care which was due, Vedant stopped speaking.

He did not say there was only one more point. Veer had already been collecting a question about the next practice in his head; he let it go back to being a question, not a reason for Aadhya to wait beyond the time arranged for her hands.

The others had their own limits. Ira wanted the eye's next quiet interval. Rudra needed the position changed before returning to his room. Veer's knee had begun asking for the movement which the chair allowed but prolonged sitting did not.

He reported it before standing.

There was help where needed. The staff member checked the transfer. The aid went to the place chosen for it rather than being left on the table because conversation had made the table seem more important than the way out.

Veer rose within the effort permitted that day. He did not offer the shoulder to Rudra on his way past. Rudra had assistance already; the friendship did not need an extra injured person inserted between the helper and the chair to make it visible.

"Baad mein," Veer told him.

"Haan."

It was an ordinary goodbye between people who expected to remain part of one another's day without occupying every interval of it.

---

After his own care, Veer lay with the aid in reach and thought about the receiving hand.

He had spent a long time imagining protection from the side of the person giving it. The body which rose, held, caught, stayed. He still wanted to be that person when he could. The story had not asked him to stop wanting.

But the receiver could move. The receiver could need the cup elsewhere, the help later, the voice quieter. Being helped was not the empty place into which another person's idea of usefulness could be poured.

He knew that now from the side of a shoulder which had not been asked to hurt for the sake of accepting a drink.

When the nurse asked whether the support needed changing, he said yes and showed her where.

It was not the opposite of learning to protect someone.

The afternoon went on without becoming a continuation of the class.

Veer had food brought when he was ready for it, asked for the thing which was awkward to be moved closer and ate before it went cold. There were parts of the day which had begun to feel available only after he had made them into proof of improvement. He tried letting the meal be a meal.

Later he sent Rudra a short message asking how he was.

The answer came after a while: settled, tired, no new revelation from the ceiling. Veer replied that he would report if his own ceiling became more forthcoming. Neither boy treated the delay as evidence that a friend had gone somewhere dangerous without him.

Ira was resting. Aadhya had finished the treatment which had ended their gathering and wanted her own quiet. The four had heard one story. They did not have to keep discussing it until every private response was agreed.

There would be time for questions when attention did not have to be borrowed from care.

---

Near evening, Dhruv came to ask whether Veer wanted company for a little while.

He had two bananas in a small bag. He offered one, did not put it into a hand already occupied with getting comfortable and accepted that Veer wanted it later. The bag went where it could be reached with help if needed. Nothing had to be peeled immediately to prove hospitality had been received.

"Kya dekh rahe the?" Dhruv asked.

Veer had been looking at the aid.

"Yahi. Abhi tak taare nahi uge isme."

Dhruv sat down with the tender hand in an easy position.

"Achha hai. Raat ko dhoondhna mushkil ho jaata."

Veer laughed. The humour took no strength away from the man. That still surprised him sometimes, how little an older person had to lose by not requiring every sentence in the room to protect his dignity.

They sat through the sound of a trolley passing outside. A staff member checked something at the doorway, found that no help was being requested just then and went on. The room did not close around teacher and student as though the rest of the day had been dismissed.

---

Veer said he had been thinking about being helped.

Not because he had discovered he liked needing it. There were still moments when another person's approach made the body prepare to refuse before it knew what was being offered. He disliked how much care had to be organized around a shoulder, a knee, the space in which he could sit without either becoming an argument.

Dhruv did not tell him the feeling was ungrateful.

"Aur?" he asked.

"Kabhi lagta hai jiske liye kuchh karna hai, usse poochhna bhool jaata hoon."

He could say that much about himself without giving away another person's memory in order to explain it. The story he had heard was available for thought, not a borrowed authority which had to accompany every sentence he made.

Dhruv looked towards the aid and back.

"Aaj kisi ko poochha?"

Veer thought of the nurse adjusting the support, of telling her where, of not offering the damaged shoulder to a boy whose transfer already had help.

"Apne liye bola."

"Woh bhi shuruat hai."

It did not sound like congratulations for becoming harmless. Veer still wanted the part of himself which could rise when someone needed protecting. He did not want recovery to mean learning a polished language in which every dangerous choice was refused before the people inside it could ask for help.

But protecting a person and occupying the person's choices were different things. He had been allowed to notice the difference from the chair, while there was time to think before action narrowed the room.

He would not always have that time.

That made using it now more important, not less.

---

Dhruv asked if he should leave before Veer became tired of company.

Veer checked the actual answer. The knee was settled. The shoulder needed no new position yet. He wanted a few more minutes, not a long lesson.

He said so.

Dhruv remained without taking the invitation as permission to begin training. The surveyor's ring lay still on its cord. There was no star-light answering it in the room, no new reading laid over the injured body.

Veer remembered arriving at the akhada beyond Machli-ghat, expecting to be measured by whether he could win. He remembered the labour of getting up, the attention given to returning, the strange insult of discovering that his most certain idea of usefulness had not been the whole lesson.

Those were things he had actually lived. He could remember them without knowing all the life which had made the man beside him choose to teach that way.

He had once wanted the rest of the answer to concern a stronger stance.

Now he wondered about the person who had decided that a student needed a way back before he needed another victory.

The question did not ask the tender hand to demonstrate anything. It asked its owner for a beginning he could choose to give.

"Aapne humein jeetne se pehle lautna kyun sikhaaya tha?"

# CHAPTER NINETY-NINE — THE STAR WAS STILL THERE

---

> *"The sky in his account was clear.*
> *For a long time we had mistaken that for a clear way back."*
> *— Ira's later register, beside Dhruv's first account*

---

"Kyunki jeet tumhe uthaakar waapas nahi laati," Dhruv said.

Veer looked at the aid beside him.

The answer did not diminish winning. He had expected, perhaps, to be told that he had misunderstood it, that the things he had wanted from the akhada were childish and the wiser man had always known which desires to replace. Dhruv let the desire remain.

"Jeetna seekhna hai," he said. "Par uske baad bhi shareer tumhaara hi rehta hai. Thaka hua ho sakta hai. Galat raaste pe jaa sakta hai. Jiske liye khade hue, uske paas pahunchna bhi baaki ho sakta hai."

Veer thought of the last time he had stood before a fall he could not undo, then of sitting while someone brought help which he could not provide. Victory was not the only thing which failed to carry a person out of a place. Shame could keep him there too.

"Aapko pehle se pata tha?"

"Baat pata thi. Apne baare mein der se samajh aayi."

Dhruv shifted the tender hand to an easier position. The ring on its cord remained against his shirt. He did not lift it to make the answer more difficult to doubt.

He said that the akhada's teaching of return had existed before his own lessons. He had not invented it on the night he learned to take it personally. A person could repeat an old instruction for years while quietly assuming that its most necessary part applied to someone else.

Veer knew something of that.

"Ek raat main ek seekhne waale ko aasman samjha raha tha," Dhruv said. "Raasta mujhe saaf dikh raha tha. Maine samajh liya usse bhi dikh raha hoga."

"Phir?"

"Woh ruka. Maine suna. Dono waapas aaye. Koi badi durghatna nahi hui. Par meri baat utni poori nahi thi jitni mujhe lag rahi thi."

There was no dead person placed between them to give the lesson authority. No vanished student whose name Veer would now have to earn by enduring the rest of the story. There was a teacher who had failed to distinguish his own familiarity from the other person's knowledge.

Veer wanted to hear it.

He also noticed that the shoulder had begun asking him to move. The few minutes he had requested were becoming the time in which he would have to pretend not to notice.

"Baaki kal?" he asked.

"Kal jab theek ho."

Dhruv stood without calling the pause an interruption. He asked whether Veer needed the bag moved before he went. It was moved. Then the teacher left the room with his own hand protected, not with a disappointed look offered as the student's last task of the evening.

---

The nurse helped Veer settle the shoulder and checked the knee before the next rest.

He asked for the banana later, when he was comfortable enough to want it. The peel went into the bin with help; the fruit tasted slightly greener at one end. It was so stubbornly a banana that he smiled at himself for the feeling of relief.

Not every offering from a teacher had to turn into the instrument of a lesson.

He sent Rudra a goodnight message without forwarding Dhruv's unfinished account. The answer came before the phone was put away. Ira and Aadhya would have their own evenings; no one was called merely to make the four part of the same moment.

During the night, the knee needed a position changed. Veer reported the pull and let the person helping examine what had shifted. He did not try the old rising movement to see whether the conversation had made the body more ready for it.

He slept after the support was settled.

---

On day eighty-five, the first questions concerned the night.

The clinician wanted the actual account of the shoulder, ribs and knee. Veer gave it, including the discomfort during the visit and the position which had helped. The therapist considered what he could try with the aid, what the shoulder should not be asked to bear and where the short movement would end.

Veer had a private picture of that movement already: leaving the bed, making the distance neatly, turning without the awkward pause. It was almost funny to recognize the tidiness of it before anybody had begun.

He let the picture go far enough to hear the instructions.

The seat at the other end was placed before he rose. There was help beside him, not a test of whether he could make it unnecessary. His aid was where the permitted hand could find it. The shoulder remained out of work which belonged to the people assisting.

When he was asked whether he was ready, he checked the answer rather than the audience.

"Haan," he said. "Abhi."

The first part went as planned.

Veer rose with the help arranged, found the aid and made the short permitted distance without putting the shoulder into the work. At the turn, the knee began to complain before the rest of him was ready to call the movement finished.

He said so.

The therapist stopped him where the ground was clear, checked the position and helped him take the shorter turn towards the waiting seat. There was no fall. There was also no excuse to describe the knee as comfortable merely because he had remained upright.

He sat, let the leg settle and answered the next questions.

"Poora nahi hua," he said.

The therapist asked which part he meant. They had found where today's effort needed to stop, used the seat which had been placed for precisely that possibility and avoided making the shoulder pay for a longer route. That was part of the trial, not an event outside it.

Veer wanted to argue and could not find an honest version of the argument. The destination had been drawn differently in his head. That did not make the help at this one a failure.

The return to his room was assisted. The limits stayed in the plan. He rested before the next conversation.

---

Dhruv came only after Veer had eaten and had time to lie quietly.

The shoulder was settled, the knee supported. Veer wanted the account now, not a chance to reproduce it. Dhruv asked whether the time still worked and sat where the light did not require the boy to keep turning his head.

"Kahaan the aap?" Veer asked.

"Yahin ki zameen. Akhade ke paas. Kisi aur duniya ki baat nahi hai."

He described an earlier teaching night, without a date or a name to make it grand. A learner, a place already used for practice, the open ground beside the trees. Nothing was hunting them. The task had been ordinary for Dhruv, and he had mistaken ordinary for simple.

He could read the sky from that ground. He knew where the tree canopy broke, where the packed earth became softer and which turn brought a person back beside the practice space. He had walked it often enough that the turns had ceased to feel like information.

What he intended to teach was attention to a reference which did not move merely because the learner became uncertain.

The trouble was not that the reference failed.

---

He had taken the learner out from the practice ground and stopped where a star was clear between the upper branches.

No star was named. The name would not have helped a person who first needed to find what the teacher was looking at. Dhruv described the opening in the leaves, the bright point within it and the relation of that opening to the place where they were standing.

The other person answered that he understood.

Dhruv remembered being satisfied by the answer. The words came back in the right order. The head turned where he pointed. He had asked what was visible and heard a description close enough to his own that he allowed the difference to disappear inside it.

Then he changed position.

From the few steps beside him, the learner's opening in the canopy was not the same opening. A lower branch cut across it. The packed edge of the practice space was less obvious in the dark than Dhruv's familiar feet had made it.

Dhruv knew where both stood. The learner knew where Dhruv stood.

He had not asked whether those were the same kind of knowledge.

---

"Usne bola nahi?" Veer asked.

"Pehle wahi bola jo maine poochha."

"Aur jo aapne nahi poochha?"

Dhruv looked at him with a small, rueful pleasure. Not praise for guessing the hidden answer, but recognition that the question had found the actual difficulty.

"Uski baari der se aayi. Meri wajah se."

The admission did not make the whole night a record of cruelty. Veer could imagine an instruction given too confidently, a reply heard too generously, a student reluctant to turn an ordinary exercise into a declaration that he could not do it. None of those things required a villain. Together they could still make a person less safe.

"Aapko laga woh darr raha hai?"

"Baad mein usne kaha ki galat bolne se bach raha tha. Usse pehle main kaise jaan leta. Tab toh mujhe lag raha tha samajh gaya."

Veer heard the limit left inside the account. Dhruv could tell what he had thought; he could tell what the learner later said. He could not improve the past by pretending to have understood the other person's hesitation all along.

---

The old instruction had been to turn back towards the starting place.

Dhruv was close enough to help, far enough that the boy would have to choose the turn rather than merely follow his heels. It was not a journey into unknown ground. It was a little movement inside a place the teacher thought he knew completely.

He saw the learner turn the wrong way, hesitate and look up again.

The star was still there.

That was what had made Dhruv slow to understand. There was no cloud to blame, no sky closing over them, no broken instrument or failed power. The reference had remained clear from the place where he stood.

For a moment he almost repeated the instruction more loudly.

Instead, he heard the question the learner finally asked.

It was not about the star.

"Aap yahaan aake dikhaa sakte hain?"

Dhruv gave the remembered question in his own present voice. There was no attempt to make the learner's younger voice return to the room. He could remember the request without pretending that remembering preserved every sound of it.

The question had been plain.

Not a better star. Not the name of the thing above them. The person on the ground wanted the teacher to come to the place where the instruction had stopped being useful.

Dhruv went.

---

He crossed the few steps by the ground he knew, slowly enough that the other person did not have to move aside in a hurry to make room. He stood beside the learner and looked from there.

The branch which had barely interrupted his own view cut the opening into two. The bright point could still be found, but the dry, hard edge of the practice ground lay behind a darker patch which he had been treating as obvious. The learner could hear where he had stood before. That did not make the foot's next place visible.

"Yeh wala mod?" the learner asked.

Dhruv had pointed to a turn which was plain from the other side. From here, two small variations in the ground could answer the pointing hand. One led where he intended. The other ended against the spread of roots.

There was time to stand and understand the difference because the learner had stopped rather than guess a movement to keep the lesson looking successful.

Dhruv had nearly made that stop into evidence that more instruction was needed.

It was more looking which had been needed first.

---

"Aapne uske saath chalke waapas dikhaaya?" Veer asked.

"Haan. Apne pairon se. Koi ishaara karke zameen seedhi nahi kar di."

He said it without an impressive account of how little help had ultimately been required. They had named the change in the ground in ordinary words. They had made the turn together, stopped where its appearance changed from the other side and looked back from there too.

Nobody was asked to run the route to prove that fear had been removed.

At the practice ground they sat. The learner had been able to describe the missing part of the instruction once describing it no longer meant competing with the teacher's assurance that it was clear.

Dhruv had listened.

Then he ended the exercise for that night.

It would have been easy to ask for one neat return after the correction. The learner would probably have tried. Dhruv could no longer be certain what a successful-looking attempt would have concealed about the wish not to disappoint him.

They were both back. That was not the moment to make coming back insufficient.

---

Veer looked towards the doorway of the room.

He knew where the corridor ran. He had been helped along it, seen the turn, heard the trolley at the uneven join. If somebody now asked where the nearer seat was, he could point to it confidently.

He did not know what that same stretch looked like from Ira's rested eye, how much of it Aadhya could use without a protected hand reaching for support, which turn pulled at Rudra's ankle. He could not improve those limits by knowing the destination more firmly.

The thought did not require him to become frightened of helping anyone move. It required a question before he decided whose movement he was describing.

"Taara sahi tha," Dhruv said. "Meri jagah galat thi."

Veer heard the care inside the difference. The man was not claiming the sky had betrayed him, or that the student had failed to deserve an instruction which should have been enough. He had stood where knowing had become so familiar that he no longer noticed what he knew.

"Gussa aaya tha?" Veer asked.

"Pehle. Phir samajh aaya kis pe nikaalne jaa raha tha."

"Bola nahi?"

"Baat rok di. Baad mein kehna pada ki jo saaf bol raha tha, uska aadha uski taraf se dekha hi nahi."

That did not make him a teacher without pride. It made pride something which had not been allowed to finish the learner's sentence for him.

---

The staff member who came to check Veer's comfort did not find a pupil practising in the dark.

She found a man seated beside a supported patient, both talking quietly. Dhruv made room for the actual check. The story could wait through a question about the knee.

Veer reported that it had settled after the morning's shorter movement but was beginning to stiffen in this position. The support was adjusted. He asked whether there was time for the end of the account before the next rest and heard the answer before deciding he wanted it.

There was a little time. There was no need to fill all of it.

When the nurse had gone on, Veer looked at the teacher's hand resting without display.

"Aap us raat haare nahi," he said.

"Usne bhi nahi."

Veer had not realized that he had left the learner outside his sentence until Dhruv put him back inside it.

Veer asked whether walking back beside the learner had made it harder for him to try alone later.

The question had been waiting beneath the rest. If someone came every time a person stopped, perhaps stopping became a way of never finding out whether he could go on. He disliked the suspicion in that thought. Disliking it did not prevent him from having it.

Dhruv did not make him dress it in kinder words before answering.

"Us raat ki madad ko uski poori zindagi ka faisla kyun banaate?" he asked.

They had needed to get back safely. A later lesson could ask what the learner now knew, what still needed practice and what help remained necessary. It could not honestly begin by pretending that receiving help once had settled all those questions forever.

"Har baar saath chalna padta toh?"

"Toh dekhna padta kis kaam ke liye taiyaar hai. Akela bhejne se taiyaar ho jaana pakka nahi hota."

He was not promising that teaching could remove every risk. Veer had not asked for such a promise and would not have believed it. He was refusing to use risk as proof that the person exposed to it had finally earned trust.

That was a different refusal.

---

Dhruv said the old account could be told to the other three if they wanted to hear. He would rather answer their questions himself than have Veer preserve every remembered word while lying with a knee which needed another position.

Veer was relieved. It had not occurred to him how quickly a story could become another thing he was responsible for carrying correctly.

"Poochh loonga," he said.

He did not ask the teacher to remain until he had completed the asking. Dhruv had his own hand to attend to and his own rest. He left the room when the conversation had used the time they had agreed.

Veer lay quietly afterwards.

At first he tried to repeat the whole story in his head. The star, the opening in the branches, the turn, the question. He lost the exact order of two sentences and caught himself treating the loss as evidence that he had not listened well enough to deserve the answer.

Then he remembered the man coming to stand beside the learner.

He could ask again. That had been part of what the story was for.

---

Rudra's next message asked how the morning's movement had gone.

Veer replied with the actual result: shorter than he had imagined, knee objected at the turn, no fall, settled after help. He almost added that it was nothing to worry about. He could not know what another person should feel, and the details were more useful than the instruction.

The answer came back that Rudra's own day had stayed within the existing help and protection. No new walking award for being attentive to a teacher. He wanted to know whether the chair at the turn had been comfortable.

"Tere waali shaan nahi thi," Veer sent.

"Upgrade kara denge. Cushion par naam likhwaana hai?"

Veer smiled at the screen. The joke did not ask the boys to pretend the chairs were not needed. It made room for them to dislike a cushion without making the dislike a crisis of identity.

He said Dhruv was willing to tell an ordinary old teaching account later, if the others wanted to hear it. He did not send the story itself in pieces. Rudra wanted to listen when his care allowed.

Aadhya and Ira were asked separately through Sitara. Aadhya said yes, provided there was no practical exercise hidden behind it. Ira asked that any listening fit around the rest already planned for the ordinary eye. Sitara brought those answers, not a declaration that the group had become ready for the next phase of training.

The actual time would still have to be arranged.

---

In the late afternoon, Veer looked towards the corridor when somebody asked for help finding the right room.

His first impulse was to point before the question had finished. He knew the near turn. He had passed it often enough now to want a small use for that knowledge.

He waited.

The visitor was asking about a room on another part of the floor. The nurse gave the directions, noticed that the visitor had not understood the last turn and asked a passing colleague to show the way when she went there.

No teacher was performing. No student was watching for a lesson. A person with a bag and a worried face was allowed not to understand a corridor immediately.

Veer watched the two leave together.

He had not been made useless by failing to become the first person with an answer. He had simply not been the person who knew enough about that particular destination.

When the time came to change his own position, he used the call within reach instead of trying to demonstrate that he had learned the building by moving through it alone.

The aid remained beside him, ready for the next effort his body was actually allowed to make.

Evening put a narrow orange edge on the window frame.

Veer could see it without lifting his head far. Beyond it, the road was losing its separate colours. A horn sounded, stopped, sounded again with less conviction. Somebody outside the room was explaining that a parcel had arrived at the right building and the wrong entrance, as though the building had done this deliberately.

He listened until the explanation became laughter.

The day had not given him the distance he had pictured walking. It had given him a shorter turn, a seat which mattered, a teacher's mistake and the irritating discovery that these did not fit neatly into either progress or failure.

He had wanted a clearer answer from the knee.

The knee remained a knee. It would have to be examined again when the next effort was considered, not persuaded by the quality of the thoughts he had managed to have while resting it.

He asked for the light to be changed before it became uncomfortable to keep looking towards the window. Then he ate what had been brought, slowly enough to notice when he wanted a pause.

---

Later, after the next care, he found one point of light through the space above the lower frame.

He could not see the ground below the window from this position. The sill concealed it. He knew the road existed because he had seen it from elsewhere and heard it now; the small piece of sky supplied almost nothing about where a foot might safely go.

He lay with the two facts together.

He was not practising a star lesson. There was no attempt to hold a form, no command sent towards the light, no count which would turn looking into a permission. The aid remained beside the bed. His shoulder remained supported.

Still, he understood the old error more sharply from here. A thing could be plainly visible and not tell a person how to get where another person wanted him to go.

The light outside did not have to change for that to be true.

He thought of the learner asking the teacher to come closer. It was not a clever answer in a contest. It was an ordinary request which had interrupted the teacher's picture before the picture could become more important than the ground.

Veer had been praised often for knowing when to step forward.

He wanted to learn that other timing too.

---

Sitara brought a possible interval for the shared listening the next day, after the separate care, if the morning's reviews still allowed the move and the attention.

The room would be available for a short time. Ira could have the shaded place. Aadhya would not be asked to hold a book or repeat a hand movement. The boys would have their aids, supports and the ordinary assistance needed to reach their seats. No one was booking a night outside, reopening the akhada's exercises or proposing another passage crossing.

Veer said the arrangement sounded useful. His actual answer about attending could follow the review rather than be treated as a promise which the body must now honour.

Sitara accepted that and went to the next person who needed asking.

He noticed how much quieter an arrangement became when everyone did not have to make the same answer at the same moment.

---

Dhruv returned briefly before the last rest, after the care he had needed for his own hand.

He asked whether Veer had any question left from the account which needed clearing up now. Veer said he had understood what happened. The learner had asked, the teacher had gone to him, they had looked from the other side and walked back together. The part he was still thinking about came afterwards.

"Abhi nahi toh kal," he said.

"Poochh toh sakte ho. Lambi baat hui toh rok lenge."

Veer smiled at the wording. A request could be heard without automatically buying the rest of somebody's evening.

He asked whether Dhruv remembered being satisfied when the learner had reached the practice ground again.

"Hua tha," Dhruv said. "Phir samajh aaya ki sirf mujhe zaroorat padne pe saath chal dena kaafi nahi hoga."

Veer looked at him. The teacher had not withdrawn the value of helping. He was noticing that the next lesson could not depend on the learner discovering the same missing instruction and having to ask for it again.

---

The ring lay quiet against Dhruv's shirt. The tender hand was not raised to demonstrate a corrected way of pointing.

Veer had expected the account to end when both people returned. Now he saw why return was not merely an ending. A person brought something back: a fear admitted, a turn understood, a failure in the teacher's instruction which could be left unchanged if everybody was too relieved to look at it.

He had heard what Dhruv had done once he saw the difficulty. He had not heard what the man did before the next learner had to stop and tell him.

That was a different part of responsibility. It did not wait at the end of a route with a comforting voice. It changed the beginning.

Veer looked away from the little light beyond the window and asked the man beside the bed.

"Uske baad aapne apni agli class kaise shuru ki?"

# CHAPTER ONE HUNDRED — WHERE HE CHOSE TO STAND

---

> *"His correction was not a new star.*
> *He changed the place from which he asked another person to look."*
> *— Ira's later register, conclusion of Dhruv's first account*

---

"Uski jagah se," Dhruv said.

Veer waited.

"Jahaan seekhne waala khada hota. Pehle wahaan se dekha. Sirf wahaan tak pahunchke nahi, wahaan se lautke bhi."

The answer was smaller than a new method with a name. It was not smaller than the difficulty it corrected. Dhruv had known the ground before the old learner came; knowing had not prevented him from teaching half of it as though the other half could be supplied by trust.

At the next lesson, he had begun beside the learner rather than at the place which made his own instruction easiest to give. They had looked at the starting ground, the turn and the view on returning before the exercise became a test of any one of those things. He had asked what could actually be seen and accepted an answer which did not sound like his own.

"Phir akela?" Veer asked.

"Jitna us waqt theek tha. Aur jab kehne ki zaroorat hui ki abhi nahi, toh uske liye alag se himmat jama karne ki zaroorat kam padi."

Not gone. Dhruv did not claim the correction had made every later learner fearless or every instruction complete. It had made one common mistake less likely to arrive dressed as the pupil's failure.

"Aasman ki baat badli?"

"Nahi. Pehle zameen ki baat chhootni kam hui."

The old teaching still belonged to its nights, its careful attention, its one learner at a time. This was not a new permission to practise it from a hospital bed. It was the account of a teacher learning that the person beneath a clear sky could still need a clearer beginning.

---

Dhruv looked towards the time and asked whether the rest of the answer could wait for the agreed listening.

Veer said it could. The immediate question had been answered. He did not need the teacher to remain until every implication had been converted into a sentence he could keep.

They ended while the shoulder was still settled.

During the night, Veer woke once to the sound of care being given in another room. He checked his own position before deciding he needed nothing changed just then. Later he did need help, and asked. The difference between those two answers mattered more than being the person who always said the same one.

He slept again.

---

On day eighty-six, the proposed gathering became a series of actual decisions.

Rudra's team reviewed the previous days' transfers and the continuing ankle and knee limits. The wrist would remain protected. The chair would take the distance to the room; there would be no extra stand for the sake of arriving as a less conspicuous patient. His breathing had stayed settled, but that was one part of the body, not permission for the others to be forgotten.

Veer's own examination and movement plan retained the aid, the help and the shorter effort if the knee objected. Aadhya's hands were attended to before she decided whether the listening interval fitted the day. Ira had her eye care and the choice of a shaded seat, ordinary right-eye use kept brief and comfortable, the left still protected.

They were asked about now, not yesterday's intention.

Each chose to come for the short account with the limits described. Nobody had to earn the seat by agreeing to a practical exercise afterwards.

---

This time Rudra asked where the chair could be placed before the others arrived.

A position near the window gave the better view of the small court below. It also asked him to keep turning if he wanted to see the person speaking. Another place gave less of the court and more of the room.

He chose the second.

The staff member moving the chair checked that the foot support remained clear, settled the position and asked whether the choice still felt right once he was actually in it. He said yes after looking, not before. The view could be smaller without the decision being a defeat.

When Veer came with the assistance and aid arranged, Rudra did not have to raise his voice to be heard. Aadhya could settle the hands without moving her chair out of somebody else's way. Ira found the shade she had requested and rested the ordinary eye once she knew where the people were.

Dhruv entered without the instruments of a night lesson.

The ring was on its cord. The hand which still needed care was not offered as evidence that he could now demonstrate whatever his story described. He sat with the four and asked where they wanted him, so no one had to spend the whole interval adapting to a position he had chosen alone.

Rudra moved his good hand towards an empty place, then stopped long enough to ask Ira whether the voice would be comfortable from there.

She listened to Dhruv answer and said it would.

Dhruv told the account from its beginning.

The three who had not heard it were not asked to understand from the last question Veer remembered. They heard about the familiar ground, the opening in the branches, the answer which had sounded complete to the man asking for it. They heard that the sky had stayed clear while the learner's next turn had become uncertain.

Rudra found himself watching where the teacher looked when he spoke of the mistake.

Not away from them. Not towards Veer as though the boy who already knew the story ought to supply proof of its meaning. Dhruv described what he had assumed, what the other person had eventually asked and what became visible when he went to stand beside him.

The remembered night had room for an ordinary error. It did not need to conceal a fatal one for the telling to matter.

"Aapne uski jagah se dekh liya," Ira said. "Usne aapki jagah se dekha?"

"Baad mein, jab dono taraf dekhna kaam ka tha. Pehle usse meri jagah pahunchne ko nahi kaha ki tab uski baat sunoonga."

Rudra looked down at the brace.

The answer did not make their positions identical. That would have been another kind of pretending. It meant the difference between them could be examined before the person with less freedom to move was required to remove it alone.

---

Aadhya asked what happened if the learner could describe the ground and still did not want to try.

Dhruv said the refusal would need hearing, not immediately improving into a more acceptable answer. There could be something he had failed to ask, a fear which needed time, a body unready for the task. There could also be a skill still needing work. He would not know which by deciding that reluctance always meant the same thing.

"Phir toh bahut der lag sakti hai," Veer said.

"Lagti hai. Jaldi samajh lene ka daawa karne se jo der baad mein lagti hai, woh bhi gino."

There was a dry humour in it which made Rudra smile. He had never heard a teacher offer patience as a way of taking the shorter road through an honest difficulty. Patience usually arrived as something the student was asked to have while the adult remained certain.

Here it was work the teacher could fail to do too.

"Galat jagah khade hoke sahi baat keh sakte hain," Dhruv said. "Par phir sahi baat bhi kaam nahi aati."

He did not ask them to turn the sentence into a rule which would answer every future situation. A clear instruction could be useful; an order given in danger could need acting on before there was time for a long conversation. This had been a teaching night, with time available, and he had nearly spent that time defending how obvious the answer seemed to him.

Rudra was glad the account kept its actual size.

---

Veer asked if the old learner had finally made the turn without help.

Dhruv answered about the later practice he had actually seen. The learner had learned more of that ground, needed less prompting there and still had things to ask. The success did not prove that a different place, a different night or a tired body would require nothing further.

There was no name given to the improvement. It was not a new kind of person emerging from the trees.

Rudra thought of the ward steps he had managed before the journey and the morning afterwards when the body had needed less asked of it. The earlier movement had been real. So had the later limit. He had wanted one to cancel the other because the account would then be easier to tell.

It was possible for learning to remain true without becoming a guarantee.

When Ira asked about keeping her own account, Dhruv agreed that she could record what he had chosen to tell. A later correction could stand with it. She did not have to spend the eye's rest taking down every word now.

The permission did not turn the memory into a record of the whole man. None of them had been given the life he had not described.

---

Rudra asked whether he could try a little more movement soon.

The question escaped into the quiet after the story. He had meant to leave it for the treating people. Hearing himself ask a teacher first showed him the old shortcut: if the desire sounded like learning, perhaps the answer could come from someone who understood why he wanted it.

Dhruv did understand.

He still did not give the permission.

"Unse poochho jo abhi tumhaara shareer dekh rahe hain," he said. "Main raasta bataane mein madad kar sakta hoon. Aaj kitna chalna hai, woh kahaani se nahi niklega."

Rudra nodded, then asked if the teacher would help him put the practical question clearly instead of answering it in their place.

He would.

When the interval ended, the four left with the assistance already arranged. There was no extra drill tucked into the way back. Rudra let the chair take the corridor and saved the question for the people who could examine the ankle before answering it.

The practical question reached the clinician and therapist that afternoon.

Rudra told them what he wanted, not what the story had made him think he ought to be able to do. They heard how the recent transfers had felt, where the knee pulled, how quickly the ankle tired and what the chair had allowed him to do without adding those efforts together.

There would be no extra attempt after the listening interval. They would review him the next morning, after rest, and decide from the examination and the night he actually had.

He disliked waiting less once the waiting had something inside it besides obedience.

He ate, received the care already due and slept. Once in the night he needed the leg's position changed. He said where it had begun to pull and let the help arrive before trying to solve it by moving the braced wrist into use.

---

On day eighty-seven, the review allowed the few protected steps he had managed before the first visit, with the same necessary assistance and no new freedom to load the wrist or abandon the ankle support.

The therapist showed where the effort would end and where he could sit sooner. His good hand had the permitted support. The chair was brought behind, not left across the room as an encouragement to finish the whole distance.

He rose with help.

For the first moments he listened too closely to the floor, as if the next instruction might arrive through the part of the body touching it. It remained a floor. The useful information came from the therapist, the support, the ankle's actual response and his willingness to say when that response changed.

At the turn he asked to pause.

They paused. The knee needed a smaller adjustment before the next movement. He sat in the chair brought for him rather than spend the remaining effort making the pause look unnecessary.

It had been a little more than he could manage comfortably after the outing. It was not the right to make the next journey longer.

He gave the account and rested.

---

The others had their own work, and it did not become four versions of the same improvement.

Veer practised the short aided movement his team allowed, with the shoulder protected and the seat placed before the turn. He reported the knee's response instead of using Rudra's steps as a distance to equal. His ribs still disliked some positions and the unexpected laugh; the shoulder was not available for lifting a friend merely because the legs had moved.

Aadhya's hand review allowed small ordinary uses where comfortable, not hard grip or rough contact. A light cloth could be moved; that did not restore the altered feeling in the fingers. She put it down when effort began changing the ease of the task. No leaf grew because the hands wanted a better answer about themselves.

Ira kept the left cover. Short comfortable use of the ordinary eye and the quiet afterwards remained part of care. She chose listening when looking was enough for the time being. The choice did not become evidence that another kind of sight had supplied what she had missed.

Their necessary functional updates could be shared as agreed. Their private responses did not have to become a group report.

---

That night Rudra slept badly.

There was no dramatic reason he could give the morning for it. The position had needed changing, the room had been noisy for a while and sleep had returned in pieces. On day eighty-eight he told the treating people before they considered another effort.

The examination did not become a trial of whether yesterday's progress had been honest. The plan was shortened. He did the necessary supported movement, received the care and let the chair take the rest. Nobody asked the account of the steps to be torn up.

He could still remember making them.

He could also need less asked of him today.

After food, he asked about a short seated time in the small courtyard if a later review found it suitable. He described the help it would need and asked for the others to be considered separately, not included inside his wish before they had been told.

The answer did not arrive as a booking. It arrived as questions about the route, shade, seating, return and the next care interval.

---

By the morning of day eighty-nine, they had slept, been seen again and heard the arrangements individually.

The distance to the court would be taken with assistance, not used as an exercise added to the morning. The chairs and supports would go where they were needed. Ira could stay in shade with spoken guidance and no demand to keep looking. Aadhya would not carry things in hands which still needed protecting.
